# Chapter One: The Hours After

**MARCUS**

They came round the Kettle in the morning, on the young flood, into the sun.

The shoal was smoking. On a still day it always did: a long low breath of white lying flat along the water over the warm ground. The *Forward* passed through the edge of it with her oars dipping slow. The smell drifted in over the rail, sulphur and weed and the wet warm air of a bath-house. Coming home with the iron, a man on the benches had said *Oh, gods* to that smell as if it were a girl's name. This time the men lifted their heads at it and breathed it in, and bent to the oars again. The *Forward* came out of the smoke into the clear with the headland on her bow.

Marcus stood at the rail forward with his cloak open, because the sun was on him and he wanted it. He had slept in the last of the dark, sitting against the foot of the mast with his head on his knees, and dreamed of the dip in the gorse and the torches going down into it. He had woken stiff in the neck, with the taste of the dream still in his mouth. Felix was not here to put water in his hand. He had got his own from the cask, and drunk it, and come forward to watch the town come.

The headland slid back, and there was the town, all of it at once, small and bright and near, like a thing painted on the side of a cup. The morning had got into the whitewash. Nets lay spread to dry on the roofs of the Row, brown on white. The bakehouse at the foot of Rope Street was smoking, and above it all stood the grey of the fort.

The quay was full.

He had not thought it would be, at this hour. Somebody up at the fort must have seen the masts at first light and run down the hill with it, and the town had come out as it was, with the bread half made and the nets half mended. He could see the colours of it from here: a long uneven fringe along the edge of the stone, shawls and aprons and the dark coats of the fishermen, and a child up on somebody's shoulders. At the foot of the quay stairs Tuccius's boys stood in their two ranks, small as pegs.

They were not waving. They stood all along the quay with their faces to the sea, and they were counting the boats.

He could almost hear it. The *Forward*. The *Constancy* astern of her, Nerva's ship. The cohort's galleys, and the transports, low and slow, one and another coming round the Kettle in their file, out of the smoke. The fringe along the quay took them in. When the last of them was clear of the smoke, the quay saw that it was the last, and the whole long line of it eased at once, like a held rope when the weight comes off it.

He had brought every boat home.

Then the town began on the faces, and was still again.

His eyes went up the hill as they always did, to the end of Netmakers' Row and Prisca's window, which looked out over the gap to the sea.

Her lamp was lit.

It was full morning. The sun stood well up over the eastern mole, and the whitewash of the Row was bright with it. In the small square window at the end the red lamp was burning, low and steady, with its back to the room. In daylight it was hardly a light at all. It was a little warm place in the white of the wall, like a coal that has been forgotten in a grate.

He did not know what it meant. He kept it in sight all the way in, past the mole-head and its tower and on through the gap into the harbor.

---

Valeria was at the rail beside him when the *Forward* came alongside. He had not heard her come up from aft. She stood with the Farrier grounded beside her and her face set toward the quay, and the spring had put more iron into her plaits at the temples.

"I've three to write," she said.

"I know."

"Their people are at Callow. Two of them. The other's from the Long Field." She had a board under her arm with a sheet pinned to it, the top of it close-written in her blunt soldier's hand and the rest bare. "I've started them every day since the strand."

"Do you want me to—"

"No." Her right thumb settled on the worn place on the haft, and stayed there. "They were mine. They'll have it from me." She looked down at the board. "It's the bank. I want to say why I couldn't get down to them, and every way I say it, it sounds like I'm asking them to say it was all right."

He had nothing to give her for that. The plank banged down. The quay was near enough now that he could smell it, fish and woodsmoke and hot wet stone, and pick out the faces one at a time, every one turned up to the rail.

"It wasn't all right," said Valeria, to the board. "That's the trouble. I'll write that."

She tucked the board under her arm, took up the Farrier, and went to see her files down.

---

He went down first. The stones came up hard under his boots, and his knees kept the swell a little after. The steam stood round him to the waist. The noise of the town rose with it. It was not cheering, not yet. It was a long low sound of a great many people saying names under their breath, and a child somewhere asking a question over and over in a high clear voice, and nobody answering it.

"Highness."

It was Felix.

He was standing at the foot of the plank where he had no business to be, inside Tuccius's ranks, thin and stooped in a clean shirt, with a jug in one hand and a cup in the other. He had the look of a man who had been told twice to stand back and had decided not to hear it a third time. He held out the cup.

It was water, cold, and it tasted of the fort's spring, of iron and stone. Marcus drank it standing on the quay with the cohort coming down the plank behind him, and gave the cup back.

"You walked that down from the fort," he said.

"It's downhill, Highness. Strictly." Felix filled the cup again and held it ready. He had stayed behind. He had kept the tower room and the fort's accounts and Marcus's other shirt all spring, and he had stood on this quay to see them go. Now his eyes were on the plank behind Marcus, moving from face to face, and his mouth tightened at one that was not there.

"Tappo," said Felix.

"Yes."

Felix nodded, and stood there with the full cup.

Tuccius was at the head of his boys with his helmet under his arm. The seam through his eyebrow had healed pale and flat while they were gone. He brought his heels together.

"Held, Highness," he said. "All spring. It's written."

"Was there anything?"

"Boats. Twice. They looked at us and went away." Tuccius rubbed the end of his nose with the back of his wrist, quickly, and fixed his eyes on the *Forward*'s rail. "It's written. Afternoon, if you like. I'll not keep you now." He moved his head a little at the quay behind him. "They'll want you now."

Marcus turned round, and the town was coming in off the edges.

---

It was not a rush. The cohort was down on the stones, and the Lanciarii behind them, and the transports' men were coming down their own planks along the quay. The town came in among them as the flood comes in over flat sand, by small channels everywhere at once. A woman got her arms round a man's neck and kept them there. An old fisherman put a gurnard into Macro's hands, still wet from the boat, and Macro, who had been a bishop's cook, smelled its gills before he said thank you. Rufo stepped down with his helmet in his hand and Sylara's fair lock plaited over his ear, and three net-wives he had never been introduced to had hold of him before he reached the bottom. Oppius never stopped on the quay at all. He walked straight through it with a sack on his shoulder, toward the foot of Rope Street and the bakehouse door, and the town got out of his way.

Marcus walked up the quay.

He had thought about this at the rail through the whole wet fortnight of the passage, and made an order in his head: the women of the dead first, then the people of the missing, then the rest. He had the names in his coat. He had Pen's sheet of the volunteers in its oilskin, with Galba's men on it in Pen's upright hand. Every night by the lantern he had gone down it and put a mark against the men who had come back. The sheet had fewer marks than names.

Now the order fell out of his head, because the town would not come at him in order. It took him as he came. A woman he had never seen took his hand and held it and said, "*Thank* you," with her husband grinning behind her, whole, with a bandage round his head; and let him go. A boy ran at his legs and was snatched back. A man of the third century who had married into the Row at Midwinter saw him through the press. He took Marcus by the arm and walked him into the middle of his wife's people, who had come down in a body.

"That's him," the man said. "That's the one I said."

The wife's mother looked Marcus over with her hands folded under her apron. Then she gave him one nod, the nod a creditor gives a man who has paid what he owed.

He went on.

---

He knew where the net-wife would be. She would be at the foot of the Row, where its stair met the quay, because she had stood there at Midwinter and again on the morning they sailed. That was where she had come out of the line to take Tappo's face in her hands and set his cap straight.

She was there. She stood on the bottom step with her shawl down round her shoulders and her arms at her sides, a small woman with a net-wife's hands, brown and cracked across the knuckles, and the smell of tarred twine about her. She was looking past him at the cohort coming up the quay, one face and then the next and then the next.

She saw him coming, and saw his face, and stopped looking at the cohort.

He stopped in front of her.

"He was at Bastion," Marcus said. "On the last day. In the retreat."

She waited.

"The General was down on the slope under his horse, with a lance in him, and Liora was up on the road. Tappo and four others carried her down to him. Riders came at them all the way down. The others fell. He carried her the last of it on his back, with a lance wound in his shoulder, and set her down by the General."

The net-wife's mouth had gone small.

"His cap," she said.

"Liora has it. He took it off at the end, and she kept it. It's in her bag."

The net-wife nodded. She nodded several times, quickly, looking at the stones between his boots.

"Let her keep it," she said. "I sewed it at Midwinter. Out of the ends. There was a bolt of the king's red, and they let us have the ends." She was not crying. Her hands had come up and were holding each other in front of her, the cracked fingers turning over and over. "He'd no hat. All the cold weather he went up and down the Row with no hat and his ears red. I said, *that one wants a hat.*" She stopped. "He sang at me. Every time he went by."

"I know," said Marcus. "I heard him."

"He was a thief," said the net-wife. "Did you know that? He told me. He said, I was a thief in the capital, missus, I'll not lie to you. And I said I'd lock up my nets, then." Something crossed her face that was nearly a smile, and was gone. "He said, I'll sing to them instead."

She looked up at him then, for the first time, straight in the face.

"Did he know he'd done it? That he'd got her there?"

Marcus had it from Roderic, who had told it once at the rail on the passage, in the dark, and not again.

"He knew," he said. "He set her down and said, *General. Brought her.*"

"Brought her," the net-wife said after him, under her breath.

Then she turned and went up the stair into the Row, slowly, with one hand on the wall.

He stood at the foot of the stair until she was out of sight.

---

He had never seen Sosius's sister.

He knew her because she was the one person on the quay who was not looking at the cohort. She stood by herself near the end of the fish-market, a young woman with a baby on her hip and a parcel tied with string in her other hand, and she was watching the *Forward*'s plank. Every time a man stepped off it she searched his face, and turned back for the next.

He went to her and said her brother's name. She understood before he had finished, and would not let herself, and then did.

He told her the rest as he had heard Liora give it on the road, walking beside her litter in the rain. Sosius had been hit early, and kept it to himself. He had run the rest of the way down with his hand pressed to his side, and gone down twenty paces short.

She heard it all with the baby on her hip, looking at his face.

"He wouldn't tell you," she said at last. "He'd never tell you. He'd not tell you his feet were wet. That's why I send them." She looked at the parcel in her hand as if somebody else had put it there. "I'd these ready for when you came in. I thought, he'll have worn through the last."

The baby reached for the string, and she let it.

"You can give them to somebody," she said. "Can you? Somebody who needs them."

"Yes."

She held the parcel out, and he took it. It was soft and light, and warm from her hand.

He carried it on up the quay, because he could not think what else to do.

---

Bruttius and Trebonius and Egnatius had nobody on the quay. Their people would have letters, in his own hand.

There were others. He went to them all.

For Galba's men there was nothing to tell. He had Pen's sheet, with its marks against the men who had come down the road at dawn. Here and there on the quay were people who had come down for a man with no mark against his name, and who had seen already, from the plank, that he was not coming off it. A fisherman of the Row with his cap in his hands. An old woman from the yard's cookhouse, who was nobody's mother that Marcus knew of and had fed one of them all winter. A girl. He told each of them the same thing, because it was the only true thing he had.

"He turned back," he said. "At the end, in the gorse, for men who were down. Nobody saw him after."

"Is he dead?"

"I don't know."

A death they could have taken home. The fisherman asked him twice whether he knew, and was told twice that he did not. The old woman said, "Then I'll wait," and sat down on a bollard at the edge of the quay with her back straight and her eyes on the gap, as though another boat might come through it.

When he had been to them all he stood at the head of the quay by the harbor master's door, with Sosius's socks in his hand, and he was shaking. Not his hands. All of him, a little, like a sail held too close to the wind.

Felix was beside him with the jug.

"That's the last of them, Highness. The ones who came down for someone." He filled the cup and held it out. "Strictly, you've seen everyone."

Marcus drank the water.

"How did you know?"

"I've been on this quay since the masts showed." Felix took the cup back. "I watched who walked down the hill, and who they looked for. It's not hard. You can see a good deal from a quay if you keep still." He held out his hand for the parcel. "I'll put that by."

"They're socks," said Marcus. "Sosius's sister. She wants them given to somebody who needs them."

Felix weighed the parcel in his hand. Then he put it inside his shirt, against his chest, as he used to carry letters through the rain at the capital.

"I'll find him," said Felix. He looked up the hill to the end of the Row. "Prisca's had that lit since you sailed, Highness. Day and night. Oil's dear. She said she didn't care."

---

Behind him the town had begun to cheer, a little. It broke out of the crowd in odd places, where somebody had got somebody back, and ran along the quay and died, and broke out somewhere else. A pipe started a tune and lost it.

Roderic strode by at the far edge of it with three of Tuccius's boys trotting after him. He was the biggest man on the quay by a head, broad through the chest, in his good coat with his beard combed out for the town, and nothing in his walk remembered the black lance. He was going fast up the quay toward the crown's road and the stable, and stopping for no one. A fisherman tried to take his hand and was given it, shaken, and left behind. He had been away from Thistle since the spring.

Liora came down the transport's plank last, after the wounded, with her bags on her shoulder.

She was in the white, washed in salt water and dried in the wind until it had gone the grey of an old sail. Her hair was tied back at the nape with a piece of cord, honey-gold and dull with salt. She stopped at the foot of the plank, and the town saw her.

A stillness spread along the quay from the plank. People turned and saw her, and stopped what they were saying. A woman put her hand to her mouth. Then they moved: not to her, not to touch her, but toward her, a slow shuffle along the stones, until there was a ring of the town round the foot of the plank with a space in the middle and Liora in it.

She stood in the space a moment.

"I've men to get up the hill," she said. "To the Baths. Some of them can't walk." She hitched the bags on her shoulder. "If anyone has a cart."

Somebody had a cart. Somebody else had a door off its hinges for a litter. By the time Marcus had crossed the quay they were lifting the first of the wounded. Soon the men of the town were carrying them up the crown's road toward the Baths at a walk, with Liora beside the first of them and her hand flat on his chest. The ring broke up into a line along the road to watch her go by.

---

In the afternoon Tuccius's report was waiting on the table in the harbor master's room, under a stone. It was in Ennia's big straight hand, as the last had been, and longer, because the girl had been given more to write and had written all of it.

*The Prefect's home guard held Cyrene from the day the fleet sailed to the day it came back. Two Tharsian boats stood off the Kettle in the spring and went away. One stood off again at the next new moon and went away again. Nobody landed. Pullus is now a file-leader. The bakehouse oven cracked and Fausta mended it with clay from the black beach. The prisoners asked if they could stand a watch on the slip gate and the home guard said no. Then the home guard said yes. Nothing went wrong.*

*Said by Tuccius. Written by Ennia, of Netmakers' Row.*

Marcus put it inside his coat with the other papers. Tuccius stood by the door at his ease, which on him looked much like attention, waiting to be dismissed.

"You let the prisoners stand a watch."

"Asked, Highness. In the spring. Their own officer brought it." Tuccius was looking at the window. "Said they'd as soon not be burned in their beds by their own side as by ours. I thought about it a fortnight. Then I let them have the slip gate, nights, with two of mine." He paused. "It's in the report."

"It's a good report."

"She's particular," said Tuccius, with his chin up.

Marcus nearly asked him then. *Do you know what I did at the gate? Has it come here yet?* He could see in Tuccius's broad stubborn face that the man did not know, and Ennia's report was in his coat, saying *Nothing went wrong*.

"Thank you, Tuccius," he said.

Tuccius nodded and went out. His boots sounded on the stair, and then in the street, going away at a drill pace; and Marcus stood alone in the harbor master's room with the red lamp unlit on the sill.

---

The room over the slip gate smelled of lamp oil and ink and the sea, as it always had.

The Navarch stood up when he came in, unfolding from the stool by the window in sections, tall and stooped under the low beams in his long black sea-coat. He made the small exact bow he gave everybody, from Marcus down to the boy who brought his water.

"Highness. You are home."

"Navarch."

"Sit, if you will. The other stool is sound." The Navarch did not sit until Marcus had. Then he folded his long white hands on the table beside the book, which lay open at a page half written, with the pen across it. The window behind him was open to the harbor, and through it Marcus could see the masts of his own fleet at their moorings, quiet.

"I saw you come in," the Navarch said. "From here. I saw the town count your boats." He studied his folded hands. "It is a thing I have seen done on a good many quays. It is the same on all of them."

"They were all there."

"Yes. I saw that." The pale heavy-lidded eyes came up. "And then they counted the faces. That is also the same on all of them."

Marcus said nothing.

The Navarch's right hand left the table, and its fingers closed on a stylus that was not there, and had not been for a year; and opened again; and took up the pen instead.

"How many did you leave on the field, Highness?"

Marcus told him.

He gave him the number he had from Liora, and Valeria's three on the bank, and the five who had carried Liora down the slope. Then he gave him the number of Galba's men who had not come down the road, and said that he did not know if they were dead. He said it all in a level voice, looking at the Navarch's hands, and the Navarch heard it out without moving. Then he dipped the pen and wrote in his small Tharsian hand, and the figures ran down the page upside down, where Marcus could not read them.

The Navarch blotted the line.

He asked nothing else. Marcus had been ready for it all the way up the stair, and it did not come, and he sat with the readiness in his chest and nowhere to put it.

"You don't ask what happened," he said.

"No." The Navarch laid the pen down along the edge of the book. "You will tell me, I think, or you will not. Either way I shall hear it. Your quay talks, and my clerks hear the quay." He paused. "I asked the number because the number is mine to write. The rest is yours."

"I could have had the gate," said Marcus.

He had not known he was going to say it. It was out in the small room in the afternoon light, flat and hard, in his own voice.

The Navarch waited.

"Tiberius keeps two hundred people on his books," Marcus said. "Living. In pens, in a quarry behind the outer works. Ours, from the stair: the ones who were still on the beach when I went aboard. He'd written them off to Livia. If I took the gate they were lost stock, and hers. The gate was standing open. I turned the cohort round in it."

The Navarch's hand lay flat on the open book. It did not go to the pen.

"Yes," he said at last. "Well." He looked down at the line he had written, and up at Marcus again, with nothing on his long pale face that Marcus could read. "That is not a thing I write, Highness. I have no column for it."

He closed the book.

---

They lit the first fire on the quay by the slip at dusk, where they had lit them every homecoming since the town was theirs.

The town brought the meat down. It arrived on spits and in pots, carried down the crown's road and the Row and Rope Street by women and boys: a whole sheep, and a pig split and spitted, and sausages in long dark coils. There was fish, cod and gurnard and the flat grey things the boats brought in off the Kettle that had no name outside Cyrene. Fausta's bread followed in baskets, steaming, with Oppius carrying the biggest of them and walking beside her like a man who has been told where to walk. The smoke of the fat climbed into the still evening and hung over the harbor: mutton and crackling, burnt fish-skin, the sour hot crust of the loaves. Men who had eaten salt pork and biscuit since the strand sat down on the stones and ate with both hands, and did not talk.

Marcus sat on the edge of the slip with a heel of bread and a rib of mutton, and was hungry, which surprised him. Felix had a cup of water at his elbow before he had sat down. He stood behind him in the firelight looking out at the masts, as he used to stand behind his chair at palace feasts and look at the ceiling. A skin of the town's wine travelled along the slip from hand to hand, and when it reached Marcus he passed it on.

Alana sat down beside him on the slip's edge with her own bread, and for a time neither of them spoke. She had been up the hill to the tower and changed and come down again, and there was clean ink on her fingers already. She ate left-handed. Her right hand lay in her lap, palm up, and now and then a fine shake ran through its fingers and was gone. She let it shake.

"Rusca has the weather-book," she said, when she had eaten. "She asked for it. She wants to put something right."

"In your book?"

"In her own column. I gave her one." Her eyes were on the fire. "She's exact about it. I'm not to look till she's done."

Down the quay the cohort had got round its own fire, and somebody was singing, badly, and somebody else was telling him so.

The lamps were coming on up the hill. Windows turned gold up the steps of the town as the candles were lit, and here and there among them burned the low red of a clay lamp set with its back to the room, which was Tharsis's custom and not Cyrene's. Prisca's, at the end of the Row, which had burned all day. The brothers' two: Fausta's over the drying loft, and Tuccius's in the harbor master's window at the head of the quay.

Then another.

It was halfway up Netmakers' Row, in a window he had never noticed. It came on as he looked, low and red, and steadied.

A little after, down by the fish-market, in a room over a chandler's, one more.

Beside him Alana had seen them too. Her lips moved, and stopped.

"Two," she said, under her breath, and nothing else.

Down the quay the singing had stopped. He turned his head.

Roderic was standing up.

He stood at the edge of the cohort's fire with his cup in his hand, in his good coat, with the firelight in the grey of his beard. The cohort had seen him stand, and gone quiet round him, as a field goes quiet under a hawk. He looked past them at the two new lamps, and then down into his cup. He set the cup on the stones beside him, carefully, and straightened, and spoke up the quay in the voice he had used on parade grounds all his life: not loud, and carrying to the slip.

"Bruttius," said Roderic. "Of the fourth century. A net-maker's son, of the old harbor."

The quay was still. At the far fire the Lanciarii turned on their benches. Up the crown's road a woman carrying a pot stopped where she was.

"Trebonius. Of the fourth. A drover. He had the bridle, and he held it." Roderic's voice did not change. "Egnatius. Of the second. A cooper's man, from Barrel Lane. He'd no shield; his shield was under her. Sosius. Of the fourth. The youngest. He was hit at the top of the swell and told nobody."

He stopped. His chest rose and fell once.

"Tappo," said Roderic. "Of the cohort. A thief from the capital, and a singer. He got her there."

He stood a moment longer with his hands at his sides. Then he bent, and took up his cup, and sat down.

Marcus could hear the fire, and the water slapping under the quay, and somewhere up the hill a dog. On the far side of the slip Liora sat on an upturned basket with her bags at her feet and a bowl of broth going cold in her lap. Her face was turned toward Roderic across the quay. The corner of her mouth went in. A little way off from her, Gaius sat on a net-box with his bread on his knee, and his eyes were on her.

Roderic drank from his cup.

Rufo, at the cohort's fire, said "*Tappo*," as if the man had only gone out of the room and might hear; and somebody else said it, and somebody else, all round the fire, under their breath and not together. At the Lanciarii's fire somebody said "*Sosius*." The five names passed along the quay in the dark like that, in no order, until they stopped.

The man who had been singing began again, quietly, and this time nobody told him anything.

---

Rusca was at the table in the tower room when he came up, with the lamp drawn close and Alana's weather-book open in front of her, and a pen in her hand.

He stopped in the door. He had come up ahead of Alana, who had stayed by the fire to see Liora up the hill, and he had forgotten that the room would not be empty.

The quaestor did not look up. She was writing slowly, in her small close clerk's hand, in a column ruled down the outer edge of the right-hand page that he had not seen before. At the end of each line she wet her thumb and dried it on the square of linen in her cuff, and turned back a page, and checked something there, and turned forward again.

"I'll be finished directly, Highness," she said, still not looking up. "She said I might."

"I know. She told me."

He crossed to the window. The harbor lay black below with the fleet's riding lights on it, and the fire on the quay had burned down to a red bed. Up the hill the town's windows climbed in their steps, and among them, here and there, the red of the lamps.

Behind him the pen scratched, and stopped.

"It was the dip," said Rusca.

He turned round.

She had laid the pen down. She sat back from the book, white with tiredness, and looked at him.

"On the brow," she said, "you asked me what they'd send, and when. I said the fed levy, after the second hour of the night, among the boats." She touched the page. "That's what I wrote at the time. I've struck it. They came at the dip, not the boats, and at the third hour, not the second, because your rearguard was in the dip and they had to go through it. I had the levy right and the place wrong." Her mouth thinned. "I don't like having the place wrong."

"You had the levy right."

"Yes." She ran her eye down the column. "I've entered them here. The ones who didn't come back."

She turned the book round on the table to face him. It was in Tharsian, in her small hand, and he could not read most of it; but the heading at the top of the column was a single word, underlined, and he knew that word. He had seen it in the Navarch's books, and in the Survey's dockets at Stoneheart.

*Lost.*

"Not dead," he said.

"No." Rusca's thumb was on the linen in her cuff. "In our books a man you can't account for isn't dead. You can't strike him, because you don't know. He's lost. He's carried. His pay runs on, and somebody owes it, and one day somebody has to come and find out whether to pay it." She closed the book on her finger, carefully, as though it might bruise. "I don't know what your books do, Highness. That's how ours do it. I thought she'd want it written down, so I wrote it."

"Thank you."

"It's only a column." She stood and gave him the weather-book, and he took it. "Good night, Highness."

He heard her go down the stair and round toward the west tower, and the Lanciarius on the landing say something, and her answer, and a door.

---

Alana came up a little after, slowly, with her bad arm held in to her side.

"Liora's at the Baths," she said. "She won't sleep. She's sitting up with the boy from the *Forward* whose leg went bad on the passage. She says it'll be all right, and she says she'll sleep tomorrow." She saw the weather-book in his hands. "Rusca's done?"

"Yes."

"Did you look?"

"She showed me."

Alana nodded. She took the book from him and opened it on the table at Rusca's column, and her eyes found the heading, and her lips moved once over the word. She left it open there, with her left hand on the page.

"I'm so tired, Marcus," she said.

"You said to ask you again at home."

She went still.

He had not meant to say it like that, in the middle of the room, with his boots still on and the smoke of the fire in his coat.

"This is home," he said. He did not move toward her. "What's in your new column? The last line."

Alana stood across the table from him for what seemed a long time, with her hand still on the page.

Then she smiled. It was not much of a smile. It was tired through, and her eyes were wet, and there was ink at her temple where she had pushed her hair back.

"Not yet," she said.

"Not yet?"

"Not yet." She came round the table and put her good hand on his chest, over the place where his papers were, and stood with her forehead nearly touching his chin. "You'll know when. I promise you'll know when."

He put his arms round her, carefully, for the arm. She leaned into him. She smelled of salt and smoke and ink, and her hair was coming down out of its pins on one side, and she was warm.

"Ask me again," she said against his coat. "Keep asking. I like it."

---

The cutter came on the eighth day, in the afternoon, on the flood.

By then the quay had the gate. Marcus could not have said who brought it: the Navarch's clerks, or the transports' crews in the wine-shops, or the cohort talking in its sleep. He knew it had come by the way the net-wives stopped talking when he passed, and then, after a moment, went on.

He was in the yard that afternoon. He had gone down, as he did most afternoons that week, to see Gaius's second keel, which Velthur's boy had laid in the spring while they were gone, a finger out at the third scarf. Gaius had seen that from the deck on the first morning, as they came alongside, and had been taking it up ever since, timber by timber. Marcus was standing on the slip in the sawdust with his hands behind his back, being told about scarfs, when one of Tuccius's boys came running down from the mole-head with the word.

A sail. Off the Kettle. White, with a sun on it.

Gaius stopped talking.

He stood on the slip with his rule in his hand and his weight on his good leg. His brows were drawn down. He stared out past the end of the slip at the gap, where there was nothing yet to see.

"It'll be the Vigil," Marcus said.

"Yes," said Gaius. "I expect it will."

He put the rule away in his belt, and went on looking at the gap.

---

It was a small old cutter, white-painted and peeling, with the returning sun stitched on its patched white sail in gold thread gone brown at the edges. Marcus had seen it once before, two days out from the fen in the spring, when it spoke the fleet with Cassandra's report for the capital: the day he turned for Ghoul's End. He knew it from the head of the slip as soon as it cleared the Kettle.

It came in through the gap and rounded up to the long quay, and two sisters in white jumped down onto the stones with lines in their hands. Half the town had seen the sail, and came down to see who it was.

The plank was run out badly. The cutter's boy shoved it out from the rail on its rope, and it landed on the edge of the quay with one corner on the stone and one corner on nothing, and rocked.

A woman came to the cutter's rail and considered it.

She was heavyset and not tall, in a white cloak over a mail shirt that had been let out at the sides at least once. She wore a plain steel cap, and a coif under it so white and so starched it seemed to stand off her head by itself. She had a short spear in one hand with a small bronze bell at the socket, and her own bag in the other. Below the short sleeves of the mail her forearms were a ship's carpenter's, thick and brown. Her face was broad and weathered, and the grey eyes in it were looking at the plank as though it had been brought to her for an opinion.

She put the bag down, shifted the spear into the crook of her arm, and with one finger tapped the end of the plank twice where it lay on the rail.

"That's laid wrong," she said. "It'll tip." She looked down at the cutter's boy. "Has nobody ever shown you? You lay a plank on a quay with the cleats *down*, so they bite on the stone, and the long end inboard. That's not a plank. That's an accident with a rope on it."

The boy stared up at her. The cleats were up.

He turned it over. It lay flat on the stone, and stayed there.

"Thank you," said the woman, and picked up her bag, and came down it.

At the foot of the plank she stopped and took in the quay: the steam, the town, and the cohort. They had come up from the yard and the barracks to see the white sail, and stood now in a loose untidy crowd at the edge of the quay, staring at her. She looked them all over slowly, from one end to the other, and Marcus had the clear impression that she was counting things that were wrong.

Then her eyes came to him, and stopped.

"You'll be the Prefect," she said. "The prince."

"I'm Marcus."

"Sister Domitia. Of the White." She did not bow, and she did not hold out her hand. "I'm sent to you in the healer's place, Highness, by the Abbess." She took a letter from her bag and gave it to him. "That's yours." He put it inside his coat. "There's one for the healer, to go into her own hand." She glanced past him, up the quay. "Is she here?"

---

Liora was there.

She had come down from the Baths when the sail was seen. She stood at the edge of the crowd at the foot of the crown's road, with her hands still red from the basin and a smear of something on her apron. She looked at the cutter, and the sun on the sail, and the sister on the quay, and her face went still.

She walked down the quay. The crowd let her through.

One of the sisters from the lines, a thin young woman Marcus had never seen, met her halfway with a letter held out in both hands. It was folded small and sealed in white wax, and the seal was the returning sun.

Liora took it and looked at the seal. Then she broke it with a fingernail and read the letter standing in the middle of the quay, with the town round her and the cohort at the edge of it, and the cutter's boy coiling down a line behind her.

She read down the page once, and then from the top again, slowly. Her lips did not move, and her face did not change, and the hand that held the page did not shake. When she had finished she folded it along its creases and stood holding it.

Then she held it out to him.

It was a short letter in a firm clerkly hand, addressed to *Liora, of the Vigil of the Returning Sun, healer with the Prefect's army at Cyrene*, and signed *Cassandra, Knight-Abbess*. The order claimed its training and its hands. It thanked her, in a formal line, for her service in the field. It required her home to the house by the Lower Canal, in the capital, for a vigil of reflection, at the order's need and for a time the order would set. A sister of the White came with it to take her place with the army, and the cutter would carry her north on the first ebb after it came in.

That was all. There was no why in it. It said nothing of Bastion, or Ghoul's End, or any of the things Marcus was looking for in it as he read; and there was nothing in it anywhere that a man could have put his finger on and called wrong.

He gave it back to her.

"I can send it back," he said.

She looked at him.

"I'm Prefect of Cyrene. You're with my army, and the army's the crown's." He kept his voice low. The town was all round them, and the cohort at the edge of the quay was close enough to hear if it listened, and it was listening. "I can write to the Abbess that I need you here, and the crown needs you, and the order can have you back when the war's done. She can't take you off my quay if I don't give you up."

"No," said Liora. "She can't."

"Then let me."

Liora looked down at the folded letter in her hand. The corner of her mouth went in.

"It's my order's house, Highness," she said. "I walked into it behind a barrow, when I'd nowhere else to walk. They took me in and taught me what I know, and never asked me to say anything I couldn't say." She turned the letter over in her fingers. "They've asked me home. They've not sent anyone to make me. They've asked."

"She hasn't said why."

"No. I expect she'll tell me when I'm there."

"Liora—"

"I won't fight my way out of anything," said Liora. She said it quietly, to the letter. Then she lifted her head. "I don't. You know I don't. I'm not going to start with the house that took me in." She folded the letter once more, small, and put it in her apron pocket. "I'll go."

He opened his mouth.

"Let her be, Highness," said Sister Domitia.

She was beside them with her bag and her belled spear. She looked Liora over from her hair to her shoes, as she had looked over the plank and the quay and the cohort, and for a long moment the finger that had tapped the plank lay still on the spear-shaft.

"She's said she'll go," said Domitia. "That's the answer. She'll have thought about it longer than you have." She glanced at Liora's red hands and the apron, and back at her face. "You'll want to finish whatever that is you've left at the Baths, Sister. I'll come up and see it, if you'll show me. The cutter sails on the ebb. That gives you till dusk."

Liora looked at her.

"Thank you," she said. "Yes. Come up and I'll show you."

They walked up the crown's road together, Liora in her grey-white and Domitia in her starched coif, with the spear, side by side and not talking. The cohort at the edge of the quay watched them go by, and not one man of them said anything.

---

They walked down again at dusk.

Marcus had read his own letter by then, at the harbor master's window. It said what hers had said, in fewer words.

Liora had her bag now, and Tappo's cap was in it; Marcus knew that without seeing it. Halfway along the quay she lifted her head and looked up Netmakers' Row, once, at a window there, and went on.

The town had come to see her off. It lined the long quay from the foot of the crown's road to the cutter's plank, in the evening light with the steam coming up round it, and it stood back from her as she came. Some of them touched her sleeve as she passed, and some only wanted to.

The cohort was there, all of them, standing along the edge of the quay by the cutter in no order and no rank, bareheaded. As she drew near them Fingers stepped out of the line and held out his hand for her bag. She gave it to him. He carried it the last twenty paces to the plank and set it down at the foot of it, on the stone, and stepped back into the line with his eyes on the stones.

Gaius was at the plank.

He had been sitting on a bollard at the foot of it since the cutter tied up, with his bad leg out straight in front of him and his tablet on his knee. Marcus had seen him there all afternoon, from the quay and from the window of the harbor master's house, in the same place, drawing nothing. He did not get up now. He sat with the tablet on his good knee and watched her come.

Liora stopped in front of him.

Neither of them said anything. The town was behind her and the cutter behind him, and the sisters on the cutter's deck were waiting, and the boy had his hand on the line.

She bent and put out her hand, and laid it on his knee, the bad one, flat, through the wool of his breeches.

Gaius went rigid. The stubble of his eyebrows went up as far as it could go, which was not far. He sat with her hand on his knee, and in the dusk the red-gold came into the rims of her fingers, faint.

Gaius let it stay there for the length of one breath.

Then he put his own hand over hers, and lifted it off his knee, and held it a moment, and gave it back to her.

"After," said Gaius.

Liora straightened and looked down at him.

"After," she said.

She picked up her bag from the foot of the plank and carried it aboard. The boy cast off, and two sisters on the stern pushed off from the quay with their spear-butts. The cutter swung out into the harbor on the first of the ebb. The wind had come round with the dusk, and her sail filled, and the gold sun stitched on it went red in the last of the light.

The town on the quay stood and watched it go.

Marcus did not. He watched Gaius.

Gaius had got up off the bollard as the lines came in, slowly, with a hand on it. He stood on the quay with his bad leg straight and kept his eyes on the white sail all the way down the harbor, through the gap and out past the mole-head into the dusk, until there was no sail to see. His hand was still a little way out from his side, where it had given hers back.

---

# Chapter Two: Sister Domitia

**GAIUS**

The keel was down again by the morning of the ninth day, and it was right.

He had taken up every timber the boy had laid and laid it again himself, the long scarfs first and then the hog over them. At first light he had sighted down it from the stern end, on his belly on the slip with one eye shut, while the boy stood by and said nothing. It ran from the cradle at the head of the slip to the cradle at the foot like a line ruled on a drawing. When he put his eye to the end of it, there was nothing between his eye and the stem but oak.

He had slept beside it. He had not slept much.

The yard was hot already. Early summer had come to Cyrene while they were gone. The steam off the town's gutters, which had hung white over the roofs all winter, lay low and thin in the mornings now, and burned off soon after sunrise. The pitch-kettle by the shed door was going, and the yard smelled of hot pitch and green oak and the sea. Under all of it was the bad-egg breath of the springs, which a man stopped smelling after a week in Cyrene. He had been home eight days. He could still smell it.

"The garboard," said Velthur's boy.

"I know."

"You said to tell you when the—"

"I know." He got up off the slip, the bad leg first, and the knee told him about the night on the shavings. "Bring it."

The boy went for it. He was a town lad of fifteen or near it, all wrist and elbow still, whom Velthur had taken on last autumn because nobody else would have him, and he wore Velthur's plane in his belt.

When the fleet sailed for Stoneheart, Gaius had told him to keep the shed. He was to lay the frames for the second keel out on the floor and look at them every day, and not touch them with an edge until Gaius was back. Velthur would show him why. Velthur had been dead since the raid. The boy had looked at the frames all winter. In the spring, with nobody to show him anything, he had taken down the adze and laid the keel himself, from the frames and from what he remembered of the brothers' hands. He had laid it a finger out at the third scarf.

Gaius had seen it from the deck on the first morning, as they came alongside. He had said so on the slip, in front of the boy and half the yard, and the boy had said, "Yes, master," and gone and got the adze; and Gaius had been taking it up ever since, timber by timber. He was still at it on the eighth day, when the white cutter came in round the Kettle. He had finished it that night by the lamp, after the cutter had gone out with her.

It was right now. He kept his eye on it.

At the far end of the shed, past the steam-box and the frames, the house stood with its door on the latch. He had shut it himself last night, because the wind had come round and it was banging. He had not gone in. He had stood in the doorway with his hand on the bar and smelled the bread she had left on the board, and the herbs she hung over the hearth, and the cold hearth under them. Then he had taken his hand off the bar and gone back to the keel.

There was a smell of new bread coming across the yard now from the bakehouse at the foot of Rope Street. Most mornings that winter, and every morning since they came home, she had come down the hill from the Baths with the first of it under a cloth. She had made him eat it sitting on the keel, and argued with him about mortar.

It was the second hour. The bread was not coming.

"Master," said the boy, with the garboard plank on his shoulder.

"Not there," said Gaius. "In the shade. If the sun gets on it, it'll cup."

The boy stood with the plank on his shoulder.

"You know that," said Gaius. "I know you know that. Put it down."

"Yes, master."

---

She came down into the yard a little before the third hour, on her round.

He heard her before he saw her. There was a small bronze bell at the socket of her spear, under the head, and it spoke when she walked, once at every second step: not loud, a dry little *tink*. The yard had heard a great many things come down the crown's road in the last year and stopped for none of them. It stopped for that. Two of the sawyers in the pit looked up, and a boy at the pitch-kettle; and Rusticus and Salvius, who were caulking the old liburna on the hard and had been arguing about it since dawn, stopped arguing.

She stopped at the head of the yard, where the crown's road came down onto the cobbles.

She was in the white, and the steel cap, in this heat, which he would not have worn for anybody; and she had a wooden case under her left arm, the kind a physician carries, on a strap. She looked the yard over, the slips and the sheds, the stacked oak, the steam-box, the sawpit and the men, as a master goes over a hull that another yard has built.

Then she walked on down with the bell speaking, passed the keel without a glance, and went round the end of the shed toward the sea-wall.

He knew what was round there. He put the adze down.

The cohort's trench was round there. He had sited it himself in the winter, when the cohort came back from the Teeth and filled the old barracks on the sea-wall, and the barracks' own pit was under the Tharsian garrison's ash-heap. He had walked the ground and taken the fall and pegged it: a deep trench, with a soakaway at the low end, well away from the slips and the water-casks and down the slope from everything. The cohort had dug it in an afternoon and been grateful. He had been pleased with it.

She was standing at the low end of it, by the soakaway, with her nose wrinkled, looking at the ground.

"Who dug this?" she said, without turning round.

"The cohort."

"Who told them where?"

He had several answers.

"I did," he said.

She turned round then and took him in: the leather apron, the lenses pushed up on his forehead, the adze-dust, the knee. Her eyes were grey, and not unfriendly. They were the eyes of a woman at a badly made joint, seeing how badly.

"You're the engineer," she said.

"Yes."

"I was told about you." She did not say what. She turned back to the soakaway and pointed the butt of her spear at the ground beyond it, where the slope ran on down toward the sea-wall and the gutter along its foot. "Where does that go?"

"Down. Under the wall. Out."

"Does it."

"It's the low ground. I walked it. It falls a—" He stopped, because she would want a number, and it was none of her business. "It falls toward the wall. Everything falls toward the wall. It's a yard on a slope, Sister. I didn't put the slope there."

"No," she said. "You put a latrine on it."

She walked a few steps down the slope, away from the trench, with her head bent. At the foot of it, in the angle of the sea-wall where the barracks' cookhouse stood, there was a stone curb in the ground under a wooden cover. She stopped beside it, shifted her spear into the crook of her arm, and tapped the cover twice with the end of one finger.

"What's this?"

"The cookhouse well."

"How deep?"

"It's not deep. It's the spring. It comes up warm under the whole yard. They sank a shaft to it here so the cooks wouldn't have to carry from the fort."

"And when it rains?"

He was seeing it. He had walked that ground in a hard frost, when the clay of the slope was iron everywhere but where the warmth came up through it, and nothing soaked and nothing ran. He had pegged his fall and gone back to his hull. He had not walked it in summer. He had not walked it after rain. He saw the soakaway full, and the wet going down through the made ground under the cobbles, by the old warm channels the spring had cut for itself. It came out at the bottom, in the shaft where the cooks dipped their kettles every morning.

"It's warm ground," she said. "Warm ground drinks. Somebody told you a trench wants a fall, and you gave it one. You gave it one right into the cooks' pot." She took her finger off the well-cover. "I've seen a camp go down with the flux in a fortnight from less. The Lanciarii's camp below Hollowmere, the year before the war. We buried a score. They'd dug theirs above the horse-lines."

"Nobody's had the flux."

"It's not rained since you came home," she said. "It will."

The sun lay hot across his shoulders, and he hated her with a pure clean hatred he had not felt for anybody since the quaestor's man with the rod. She had seen it in the time it took to walk round the end of a shed.

"Where?" he said.

She pointed with the spear-butt, across the yard the other way, past the sawpit, to the corner by the old slip, where nothing was and the ground fell toward the open harbor and away from everything.

"There," she said. "Fill this one in with lime. And shift the soakaway, or don't have one. Let it go to the sea. The sea doesn't mind."

It was where he would have put it. If he had walked the ground in summer.

"I'll have it moved," he said.

"Today."

"Today."

She gave one nod, as though she had heard the last nail go home, and climbed the slope past him. The bell went up the yard with her, and up the crown's road, until he could not hear it.

When he got back round the end of the shed, Rusticus and Salvius were standing on the hard by the liburna with their mallets in their hands.

"Well," said Rusticus.

"She's right," said Gaius.

"About what?"

"Get spades," said Gaius. "Both of you. And lime from the kiln. And the boy."

---

They had the new trench dug and the old one limed by noon. After that the adze turned on a knot and went into the side of his hand, below the little finger.

It was not much, and it was his own fault. He had been holding the adze too far down the haft, and a man who holds an adze angry gets what it gives him. He wrapped the hand in a rag and kept on. The rag soaked through. In the end the boy, who had been watching him, said, "Master," in a voice he had not used before; and Gaius saw that he was bleeding on the keel.

He went up to the Baths.

It was a long way up for the knee in the heat, by the crown's road and the stepped lane. Halfway he stopped to rest the leg on a step. He stood there with the hand wrapped against his apron, above the yard and the harbor and the gap, where the white sail had gone out at dusk the night before. Then he went on up, because he was dripping on the step.

The Baths were the Baths: the long hall full of steam, the old pools, the smell of the warm water and the sulphur and the lye. The pallets lay under the high windows, half of them empty in the summer, and the still-room was at the end, with the herbs hung over its door. He had slept in that still-room, back to back with her on a mat, a good many nights. He did not look at the door of it.

Hostus was at the basin, washing his hands.

He was a thin, stooped man going grey, with a barber's long careful fingers and a long patient face. He washed in the hot basin from the spring, and then in the lye-water, and then in the hot again, slowly, between the fingers and under the nails and round the wrists. He dried them on a clean cloth, one finger at a time, and hung the cloth over the rail.

"Engineer," he said. "What have you done."

"Adze."

"Sit."

Gaius sat on the bench by the basin and gave Hostus the hand.

Sister Domitia came down the hall from the far end, where she had been standing over a pallet, and stopped beside them while Hostus unwrapped it. It was a clean cut, in at the side of the palm below the little finger and along the edge of the hand, not deep, and bleeding freely. Hostus turned it to the light from the high window, pressed the lips of it together with two fingers, and let them go.

"It wants sewing," Hostus said. "Three. Four. Then he keeps it dry."

"He won't," said Sister Domitia. "You'll be in the pitch by tomorrow, and the bilge water the day after, and the stitches will be black by the end of the week." She leaned her spear against the basin. "Sew it, Barber, by all means. Then bind it so it can't open. Not a rag. Linen, and a pad, and bound over the pad, and the hand in a sling." She jerked her chin up the hall at the pallet she had come from. "Not like *that*."

On the pallet at the far end, in the shade, lay Ear, who had limped up the hill an hour before him. He had a gash down his shin where somebody had dropped an oar on it at the cutter the day before, and a strip of shirt tied round it. Gaius had seen him tie it himself, sitting on the quay, with his teeth.

The strip of shirt was still on it.

"I didn't like to take it off," said Ear unhappily, from the pallet. "It's holding."

"That's not a dressing," said Sister Domitia. "That's a hope with a bandage on it. Why wasn't it brought up here yesterday?"

Ear looked at the strip of shirt.

"She was going," he said.

Sister Domitia marched up the hall to him. Gaius heard her behind him, while Hostus threaded a curved needle at the window: a low flat voice, telling Ear what a man ought to know by his age, and Ear saying *Yes, Sister*, and *Yes, Sister*, and once, *Ow*.

Hostus sewed the hand. He was quick, and his fingers were as clean as a new-planed board, and Gaius, who had been sewn before, watched the needle go in and out and felt it from a long way off. When it was done Hostus washed again, as before. Sister Domitia came back down the hall with the strip of shirt held out from her by one corner, dropped it in the slop-bucket, and stopped at the basin beside Hostus to wash her own hands.

She watched his.

He was drying them on the clean cloth, one finger at a time. She stood beside him with her own hands still wet. Her right hand lifted off the rim of the basin with one finger out, the finger that tapped things. It came up, and stopped, and stayed where it was, a little way above the stone, while Hostus dried the last finger and hung the cloth over the rail.

The finger went down again.

She washed her own the same way, the hot and the lye and the hot, and dried them on a cloth of her own.

"You'll sew that boy's leg next, Barber," she said. Then she took the linen and the pad and bound up Gaius's hand herself, over Hostus's stitches, tight and flat, in a figure that crossed at the back of the wrist.

After that she looked at the knee. He was sitting with it out straight in front of him, because the climb had stiffened it, and she looked at it as she had looked at his trench.

"That knee," she said.

"No."

"I haven't asked you anything."

"No," said Gaius.

Neither of them moved.

"It's an old injury," she said. "It's gone in the joint. I can see it from here, how you hold it. A sister could look at that. I could look at it now. It would take me the time it takes to boil a kettle. It wants seeing to before it sets worse, and it will set worse, at your age, in a shipyard, on stone."

"It's waiting," said Gaius.

Sister Domitia rolled up the end of the linen, tight, and put it in her case, and shut the case.

"Keep it dry," she said, and nodded at the hand, and left him.

---

The cohort ate on the sea-wall in the long summer evening, as it had every evening since they came home. The trestles and their benches stood outside the old barracks, where the wall was broad and warm and caught the last of the sun.

Oppius had a sheep on the spit over the cookhouse fire, with the fat dripping and spitting in the coals. Fausta's bread was passed down the trestles in baskets. There were onions done in their skins in the embers, and a pot of beans with pork in it, and somebody's jar of the yellow Cyrene wine that tasted of the springs going round. The smell of the mutton lay along the whole wall. The sun dropped behind the fort on the hill, and the harbor turned gold, and the steam rose off it in the cool.

Gaius ate at the end of a trestle with his hand in its sling, left-handed, badly. He had spent the afternoon on the slip being no use to anybody. He had not spoken to anybody, and his hand hurt.

Sister Domitia came down from the Baths at sunset. She walked the length of the trestles, looking at everybody's supper, and stopped behind Fingers to watch him eat. Fingers ate as the cohort ate, as men eat who have spent years in a prison where the slow went hungry. He stood bent over the trestle with his knife in one hand and his bread in the other, and the meat going into him in pieces too big for the job.

She tapped the trestle twice beside his bowl.

"Sit down," she said. "And chew it. You'll none of you have a gut left by forty, eating like that. I've opened men who ate like that, after. Their insides were like a cart-road."

Fingers stared up at her with his mouth full.

Then he sat down. And, as the trestle watched, chewed.

She worked on down the wall, and stopped behind the next man, and the next. By the time she reached the end of it the whole cohort was sitting in a long row eating its supper slowly with its mouth shut, like a bench of magistrates. She took a bowl of the beans herself and a heel of bread, sat down across the trestle from Gaius, and ate them, not slowly. She went back up the hill as the light went.

They watched her go, and listened, until the bell at her spear had gone all the way up the crown's road and round the turn.

"Right," said Fingers.

He put his bowl down and went into the barracks. He came out with the broom from beside the door, pulled a handful of straws out of its head, and broke them on his knee in the middle of the wall: one short, the rest long. He shuffled them behind his back. Then he held out his fist, with the ends showing level above his knuckles.

"Every man," he said. "Draw."

"For what?" said somebody.

"For who has to be nice to her."

The cohort began to laugh, the low tired laugh it had brought home from Bastion, and got up off the benches to draw. The fist went down the wall from man to man. Fingers held it steady and watched each man draw, and each man laughed or swore and threw his straw in the harbor. Fingers took his fist past Gaius without stopping.

"Not you, Engineer," said Fingers. "She's had her go at you."

Ear drew. Pen drew, with his long white fingers, delicately, and held his straw up to the last of the light and said, "Saved by the length of a straw. It's how I've lived." Oppius drew; and Rufo, who had come down from the fort with Sylara for his supper, drew long, and grinned at it, and put his hand up to the plait over his ear.

Salvius drew long.

Rusticus drew short.

He turned it round in his fingers, as if there might be another end to it.

"No," said Rusticus.

"Short straw," said Fingers. "It's the law."

"It's a broom."

"It's the law of the broom," said Fingers. He opened his fist and wiped it on his breeches, got out of his shirt the chalk he kept for the purpose, and squatted by the barracks wall where there was a clean place on the stone. The tip of his tongue came out between his teeth. He wrote *Rusticus* on the stone, big and uneven, and under it a line, and on the line the odds.

"How long he lasts," Fingers said, without looking up. "Before she has him crying. I'll take a day. I'll take a week, at long odds. Nobody's taking a month. Oppius? A day?"

"A day," said Oppius. "On the first hour."

"I'll take the morning," said Pen.

"You're all very funny," said Rusticus.

"Hold on," said Hostus.

He was sitting at the far end of the wall, at the end of the last trestle, with his back to the cookhouse and a bowl of beans on his knee, where he had been all through supper. Gaius had not noticed him there. People did not notice Hostus; it was the main thing about him. He put the bowl down on the trestle beside him, wiped his mouth on a clean cloth, folded the cloth, and stood up.

"I'll do it," said Hostus.

The wall looked at him.

"You didn't draw," said Fingers.

"No."

"You can't take a man's short straw if you didn't draw. It's—"

"I know. The law of the broom." Hostus walked along the wall. He was not a big man, or a loud one, and he did not raise his voice now. He stopped by Fingers, over the chalk on the stone. "I work under her. Every day, up there. I'll be nice to her anyway, or we'll kill each other by the end of the week, and then there'll be nobody to sew Ear up." He held out his hand to Rusticus. "Give me your straw."

Rusticus gave it to him at once, and put both hands behind his back.

"She'll eat you," said Fingers.

"I've pulled teeth for thirty years," said Hostus. "I have talked kindly to worse-tempered people with my hands in their mouths."

Fingers squatted there and looked up at him. Then he rubbed out *Rusticus* with the heel of his hand, wrote *Hostus* with the line under it, and turned to the wall.

"Well?" he said. "A day? A week? A month, at evens?"

Down the wall, the cohort chewed.

"Nobody?" said Fingers.

Nobody.

Fingers crouched over the stone a moment longer. Then he rubbed the whole of it out with the flat of his hand, odds and line and name. He stood up, dusted off his hand, and went to get his bowl.

Hostus sat down again at the end of the wall and picked up his beans. He rubbed the back of his neck once with his free hand, and went on eating, slowly, with his mouth shut.

---

The next morning Gaius went up to have the hand looked at, because the binding had come loose in the night and Hostus had said to come.

It had come loose because he had slept on it, on the slip, by the keel. That was nobody's business. He climbed the crown's road in the cool, before the heat, with the sun just clear of the eastern sea, the town's steam lying in the lanes and the harbor flat as a plate below him. At the top of the stepped lane, by the door of the Baths, the hot spring ran out of the rock into its stone trough and steamed. There he stopped, because Sister Domitia and Hostus were standing at the trough.

They had been washing there before the day's work, side by side. Now they had stopped.

Sister Domitia had Hostus by the chin.

She had taken hold of it with her right hand, firmly, and turned his face up to the light, as Gaius would have turned a board to read the grain. Hostus let her. He was a little taller than she was, and she had to tip his head back to do it. He stood with his head back and his clean hands hanging at his sides, as a man stands for a sentence.

"Open," said Sister Domitia.

Hostus opened.

She looked in, as Gaius had seen Hostus look into a hundred mouths at the Baths, with the side of one finger drawing down his lower lip so that she could see the gums. She turned his chin a little to the left, and to the right, and took her time about it, and Hostus stared at the sky.

Then she let go of his chin.

"Well," she said. "Those are the best teeth in the camp."

Hostus shut his mouth.

"We're taught it," said Sister Domitia, wiping her fingers on her apron. "The old books. You judge a man's diet by his teeth and his gums before you judge it by anything he tells you, because he'll lie about what he eats and his mouth won't. I've been in every mouth in your cohort that would let me since yesterday." She sniffed. "Bread and salt meat and sour wine, and half of them at the honey-cakes in the capital since they were boys. Ruins. I've seen better in a sheep." She looked him up and down: the stoop, the long fingers, the clean apron. "Not yours. Yours are good. Every one. Who looks after them for you?"

"Nobody," said Hostus.

Sister Domitia waited.

"I've drawn other men's," he said. "Thirty years. Every tooth in Tanner's Lane." He stopped. He seemed to be looking for something in the air above her coif, and not finding it. "Nobody's ever—" He stopped again. "That's the first time," said Hostus, "anybody's looked."

"Well, they should have," said Sister Domitia. "It's a good mouth. You've looked after it. Keep on." She took her spear from where it leaned against the trough, and the bell spoke. "Ward's at the second hour. Don't be late."

She went in.

Hostus stood by the trough in the steam. He put up his hand and rubbed the back of his neck, and went on rubbing it, staring at the door of the Baths. Above his collar, under his hand, the back of his neck had gone a deep and spreading red.

Gaius discovered that he had urgent business with a keel.

He went down the stepped lane again, fast for the knee, and down the crown's road with his hand in its loose binding. At the head of the slip he stood and studied the keel, hard, until he could trust his face.

Fingers was sitting on the sawhorse by the shed door with his breakfast.

"You're back quick," Fingers said. "She do your hand?"

"No."

Fingers looked at him. Then he turned his eyes up the hill toward the Baths, where there was nothing to see but roofs and steam; and the tip of his tongue came out between his teeth.

"Hostus went up before light," Fingers said. "Shaved."

He got up and went round to the barracks wall, where yesterday's chalk was still a grey smear on the stone. He wrote *Hostus* again, small, low down, where only a man who was looking would see it, and drew a line; and under the line, after some thought, he wrote *oil*.

"For what?" said Gaius, who had followed him.

"Her lamp," said Fingers. "A man who's sweet on a sister of the White keeps her lamp in oil, all year, till Midwinter. There's a song about it. The Lanciarii have one too, but ours rhymes." He wrote the odds. They were short. "How long before he's buying her oil, is the question."

"You've only got him."

"Only need him." Fingers stood back from the wall. "I'll take a month," he said. "Anybody'll give me a month."

---

She came down the crown's road at the third hour that morning, on her round. She had the physician's case and a roll of linen under her arm, the spear in her hand, and her own bag over her shoulder with the day's dressings in it. At the head of the yard Ear stepped out of the shed in front of her, with his weight off his sewn leg, and held out his hand.

"Can we help, Sister?" said Ear.

"I'm not a cripple, boy."

"No, Sister." Ear went on holding out his hand. He was nineteen, and the ear that had been mended on the black beach was a pale crumpled shape on the side of his head. He had the look of a boy who has been told to do a thing and will go on doing it until somebody tells him to stop. "Only we always ask."

"Who's *we*?"

Ear thought about this.

"Everybody," he said.

Sister Domitia looked past him at the yard: at Rusticus and Salvius on the hard by the liburna, who had stopped work to watch; at the sawyers in the pit and Velthur's boy; and last at Gaius, on the slip. Nobody was smiling.

She took the bag off her shoulder and gave it to Ear.

"Carry it level," she said. "There's bottles in it."

He carried it level, down the yard behind her and round the end of the shed to the barracks, as a man carries a full pail of pitch. After that somebody carried it every day. It was never the same man two days running. Gaius never saw them settle it, and he never saw her ask. Whoever was nearest when she came down the crown's road stepped out and held out his hand and said what they always said, and she gave it to him and said, "Level."

She stopped saying, "I'm not a cripple." It took about a week.

---

She corrected the rest of Cyrene after that. The cohort learned to stand still when they heard the bell, because if a man was doing something she would see it, and it would be wrong. At night, by a lamp at the end of the Baths that Gaius could see from the slip, she wrote it all down for the Knight-Abbess.

She corrected how they washed. She had the barracks' blankets out on the sea-wall in the sun every third day, and every man's shirt boiled in the cookhouse copper with lye, and stood over the copper while it was done. She moved the Lanciarii's watch on the mole-head into the shade of the tower, with a bucket of water by them and a hat on every head. She corrected the way Gaius's sawyers stood in the pit, which had nothing to do with her; it turned out that she was a sawyer's daughter from the capital, and the pitman's back stopped hurting. On the morning Valeria sailed north on the *Patience*, Sister Domitia stopped her on the long quay in front of her own Lanciarii. There was a check in the Farrier's haft, she said, a finger below the socket, and it would split at the first hard blow. Valeria turned her spear to the light, and there was the check.

She was wrong about half of it. She told Sylara the wrong herb for an elf's fever, and Sylara told her the right one. She told Fausta the bread wanted more salt, and Fausta said nothing and sent her up a loaf with more salt in it, and she ate it and drank a jug of water and never mentioned it again. She told Gaius his steam-box was too hot. It was not. But she was right about the other half, and she was right in such a voice, and with such a finger, that the cohort did the wrong half too, mostly, in case.

Hostus began to do small things wrong. Going up to have his stitches looked at, Gaius found him on the step of the Baths in the sun, rolling a bandage the wrong way, slowly, while the bell came up the stepped lane.

The bell at her spear went up and down the crown's road all day. When it stopped, the cohort turned round to see what was wrong.

---

The *Patience* brought the mail on the fifteenth day, on the morning flood. She came every week.

Pen always met her. He stood on the long quay by her plank with his weight on one hip while the bags came off. Then he carried the capital's bag up to the harbor master's house and sorted it on the step in the shade, as he had all winter. He did it by feel through the canvas before the strings were off, and then by eye, into piles: the Prefect's, the fort's, the town's, the cohort's. He could tell a letter from a woman by the feel of the fold.

Gaius was not waiting for him. He was on the slip with the boy, setting up the garboard, with his back to the quay.

"Engineer," said Pen, at his elbow.

He had come down the yard without Gaius hearing him, as he did, with the cohort's letters in a bundle under his arm. One he held out by itself between two long fingers: a half sheet, folded small, with plain white wax and no seal in it.

"Half a sheet," said Pen, "by the feel." And he was off up the yard with the bundle.

Gaius put down his end of the plank. The boy, at the other end of it, held his.

The hand was hers. He had seen it on a great many things: on the labels of the still-room jars, on stores lists, on a note pinned to a cloth over a bowl of something she meant him to eat. It was a small round hand, out of some schoolroom with a governess in it, a long time ago. He broke the wax with a fingernail and read the letter standing on the slip.

*Gaius,*

*We came up the canal on the third morning, on the flood. The house is the same. The steps to the water are worn deeper in the middle, and somebody has whitewashed the gate. I have a room on the court side with a window that opens, and a bed, and a shelf.*

*I am working in the infirmary in the mornings with the sisters, and at the gate in the afternoons, where the canal people come with what's wrong with them. It is mostly lungs, and children's ears, and what comes of the lock-gates.*

*I eat at the bells. Tell Fausta the bread here is not as good as hers, and that I said so.*

*Is the keel down?*

*L.*

He read it again, more slowly, as he would have gone over another man's drawing: for what was on it, and then for what was not.

There was nothing that was not. It was a letter.

He folded it small and put it in the front of his apron, behind the bib.

"Master," said the boy, holding the plank. "Is it all right?"

"Yes," said Gaius. "Lift your end."

---

By then Sister Domitia had begun to eat like Liora.

It went up like a wall a man walks past every day: one morning there was a course, and the next morning there were two, and on the fifth morning it was a wall.

He saw the first course of it on the twelfth day. She stopped at the cookhouse on the sea-wall on her round, at the hour of the noon bell, and asked Oppius what the healer had eaten. Gaius was at the well at the foot of the slope, drawing a bucket for the steam-box.

"Ate?" said Oppius. He was a giant, with one eye, and he peered down at her with it from the cookhouse door as though nobody had ever asked him a harder question. "Whatever was going, Sister. She wasn't particular."

"Yes, she was," said Sister Domitia.

Oppius thought about it.

"Fish," he said. "When there was fish. Bread. Greens when she could get them, which in the winter she couldn't, so she'd go up the hill for the sorrel by the spring and come back with it in her apron. Water out of the fort spring." He thought harder. "Not after dark. Not anything. You'd bring her a bit of sausage at night when she'd been working, and she'd thank you, and give it to the engineer." He looked across at Gaius by the well, and so did she. "And she walked. Everywhere. Up and down that hill ten times a day, when she could have sent a boy."

Sister Domitia stood in the cookhouse door with her spear.

"That's the old rule," she said, more to herself than to Oppius. "Out of the books. The rule of the bells. Plain food at the bells, and the spring's water, and nothing after the dusk bell, and the rounds on foot." She made a small noise in her nose. "Nobody keeps that. Nobody's kept it in the tents in twenty years. You can't, in the tents. You eat what comes, when it comes, on your feet, and you're glad of it."

"She did," said Oppius.

She stayed where she was.

"What's the fish?" she said.

He gave her a gurnard done on the coals, and a heel of bread, and she ate it standing in the cookhouse door. Then she noticed that she was standing, and sat down on the wall to finish it, with her spear across her knees, scowling at the harbor.

After that she ate at the bells. There were no bells at Cyrene but the fort's alarm and the harbor bell, which had not been rung even for the raid. So she kept her own, in her head, by the sun, and the cohort learned to tell the hour by her. When she came down to the cookhouse, it was the noon bell. When she went up the hill, it was the dusk bell. She ate fish when there was fish, and when there was not she ate the mutton and the bread and the greens. She grumbled about all of it, the rule and the old books and the women who had written them; and she ate it anyway, and walked her round twice a day, down the crown's road and up again.

On the sixteenth evening Oppius brought her a piece of sausage after the sun was down. She had been at the Baths since noon with a fisherman's boy who had come off a mole-head onto the rocks.

It lay on its bit of bread in front of her. Gaius, at the next trestle, could smell it: pork and garlic and fennel, still hissing.

"That's after the bell," she said.

"There isn't a bell, Sister," said Oppius, kindly.

"There's always a bell." She pushed it back across the trestle to him with the tip of one finger. "Give it to the engineer."

Oppius pushed it along to Gaius.

He ate it. It was good sausage.

---

She came to the song on the twentieth night.

There was a fire on the sea-wall that night after supper, for no reason, as there sometimes was now that it was summer: driftwood from the black beach and offcuts from the yard, burning green and blue with the salt. The cohort sat round it on the wall in the dusk and passed the yellow wine, and Oppius brought out the end of the mutton. Rufo started *The Angel of the Baths*, with Sylara against his shoulder.

It was the cohort's song. A man of the Fourteenth had made it on a pallet up the hill, to a marching tune, before ever the cohort came to Cyrene; but it had been the cohort's since the Boundary House. They sang it as men sing a thing they have sung on a hundred nights, without listening to it, in a long low rough unison that went out over the harbor and came back off the moles. Gaius did not sing. He sat on the end of the wall with his knee out straight and listened to it.

Sister Domitia was there. She had come down at sunset for her fish and stayed, which she had not done before. She sat on an upturned basket at the edge of the fire with her spear across her knees, and Hostus sat on the wall beside her, a little way off. She listened to the song with her head up, as a schoolmistress listens to a child recite.

They sang it through. It came to the last chorus, and the cohort sang the chorus, and it ended, and somebody passed the wine.

"That's wrong," said Sister Domitia.

The wall turned to her.

"It's wrong," she said. "You've left a verse out." She lifted one finger off the spear and tapped the shaft twice. "I've heard it a hundred times in the capital this spring. They sing it on every corner in the lower town, and in the infirmary, and at the Abbess's gate, so I'd know.
