There's the one about the stair, and then the one about the beach, and then there's one that goes up high, before the last chorus, about the dark. Then the chorus. You went straight from the beach to the chorus. You've forgotten it."

The fire cracked. Somebody's foot moved on the stones.

Sister Domitia looked round the wall from face to face, as though waiting for somebody to tell her she was wrong; and nobody did. They watched the fire. Rufo had stopped with the jar halfway to his mouth, and put it down. Sylara had lifted her head off his shoulder. Ear was looking at his hands.

It was Fingers who answered her in the end. He was sitting on the wall with his back to the harbor, and he did not look up.

"We've not forgotten it," Fingers said. "That's Tappo's verse."

"Who's Tappo?"

"He sang it on his own. The high bit. We'd all stop, and he'd sing it, and then we'd come in again on the chorus." He was turning a chip of driftwood over in his fingers. "He started it, at the Boundary House. The night she let us have it. We don't sing it. Not since Bastion."

Sister Domitia sat on her basket.

Gaius was on the far side of the fire from her, and the light of it was on her face: the starched coif, the broad weathered cheeks, the grey eyes on Fingers. Her finger had come up off the spear-shaft again for the tap. It stayed up.

Then it came down, slowly, flat on the wood, and stayed there.

"Tappo," she said.

"Yes."

Beside her on the wall Hostus was looking at the fire.

"Who else?" said Sister Domitia.

Fingers lifted his head.

"I don't know the names," she said. "She does. The healer. She says them every night, at the end of the day, all of them. The Abbess told me so before I came. I've not learned them." She said it in the flat voice she had used on Ear. "I'm sent in her place. I ought to know them."

"Bruttius," said Oppius, from the cookhouse door.

"Trebonius," said Rufo, after a time.

"Egnatius," said Ear, to his hands.

There was a pause, and then somebody said, "Sosius," and then nobody said anything more. Sister Domitia had looked at each of them as they spoke, and then at the fire, with her mouth shut hard. She got up at last and took her spear, and the bell spoke; and she went up the crown's road in the dark with the bell going at every second step, and nobody offered to carry anything.

Gaius sat on the wall.

He had all of them. He had heard them in their places on every night he had slept in the still-room up the hill, and the three of Tharsis in theirs, which nobody on that wall would ever say. He had not said any of them.

The next morning she asked Hostus for the rest of them, back to the stair, and Rufo, who had the roll. That evening Gaius saw her on the long quay with Ear. Ear was talking. She walked beside him with her head bent, as she had walked the slope of the yard looking for the fall.

---

The second letter came on the twenty-second day. It was shorter.

*Gaius,*

*The canal is low with the summer, and smells of it. A boy from the lock-gates came to the gate with two fingers crushed, and I kept them both.*

*The doors here are shut at the dusk bell, as they always were. I had forgotten how loud the bar is.*

*Mind the knee on the stepped lane. Tell Domitia she's to make Hostus drink his own broth.*

*L.*

He read it standing on the slip, and again sitting on the keel, and again that night by the lamp in the shed with his lenses on, as though the writing were small.

*I had forgotten how loud the bar is.*

He went over it. It was in the same hand as the first, small and round and level, and the lines ran down the page as straight as the first letter's had. But they were closer together. She had written it on the same size of sheet, and left half the sheet bare at the bottom, and still the lines were closer together.

He folded it and put it behind the bib of his apron, with the first.

---

That evening, as on every evening before the *Patience* sailed, Pen wrote the cohort's letters.

He had always written them. Half the cohort could not write and the other half could not spell, and Pen could do anything with a pen that a man could ask of one, and some things nobody had. He sat on the step of the harbor master's house with his lap-desk on his knees, and the cohort queued in front of him in the long light. He wrote their letters home: to mothers in the capital, and to the women some of them had found at Stoneheart. He wrote to Fausta, still, for Oppius, though she lived at the foot of Rope Street and Oppius saw her every morning. Pen read each one back aloud in his drawling herald's voice, improving it as he went. Then he copied it fair for the bag in his upright clerk's hand, and kept the first draft.

"A man ought to keep a copy," he said, when Rusticus asked him what he wanted with it. "Of anything that matters."

Gaius did not queue. He had one person to write to, and he wrote that himself.

He had written it on the slip, on his knee, on a page torn from the back of his book of hull lines, in the plain capitals he had learned off the lintels at Corvus as a boy. It had taken him three nights to write four lines.

*The keel is down. The garboards are on. Domitia moved my latrine and she was right. Fausta says she knew.*

He had read it through on the third night, and could not see that it said anything at all, and could not see what else to put. He had folded it and sealed it with pitch, for want of wax. He took it up to Pen on the step.

"For the capital," he said.

Pen took it. He weighed it between two fingers, as he weighed all of them, and set it on the pile.

He was writing for Rusticus and Salvius, to their mother, who sold eels on the Fishmarket Steps in the capital. The two of them stood in front of the step together, arguing in whispers about what to put. Gaius stood by and watched the pen go. It was a good hand: the downstroke and the hairline, every letter the weight of the one beside it, nothing wasted. *Your loving sons.* Pen sanded it and turned it over, and wrote the direction on the back, small and neat: *To the mother of Rusticus and Salvius, who sells eels on the Fishmarket Steps, on the third step, the dry one.* Then he dipped again and wrote the date in the corner, as the clerks did, with the month and the day in figures. Gaius watched a seven go down, quick and neat, with the little hooked foot at the bottom that Pen's sevens had when he was not minding them.

It was a good seven.

He went back to the yard.

---

The third letter came on the twenty-ninth day. It was three lines.

*Gaius,*

*Cassandra came into the infirmary today and stood at the end of the ward while I worked, and then she went out again. I am well. I am eating at the bells.*

*L.*

He turned it over. The back was blank.

The boy, at the other end of the strake they were offering up, held his end and waited. He had stopped asking "Master, is it all right?" some time after the second letter. Gaius did not know when.

---

On the thirty-sixth day the *Patience* came in with the mail, and Pen met her, and carried the bag up to the step and sorted it; and he did not come down the yard.

Gaius was on the staging at the hull's stern, fairing the frames with an adze. He had taken the sling off the morning after the cut, against everyone's advice. The stitches had gone black by the end of the week, as she had said they would. Hostus had taken them out without a word, and Domitia had looked at the scar and said nothing either, which was worse. He worked. He kept his back to the quay. The morning went by, and the noon; and in the middle of the afternoon he lifted his head from the frame and saw Marcus coming down the yard from the crown's road, alone, with a letter in his hand.

It was not his letter. He knew that as he knew a timber was wrong before he had the rule on it. He put down the adze.

Marcus came to the foot of the staging and stood there looking at the frames and the half-built hull, as he always did in the yard, as though he had come to see the work. The cut on his forearm from the gate night was a seam now. He had the letter folded small in his hand, with the broken white wax on it.

"Come down," Marcus said.

Gaius came down. He was slow about it, with the knee, and Marcus did not offer to help, and did not look away either.

"Read it," said Marcus, and held it out.

It was in a secretary's hand, quick and slanting, on good paper, and addressed to *His Highness the Prefect of Cyrene*. It presented the Abbess's compliments. It said that the healer Liora was in reflection in the order's house, as the order had required, and would write to her friends when her reflection was finished. The Abbess trusted that His Highness would understand, and would not look for letters until then. It was signed, *For the Knight-Abbess, by her hand, Sister Philippa, Secretary.*

He gave it back.

"When what's finished?" said Gaius.

"It doesn't say."

"No."

They stood at the foot of the staging. The yard went on round them: the adzes and the mallets, the saw in the pit, the smell of the pitch, the sea. The boy was up on the staging above them, fairing the frame Gaius had left, with his eyes on his work.

"I've written to the Abbess," Marcus said. "This afternoon. And to my father." He folded the letter again. "My father answers everything, in his own time. The Abbess will answer too, very politely, and say this again." He put it inside his coat. "It's the one house in the kingdom the crown can't walk into, Gaius. They built it so."

"I know."

"So I'll write," said Marcus. "And wait. And write again."

Gaius turned to the hull.

"She said she wouldn't fight her way out of anything," he said.

"Yes."

"She didn't say anything about anybody else."

Marcus turned his head.

Gaius did not look round. He kept his eyes on the run of the frames along the hull, which were fair now from the stern to the turn of the bilge, and would be fair to the stem by the end of the week.

"Don't tell me," said Marcus at last.

"I've nothing to tell."

"No. When you have," said Marcus, "tell me first." He tipped his head at the boy on the staging. "That's Velthur's boy."

"Yes."

"He's good?"

"He's good," said Gaius. "He laid her a finger out at the third scarf. I took it up."

"Did you tell him he was good?"

Up on the staging the boy had stopped fairing. He stood with the adze in his hand and his back to them, very still.

"No," said Gaius.

Marcus nodded and went back up the yard toward the crown's road, with his hand on the front of his coat where the letter was.

Gaius stood at the foot of the staging.

"Boy," he said.

The boy turned round.

"Your scarfs," said Gaius. "Apart from the third. They were good scarfs."

The boy stood on the staging above him. Then he turned back to the frame and went on fairing it, without a word; and twice he stopped and put his forearm across his eyes, and went on.

---

The book was in Netmakers' Row.

He had lent it to Alana in the winter for her letters class. The hot sand out of it had gone onto the Navarchy's marines on the long stepped lane: the only use, so far as he knew, that anybody had ever made of the third volume. The child in the front of the class had asked to keep it to copy the plates. Alana had said she must ask the engineer, and then the engineer had sailed.

He went up the Row in the evening. It was a steep cobbled street of net-lofts and narrow houses, with the nets hung out to dry from the upper windows and the smell of tar and fish-scale and wet hemp in it. Halfway up he passed the window where Tappo's red lamp burned. At the top, when he asked, a woman at a door turned and called up the stairs behind her for Ennia, and a small dark girl came down them with the book in both arms, held against her chest.

She did not give it to him.

"You're the engineer," said Ennia.

"Yes."

"It's yours."

"Yes."

"I've copied the plates." She held it tighter. "Not all. There's the one with the crane. I hadn't done the crane." She had a thin fierce face, a great deal of ink on her fingers, and a front tooth missing. "Do you want it now? For good?"

"For a week."

"You'll bring it back?"

"In a week."

"You've to sign for it," said Ennia. She ran back up the stairs and down again with a slate and a stub of chalk, and held them up to him. He wrote his name on the slate in his lintel capitals. She turned the slate over and checked the back of it as well, and gave him the book.

He took it down the Row under his arm and sat with it on the sea-wall by the yard, with the harbor going grey below him, and opened it.

The third volume of the great Anaxis, on war-machines. The leather was cracked at the spine and the boards were warped. A child's ink was on the edges of the pages where she had turned them, and a child's slate-dust in the gutters. Nobody read the third volume. He had told Liora so at Silverwood, across a stump. She had told him about the plate of the stone-thrower, with its pieces drawn out round the edge, and how as a girl she used to cover the lines with her hand and put it together in her head.

He turned the pages. Bolt-throwers and their skeins. The *palintone*. He put his hand over the lines and put it together in his head, and it went together at once. He took his hand away.

Rams. Towers. The cauldrons of the hot sand, which a child had copied out somewhere in the Row; he wondered how she had done the flames.

He was hunting for something, and did not know what until it was under his hand.

It was near the back, after the towers: a plate he had forgotten was there. Two men under a roof of shields, at the foot of a gate. Between them, on the ground, stood a thing like a stool. It was a squat oak frame with a great bronze screw standing up through the middle of it, threaded its whole length, and a bar through the head of the screw for the two men to turn. The head of the screw was set under the bottom rail of the gate's leaf, at the end where it hung.

He had jack-screws in the shed, laid by against a hull sticking on her ways. He had never once thought of a gate.

The text beside the plate was in Anaxis's dry old crabbed style, which he had loved as a boy and loved still. *A gate hung upon pintles is lifted from them, not broken; and a gate lifted from its pintles has no more strength in it than a shutter in the wind. For which the screw-jack, as here, worked by two men under cover, who raise the leaf the depth of the pintle and a little more, when it will fall outward of its own weight. It is quieter than a ram, and it wants no run-up, and it does not need to break anything.*

He sat on the sea-wall with the book on his knees until it was too dark to see the plate. Then he went up the hill to find Domitia.

---

She was at the Baths, at the long table at the end of the hall under the hanging herbs, writing.

It was her letter to the Knight-Abbess: a page every night and sometimes two, in a big plain hand, sealed and put in the Vigil's bag for the *Patience*. The whole cohort knew about it. Some of them thought it was funny, and some of them did not.

Her head came up when the door went. It was after dusk. There was a cup of the spring's water by her, and nothing else on the table but the page and the lamp.

"Engineer," she said. "Is it the hand?"

"No."

"The knee."

"No." He walked up the hall. There was a fisherman's boy asleep on one of the pallets with his leg in splints, and nobody else. "The house," he said. "In the capital. Your order's house, by the canal."

Her pen stopped.

"What about it?"

He had thought about this all the way up the hill, and he had not thought of a good way. He did not have good ways. He had walls, and he knew where to put the weight on them.

"Liora said in her letter," he said, "that she'd forgotten how loud the bar is. On the door, when they shut it at the dusk bell." He stood at the end of the table. "I've been trying to see it. I'm a builder; I see things by how they're made. I'd have thought a door that size would hang on great strap hinges, a hand thick, iron, bolted through—"

"Pintles," said Domitia.

He waited.

"Strap and pintle," she said. "Not hinges. *Hinges*, he says." She put down her pen. "The straps are iron, and they run the width of each leaf, three to a leaf. They're forged with an eye at the hinge end, and the eyes drop over iron pintles leaded into the stone of the jamb. I oiled them myself as a novice, every spring for four years. You stand on a ladder with a feather and a pot, and the leaf hangs over your head like the lid of a coffin, and if you drop the pot Sister Agrippina makes you scrub the porch." She sniffed. "Bolted through. Nobody bolts a strap through the leaf on a door that size. You'd split it. They're clenched."

"Clenched," said Gaius.

"Nails, clenched over on the inside. Every hand's length. I'd have thought a builder knew that."

"It's a big door, then."

"It's two doors. Two leaves." She laid her right arm along the edge of the table, from the elbow to the fingertips. "That," she said, "and that again, and a bit, for the width of each leaf. And the height of the porch roof. Oak. Four fingers thick. Ledged on the inside, not braced."

"How high's that?"

"The top pintle wants the fifth rung of the ladder," said Domitia, "and a stretch."

"May I?" he said.

She looked at him, and at the boxwood rule he had taken out of his apron. Then she held her arm still.

He laid the rule along it, over the sleeve, from the point of the elbow to the tip of the longest finger. It was a strong arm, thick through the wrist, and the hand at the end of it was square and red from the lye, with an old white scar across the back. He read the rule, and took it off, and put it away, and did not write anything down.

"You've the wrong end of your rule," said Domitia.

He had.

"It's the same length either end," he said.

"Then it doesn't matter, does it." She took her arm back. "Anything else, Engineer? Or can I finish my letter."

"She says her room's on the court side. Over the door, I suppose, to hear it like that."

"Over the door?" said Domitia. "The court side's the back of the house. Up the stair and along the passage, as far from the door as you can get and still be in it. You'd hear that bar in the cellars."

"The bar," he said.

"The bar," she said, "is oak. It was meant to be iron. Some abbess a hundred years ago ordered an iron one from the smith on the Canal Steps and died before it was paid for, and they never got round to it. It drops into iron keepers, one on each leaf and one each side in the stone, and it takes two sisters to lift it, or Sister Agrippina, who is from the hills. It's lifted from the inside." She picked up her pen. "Of course it is. What use is a bar that lifts from both sides?"

He had built one. It was on the door at the end of the shed, with a lifter through a slot in the leaf, and no lock.

"None," he said.

She dipped her pen.

"Good night, Engineer," said Domitia, and began to write again. "Mind you're not out after the bell."

He went down the hall. At the door he turned once. She was bent over the page in the light of the one lamp, her coif as white as a sail; and the pen was not moving. Her eyes were on him.

He went out.

---

He did not sleep on the slip that night.

He went into the house first. The bread she had left had gone green, and he took it out to the hard and broke it for the gulls. Then he shut the house door on the latch, and lit the lamp in the shed, on the bench under the empty loft. He opened the book at the plate and brushed Ennia's slate-dust out of its gutter with his finger. He set his rule beside it, and a clean planed board, and a stub of chalk. He took one of his own jack-screws down off its pegs and stood it on the bench by the lamp, where he could see the thread. Then he began to draw.

He drew the jack first.

Not Anaxis's jack. Anaxis's would do for a gate in a war, with men to spare and time to set it, and a frame like a stool that two men could carry. His would have to go in under shields, in a street, and be set and turned in the time it took the street to notice. He drew the screw. He drew the frame round it, and threw the frame away and drew it again, lower; and then again, with a shoe to take the bottom rail of the leaf and a bed to spread the weight on stone. The lamp burned down. Outside the shed door the harbor was black. He trimmed the lamp, and sharpened the chalk on the edge of the bench.

Then he drew the door. He had never seen it. He had nothing of it but what a sister had told him across a table; but he had her arm, and the rule's length of it, and the fifth rung of a ladder, and four fingers of oak. He drew it ledged and not braced, two leaves, with the straps across them and the pintles in the jamb, and the bar behind them that he would not have to touch. He drew it in a corner of the board, small, so he would know what he was lifting.

---

# Chapter Three: A Vow Is a House

**VALERIA**

They were singing it at the Abbess's gate when she came down the canal walk, and they were singing it badly, which was how she knew they meant it.

There were a score of them, and more coming. Bargewomen with their sleeves rolled to the shoulder. A lamplighter with his pole. Two boys from the tanneries, who stank of it, and a girl with a baby on her hip. An old soldier with one leg and a crutch under the stump held the tune for the rest of them, in a voice like a gate on a dry hinge. They stood on the stones of the Lower Canal walk in the steam, with the afternoon heat coming down on them off a white wall, and sang up at a row of shuttered windows. The shutters stayed shut. They sang anyway.

*The Angel of the Baths.* Valeria knew it as the cohort sang it at Cyrene, loud, off the barracks benches. In the last verse, just before the last chorus, Tappo's voice used to go up over the rest, high and alone: the verse about the dark. Since Bastion the cohort had left that verse out. Here the old soldier took it, a beat early and a note flat, and the bargewomen followed him in. All of it went up the white wall to the shutters, as if nobody in the city had been told Tappo was dead.

She stood at the back with the Farrier on her shoulder until they had done. The heat came up through the soles of her boots. In winter the Lower Canal's steam was a kindness; in midsummer it was a second sun, and the sulphur in it sat at the back of the throat like a bad coin. Her mail was hot through the padding. Sweat ran down the groove of her back under the arming coat and stayed in the band of her breeches.

When it was over the old soldier wiped his face on his sleeve and saw her, and saw the Farrier, and drew himself up on the crutch as if somebody had called the roll.

"Captain."

She did not know him. That did not mean he did not know her. Half the men who had come home from Hollowmere on the carts knew her face from the causeway, and the other half had been told it.

"Soldier."

"Have they let you in to her?"

"Not yet."

"They'll not. Not unless you've something wrong with you." He spat neatly into the canal. "The sick go in to her, afternoons, and come out mended. The rest of us get the sister at the gate. We come every day." He pointed with his chin at the shutters. "She sleeps up there. Third window. Sister says she's well. Says it every day, the same. You'd think they'd vary it."

The house of the Vigil of the Returning Sun stood where the Lower Canal bent toward the sea: a long white wall running back from the water, with no window in it low enough for a man on a horse to reach. Its great door was on the street face, round the corner, two leaves of black oak under a porch with a stone sun over it. She had looked the door over the day she landed, on her way down the hill, for how it would be held and what it would cost to take. She had put it down as the kind you went round. On the canal side the wall had one way in. That was the poor gate: an arch as deep as a short passage, with a bench along either side for the people who waited, and an iron grille at its far end. A sister in white sat on a stool behind the grille. Beyond her lay a small paved court with a well in it, and the sun on the stones.

Across the walk from the arch, at the edge of the water, an old man stood beside a litter.

She had seen him on her first day too, and not known what he was. A small man, bent, white-haired, in a coat of good black wool, old-fashioned and cut long, made for someone broader in the chest than he was now. His four bearers, in grey livery, sat on the canal steps below him in the shade of the litter's canopy with their poles laid down beside them, and threw stones into the steam. The old man did not sit. The litter had a cushioned seat and curtains, and he stood beside it with one hand on its roof, as she had seen men stand by a horse they did not trust, and watched the arch and the square of sunlit court at the end of it.

Below the walk behind him, where the canal widened toward its mouth, lay a strip of grey sand, wet along its lower edge. A few stakes had been driven in at the top, with a rope slung between them. There was nothing on it.

"Who's that?" she said to the old soldier.

He followed her eyes, and lowered his voice, which nobody on the Lower Canal ever did. "That's her father. One of the old houses on the Old Hill, him. Mela. Comes every afternoon at the third bell and stands there till the sixth, and goes home. Sends a basket in." He shook his head. "Sister at the gate says she's well."

"To him as well?"

"To him as well." He settled the crutch under the stump. "Same words. I've heard her."

---

She had come north with the Bastion returns: the dead by name, and the wounded, and the stores spent and lost, and the plain account of what had been done at the gate and why. The council had sent her south with a writ in the first place, under orders to report everything, and she carried this report herself.

So she had stood on the long quay at Cyrene on the morning the *Patience* sailed, with the returns in an oilcloth bag under her arm. The Vigil's new sister had come down the quay on her round and stopped her in front of her own Lanciarii. Sister Domitia tapped the Farrier's haft twice with one finger. There was a check in the ash, she said, a finger below the socket, and it would split at the first hard blow.

Valeria turned the spear to the light, and there was the check: a hair-line running with the grain, that she had not seen in nineteen years of looking at that haft every morning.

"Thank you, Sister."

"Get it bound," said Domitia, and went on along the quay to see whether the watch on the mole had its hats on.

She had got it bound. It was the first thing she did in the capital, before the returns, before anything. She climbed the Canal Steps to the smith with the green door, who was fat and bald and sweated like a cheese. She stood over him in the coal-smoke while he whipped the haft with soft iron wire, tight and close, and hammered the whipping down into the ash until it lay flush and would not catch a hand. He did it well. He talked the whole time.

"Ash," he said. "Good ash. Old. Somebody's thumb on it, here. I don't touch the thumb-place. You'll want the thumb-place." He bit off the wire. "I had a Vigil sister in here last month wanting a hinge-pin made. I said to her, I'll make your pin, Sister, when your house pays my grandfather's grandfather for the bar."

"What bar?"

"For their great door. It's in the back there still, under the sacks, and their door's still got its oak one." He laughed. "We've kept the bill. It's on the wall."

It was on the wall: a scrap of paper gone as brown as a dead oak leaf, in a glass case on a nail over the forge. The sum at its foot was in figures nobody had used since before the old king.

"They should pay it," said Valeria, and put down a silver bit for the wire, which was too much.

"They should," said the smith, and kept the bit.

Then she took the returns up the hill.

The council's clerks received them in a long hot room off the Hall of Kings, with the shutters closed against the sun and the air tasting of wax and ink. A clerk with a wet lip untied the oilcloth and counted the sheets, and wrote the count in a book, and had her sign that she had delivered so many. She signed. The sheets went into a press with a brass lock. The council, said the clerk with the wet lip, would sit when the council sat, and the captain would hold herself at its disposal.

She had held herself at the council's disposal through one whole Midwinter. She went down the hill to see about her billet.

On the third morning a steward of the palace came down to the Lanciarii's billet under the hill. He was an old man carrying a lamp he did not need in daylight, and she knew him by sight from the foot of the king's stair. He gave her a slip of the king's plain paper, squared at the corners and folded once. There was one line on it, in a small, close, old man's hand, and no name, and no seal.

*The captain will ask after the healer, and tell me.*

It was not an order, and it did not say she was to go in. She read it all the same as she read orders, for the dead places and the ways out. There were none.

She put it inside the neck of her mail, where the Marshal's letter used to go.

---

The sister at the grille that afternoon, when the song was done, was a big raw-boned woman from the hills, with forearms like a ploughman's and a coif starched so hard it creaked when she turned her head. Her eyes went to the Farrier, and to the king's slip, and to Valeria's face, and took their time over each.

"The king asks," she said.

"The king asks."

"He can ask." She folded the slip along its own fold and handed it back through the bars. "Leave the spear."

"I'll leave it in the arch."

"You'll leave it with me."

Three people waited on the benches in the arch: a man holding a boy with a cut head against his chest, and a girl of ten or so with nothing to show what was wrong with her at all. She sat with her hands in her lap and her bare feet not reaching the ground. All three of them watched the Farrier. Valeria passed it through the gap at the end of the grille, haft first. The big sister took it in one hand like a broom and stood it against the wall behind her stool. Then she lifted a bar out of two iron keepers on the inside of the grille, and swung the grille back a foot.

"In. Stand where I put you. Don't touch anything. If she tells you to go, you'll go."

"Who?"

The big sister gave her the look a sergeant gives a recruit who has asked which is his left foot.

"The healer," she said.

The court beyond the grille was small and hot and smelled of lye, and of the well, which was a warm one like every well under the Old Hill and breathed its own small breath of sulphur. A roofed walk ran down one side, with a long table under the roof, a basin, a shelf of jars, and a brazier with a kettle on it in spite of the heat. At the table, with her back to the court and her sleeves tied above the elbow with a strip of linen, a woman in plain white was bending over a bargeman's foot.

Valeria knew her by the hair before anything. It was under a white cloth, but the cloth had been tied by someone with no practice at it, and the weight of the hair had dragged it half off. Down her back from under it came a thick rope of honey-gold, plaited for work and tied off with a bit of string.

Two more sisters stood under the roof, one at either end of the walk, in the shade, with their hands folded in front of them. They were not doing anything. They had the look of men set to guard a wagon of something they had been told was valuable and not told what.

The big sister put her by the well and went back to her stool.

Liora finished the foot. She did it with her hands, both laid flat on the instep, and then with a linen strip, wound and tied, which was for the bargeman's peace of mind and not for the foot. She said something to him low, and he laughed, and got up off the bench and stood on the foot and stamped it twice and laughed again, and went out through the grille carrying his boot. Then she turned to the basin to wash her hands, and saw Valeria by the well.

The corner of her mouth went in.

"Captain."

"Healer."

"Is it Roderic?"

"He rides." Valeria had not known she would say that first. "Every morning. Up the crown's road to the fort and back. He says the horse wants it."

"Which horse?"

"Thistle."

"Thistle wants nothing in this world but apples." Liora dried her hands. She was thinner. Not much. The cloth had slipped, and under it the bones of her face lay nearer the surface than they had on the quay at Cyrene the evening she sailed, and there was a new line between her brows. Her eyes went past Valeria to the grille. "Send the next, Sister Agrippina."

The big sister sent the man with the boy. Liora sat the boy on the bench and took his head between her two hands, and he stopped crying in the middle of a breath and stared up at her.

"Who sent you?" she said to Valeria, without turning round.

"The king."

"I see. And what does the king want?"

"To know how you do."

"Tell him I do very well. I have a room on the court side with a window that opens." She bent to the cut. "Hold still, sweetheart. This won't be anything." Her eyes closed. The boy's mouth came open. Valeria had seen it done a hundred times now, at Cyrene, in the cutting at Silverwood, at Bastion in the rain with Roderic under those hands, and still she could not see what happened. She saw only that something did, and that the woman's face went inward while it happened, like a door that has someone standing in it. When Liora took her hands away the cut was a red line, and then less. "There. Tell your father to wash that, and to stop staring at me, and to give you something to eat."

The man took the boy and went out backward.

"I'll tell the king," said Valeria. "About the window."

"Tell him I eat." Liora turned back to the basin and poured water over her hands from the jug, and it ran off them pink, and then clear. "That's what they'll all want to know. Gaius will want to know whether the window is square, and the rest of them will want to know whether I eat. Write that I'm eating."

"Are you?"

"At the bells." Liora dried her hands again, finger by finger. "There's a bell for everything in this house. It's very restful. You never have to decide anything." Now she met Valeria's eyes, straight. "Write that I'm eating, Captain."

"I'll write what I see."

"Yes," said Liora. "You will." She turned to the grille. "Send the girl, Sister."

The barefoot girl came in and stood in front of her, and Liora crouched to her height and asked her name. Valeria went back out through the grille and took the Farrier from behind the big sister's stool, and Sister Agrippina let her.

Across the walk the old man stood by his litter, and the bearers threw stones into the steam.

---

The next day the poor gate was shut.

There was a sister at the grille all the same, a different one: small and freckled, with lashes so fair they hardly showed, and frightened. The benches in the arch were empty. The healer, said the small sister, was in reflection. By the Abbess's order her afternoons at the gate were ended, and two sisters would be with her day and night, all for the healer's protection, because of the crowds. While it lasted she could have nothing from outside. The sick of the Lower Canal might come in the mornings, as they always had, to the sisters of the infirmary.

"What crowds?"

The small sister looked past her to the walk: nine or ten people on the stones in the heat, and the old soldier on his crutch, getting his breath for the song.

"Those," she said.

"Those are her patients."

"I'm only to say," said the small sister, "that she's well."

Valeria went away, and came back the next afternoon, and the one after. The king's slip said *ask*, and she had asked and been answered, and a clerk would have written the answer down and gone home. She stood across the walk instead, every afternoon, with the Farrier grounded beside her. She watched the house as she had watched Bastion's gate from the brow of the Survey road the day before the fight: the hours, the doors, who went in and who came out and what they carried.

She learned the house. It rang its hours on a small high bell in the court, counting from dawn and again from noon, and the sisters moved to it as files move to a drum. She came down once at first light to see the mornings: the infirmary sisters came out to the canal steps at the first bell to take in the sick, and went in again at the third. At the sixth bell of the afternoon a cart came down the walk from the Old Hill with bread and greens and a barrel that slopped, and the big sister unloaded it alone. At the dusk bell the great door round the corner shut. She heard it from the walk: the long scrape, and then the bar going down into its keepers, a deep wooden knock that carried along the water. After that nobody went in or out until the morning bell.

And there was the third window. After the dusk bell a lamp was lit behind it, and two shadows showed in its slats, one at either side. They did not move about. On the third evening she saw them changed for two others, who took the same places. It was not a room's window, then, but a passage's, and they were standing either side of a door.

She learned who came. The old soldier, every day. The bargewomen, most days. A priest from the harbor shrine, once, who went in at the great door and came out inside the hour with his face red. Pilgrims off the ships in twos and threes, who had heard the song in some other harbor and come to see the house the Angel was kept in. They stood gazing up at the white wall as if it might do something, and bought blessed candles from a boy who had started selling them the day after the gate shut, and was doing well by it. Men who had been in the hospital tents after the stair, and said so, and did not say what she had done for them. A great many women with children.

And at the third bell, every day, till the sixth, the old man with the litter.

In the evenings, when the house had barred its door, she climbed the hill and ate late with her men. The Lanciarii had been given the old horse-barracks under the hill to sleep in: a long low stable with the stalls knocked out and a hayloft over. It still smelled faintly of horse, and much more strongly of the twelve men who had come north with her. They ate mutton and onions out of the barracks pot; and on the third night of her watching, eels out of a crock, which Galba carried up from the Fishmarket Steps with a message.

"From Felix's mother," he said, setting it down. "She says is the boy eating. And to tell him his sister's had twins, and they're both of them called Felix, and she wants to know whose fault that is."

"Whose fault is it?"

"Didn't ask, Captain." Galba sat down on an upturned bucket. His beard had grown back black where the gorse had singed it, all but one grey patch on the left side like a burn on a hide. "I thought about it on the way up. I think it's his."

The eels had been stewed in their own jelly with an onion and a heap of pepper, and they were better cold: the jelly cool on the tongue, and the pepper coming after it. Her men ate them with their fingers, sitting round the crock in their shirts with the barracks door open to the dusk. The smell of the canals came up the hill. The bells rang all over the city, and somewhere down in the lower town somebody was singing *The Angel of the Baths*, every verse.

That night she lay on her cot in the loft with her arms behind her head and went down her list.

It was a short list, in the capital. *Things that might kill me:* nothing. Nothing in this city could kill her; that was the trouble with it. *Things to deal with later:* the council, which had her returns in a press and had not sat; the Marshal, who had not sent for her, and would. And the third column, the one with no heading, where the elf had stood since the street of boats, and the king beside her since Midwinter. She put the white house on the canal in it, and the two shadows at the third window. Then, last, the old man by the litter.

---

Since the gate was shut the basket had come back every afternoon. It came out through the arch at the fifth bell in the small sister's hands, with a cloth over it knotted at the corners, and she carried it across the walk and held it out, and one of the bearers got up off the steps to take it.

On the sixth afternoon of her watching the bearer got up, and the old man moved his hand, the smallest movement, and the bearer sat down again.

He took the basket himself, in both hands. It was heavy for him. The coat shifted on his shoulders as the weight came on, and Valeria saw how little there was of him inside it.

"The healer is well, my lord," said the small sister. "She is in reflection."

"Thank you, Sister."

"I'm only to say."

"I know you are." His voice was light and dry and old, and every vowel in it was long: the long vowels of the Old Hill, that Liora had kept. "Go in. You'll be in trouble for standing in the sun."

The small sister went in. The old man stood with the basket.

Then he turned, slowly, all of a piece, and faced her along the walk. In all the afternoons she had stood there he had not once turned his head to her, as he had not once sat down in the litter.

"Captain," he said.

She went along the walk to him with the Farrier on her shoulder, and grounded it in front of him.

"My lord."

"You are the prince's captain. Valeria. Of the causeway." He said it like a man reading off a list he had made himself. "You went in. The day before they shut the gate."

"I did, my lord."

"You saw her."

"I saw her."

He did not ask anything more at once. He stood with the basket in both hands and searched her face, as if the answer might be written there small, if he could only get near enough to read it. His eyes were pale and wet at the rims.

"Is she thin?"

"A little, my lord."

"She was always thin. As a child. She would not eat what she was given." His hands moved on the handle. "Does she eat?"

"At the bells."

"At the bells." He bent his head to the basket. "I know about the bells. I had my cook go to the infirmary sisters and learn what their rule allows. Bread. Greens. A fish. Water from the spring. Nothing after the dusk bell." He lifted a corner of the cloth: a round loaf, a bunch of cress, a small cold fish wrapped in a leaf, laid out as kit is laid out for an inspection. "I sent exactly that. I thought, if I send exactly what the rule allows, the rule can have nothing to say to it." He let the cloth fall. "It comes back."

"It's their rule, my lord. Nothing from outside, while she's in reflection."

"I know their rule. I asked for her at that grille the day she came back to this house, and was told she was well. I am told so every day. My house roofed their chapel: my great-grandmother, who was healed of something in there before I was born. I forget what. There's a stone over the chapel door with our name on it." He was quiet a moment. "I have never been inside."

The steam drifted past them off the canal. Behind him, on the strip of grey sand, the rope between the stakes moved a little in the heat, and nothing else moved.

"You'll be thinking," he said, "that a father might have come to his daughter's door before this summer."

"I wasn't thinking anything, my lord."

"No. You're a soldier." He looked at the arch. "She got down out of my litter on that sand, Captain, in her good dress, one evening a long time ago, and went in at that gate behind a barrow. I could have sent men after her. I did not. I thought she would come home when she was cold." His voice did not change at all. "She was never cold enough, it seems."

Valeria said nothing. There was a thing in his face now that she had seen in other faces, in men on carts and in the files coming back down the road from the gorse.

"I write to her every Midwinter," said the old man. "She answers. She tells me who she has buried. I am told she says their names. All of them. Every night."

"Yes, my lord."

"Is there a man of mine in it?"

She did not understand him, and then she did, or near enough.

"I don't know, my lord. I've never heard her say a name I knew for yours."

He nodded.

"You'll go in again?"

"If they let me."

"If they let you." He turned the basket a little in his hands. "Then if you see her, Captain, you might tell her the cress was from home. The old bed under the chapel wall. She used to pick it there when she was small enough to get behind the bench." He stopped. "No. Don't tell her that. Tell her nothing from me. She'll know I'm here. She always knew where I was. It was the one thing she could never stop doing."

He gave the basket to the bearer then, and got into the litter at last, slowly, a hand on either side of the door, and drew the curtain. The bearers took up the poles. The litter went away up the walk toward the Old Hill in the steam, rocking a little, and the singers stood aside for it without stopping the song.

Valeria watched it out of sight, with the Farrier grounded beside her and her hand off it. The thumb of her right hand hunted along the side of her forefinger for the worn place on the haft, and came back empty.

---

The next day the council sat at last, and did not read the returns aloud. It noted their receipt. It thanked the captain. It held the matter of the gate at Bastion over to its next sitting, at the Marshal's request, so that the Marshal might study the returns. The Marshal sat at the head of the long table from the first bell to the noon one, and his eyes were everywhere but her end of it. When the clerk read out the number of sheets, he pressed the bull's head of his ring into the heel of his hand.

She took the king's slip down to the canal afterward, and held it up to the grille for the small sister to read, and asked to see the Knight-Abbess.

They kept her an hour on the poor's bench in the arch, in the heat. Out on the walk the old soldier sang *The Angel of the Baths* twice through while she waited, with the high verse, flat. Then the big sister came across the court and lifted the bar out of the grille.

"She'll see you. Leave the spear."

Valeria left it.

The big sister took her across the court and through a door, and up a stair that turned twice in the thickness of the wall. In the passage at its head the shutters were closed, and the light came through their slats in bars across the boards. At the end of the passage was a long room. A sister stood at either side of its door, and their eyes came to Valeria and left her.

The order's chapter-room, she supposed. It ran the width of the house, its windows open on the court side for the air and shuttered on the canal side against the crowd, and it was whitewashed from the floor to the beams. On the east wall a great sun had been painted in gold, with its rays going out to the corners, and some of the gold had flaked. Nothing else hung there. The floor was scrubbed boards, with benches round three walls. A sister sat writing at a table under the sun; and in front of the table, in the middle of the floor, on a three-legged stool, Liora sat with her hands in her lap.

Cassandra was standing.

She stood a pace in front of the table, between it and the stool, in a white cloak over her mail, clasped at the throat with steel in all that heat, and a steel cap on her white coif. She was younger than Valeria by some years, and she held the floor as if she had it by deed. She had a ledger open in her two hands. Her spear leaned on the table behind her, a short spear with a small bell at the socket. Her long plain face was red with the heat, under two straight dark brows.

She saw Valeria, and closed the ledger on one finger.

"State your competence," she said, "or stand aside."

"Valeria, captain of the Lanciarii. The king's officer." Valeria held out the slip. "His Majesty asks after the healer."

Cassandra did not take it. She read it where it was, in Valeria's hand, from a yard off; and her teeth clicked once.

"His Majesty may ask," she said. "His Majesty has no competence in this house, and knows it, or he would have sent a herald and not a captain. But he may ask." She turned her head a little. "Sister Philippa. Enter that the king's officer sat through this day's hearing at the king's asking, and saw that it was done by the rule."

The sister at the table wrote. She was a lean grey woman with ink on her second finger, and she did not raise her head.

"Sit," said Cassandra. "There." The end of the bench by the door. "You will not speak. You will not go to her. If you do either, you will be put out, king or no king. Do you understand me?"

"I understand you, Abbess."

Valeria sat. Her right hand reached down to her side for the Farrier and met the bench. She put both hands on her knees.

Two old sisters sat on the bench across the room, upright, with their hands folded: one with a face as brown and creased as a walnut, the other with no teeth. Witnesses. A bee had come in at an open window and was working along the whitewash under the beams, hunting for the way out again, with a dry small buzz. The room smelled of lye and hot wood, and under them, faintly, of the canal.

Liora had not turned round. She sat on the stool with her back straight, in the plain white of the house, the cloth on her hair tied better today; somebody else had tied it. Her feet were bare on the boards. From the bench by the door Valeria could see her face only in part, from the side, and it was quiet.

"From the first," said Cassandra.

---

"From the first," Liora said. "On the ninth day at Cyrene they brought Statius to me in the steam-box house, for his back."

Her voice was low and even. It was the voice in which Valeria had heard her tell Hostus how much of a thing to put in a cup.

Valeria knew the name. The day before that, on the slip, she had had the Farrier's haft across the man's throat and her knee in the back of his, because his shovel came up. It had only been his back.

"Who brought him?" said Cassandra.

"Rufo. Statius came in his irons, and Rufo stood by the door."

"And you treated it."

"I laid my hands on it. I did not warm them first. I had never done that before, to anyone on my table."

The pen moved across the paper. The bee worked along the beam.

"Did you go in?"

"No. I didn't need to. A back like that you can read with your hands." Liora's hands lay still in her lap. "Knots in the long muscles either side of the spine, below the last rib. They drew up tighter with every load, until he had to straighten and let them go. If a weight came on him turned, before he'd let them go, they would seize, and pinch the great cord down the back of the leg, and the leg would go from under him."

"Did you tell him so?"

"I told him true. *Straighten before it comes. With a load on you, turn your feet, never your back.*"

"And the warmth?"

"I kept it."

"Say what that means. For the record."

"I put nothing in. He said *Yes, healer* to the floor, and Rufo took him back to the others."

"On the thirteenth day," said Cassandra.

"On the thirteenth day we loaded the *Forward* at the water-gate stair, in the last of the light. The prisoners carried the stores. He had the slingers' lead on his left shoulder, in a keg, and every third trip he straightened at the top. I was on the landing, where the stair turns back against the rock. The ship's master stopped the line to trim her. Statius was at the bottom when it stopped: the last step, at the lip, where there's no rail. Deep water on his left. The lead on him, and past his time. I counted three, and called his name."

"You called his name."

"*Statius.* In the voice for the farthest pallet. He turned to see who it was, his head and his shoulders, under the load, with his feet where they were." A pause, no longer than a breath. "I had told him never to."

"And the leg went."

"The leg went. The lead took him over the lip, on the weed, and his chain took him down."

"Knowing his back would go."

"Knowing."

"Knowing the water was deep."

"Yes."

Old Baebius had come up to Valeria at dusk that evening, to tell her that a prisoner had gone off the foot of the stair in his irons and that everyone had been very quick. Her report had been written and sealed by then. She had studied Baebius's face, and then the seal, and left it where it was.

Cassandra opened the ledger on her finger, read something in it, and closed it again.

"Pansa," she said.

"On the eleventh day Rufo brought him in with two others, and he held his hands out to me across the bench. They were split with the frost. He said, *Sister. They tell me you've the touch.*"

"And you gave it him."

"I gave him goose-grease. Not my hands."

Sister Philippa wrote. On the far bench the toothless sister moved her lips over her gums, and was still.

"On the fourteenth day, at noon," said Liora, "I made up liniment for the prisoners' hurts, for when I was gone. Camphor in lard. In his I ground wolfsbane."

"How much?"

Liora told her how much. She told it in the old weights of the still-room, the grains and the scruples; and Valeria, who did not know a scruple from a sack of meal, saw the toothless sister shut her eyes.

"Far past the measure," said Liora. "Enough to take his hands away from him for the hours of a working day. Not enough to kill him by itself. I meant it not to kill him by itself. I scratched his name on the lid, and gave the pots out at the slips that afternoon. He rubbed his in, and said his hands had gone quiet: best they'd been since the snow. Then he went up the ladder to the staging with a strake on his shoulder, and his hand closed on a rung it couldn't feel."

"And he fell."

"Backward. A man's height and a half, onto the frost. It broke his neck."

"Where did the wolfsbane come from?"

This time Liora was slow to answer.

"From my bag," she said. "A twist of paper. Every sister of this house carries it dried, for the pain in a nerve, and is taught the measure to the grain."

The pen stopped. Then it went on.

"The order's," said Cassandra.

"The order's."

"Go on."

"Atilius," said Liora. "The sergeant. On the fifteenth day, at noon, on the black beach. They were burning Statius and Pansa, and the prisoners stood to windward, and he was across the fire from me."

"How far?"

"Ten paces of fire."

"Go on."

"I said the names, as I say them. His two at the end. He knew, then. I saw it come into his face."

"And you did the Seeing."

"Yes."

"At ten paces. Through the fire. On a man you were not touching."

"Yes."

"With your eyes shut?"

"Open. On his."

"Who taught you that?"

"Nobody. I didn't know it went that far."

"Who taught you the Seeing?"

Liora turned her head then, for the first time, a little, toward the woman standing over her.

"You did," she said.

Cassandra's face did not move. But the hand on the closed ledger went white across the back, as a hand does on a haft when the front rank of the enemy comes into its last few strides.

"Yes," said Cassandra. "I did. You had the blessing wrong for a week. You had the Seeing the first night. Go on. Say what you did."

"I went into him," said Liora. "There's a door low on the left side of the heart. His didn't close true. The strings that hold its edge had worn thin, on one side."

"The heartstrings," said Cassandra, to the table; and the pen took the word down.

"I took the thin one and its neighbor and tied them. A surgeon's knot. Two throws, then one."

"Your hands."

"Cold. I did not warm them."

Nobody on the benches moved. The bee had got out at the open window, or settled; the buzzing had stopped. Down in the court the little high bell rang the hour, three strokes, clear, and boots crossed the stones below toward the gate.

"You went in cold," said Cassandra.

"I went in cold."

"The warmth is the first thing we teach," said Cassandra. "Before the Seeing. Before the oil and the bell. A girl of ours learns to warm her hands before she learns where to lay them, so that she will never go into a living body cold, to do it harm. Because once you have done it you will know how, and you can never again not know." She stood over the stool. "I taught you that too."

"Yes. You taught me that too."

"And then?"

"He went down. Rufo called me. I went round the fire and knelt by him, and I could have undone it. One pull on the right end, and the first throw comes free."

"Did you?"

"I drew it tighter."

"And then?" said Cassandra.

"And then I was sorry," said Liora. "I laid my hand on his chest, warm, and took the pain out of him into mine. I did not loosen the knot. He died before the fire was out."

Sister Philippa wrote it, and waited with the pen lifted.

Cassandra's teeth were set; Valeria could see the line of the jaw under the edge of the coif. She stared down at the woman on the stool, and Valeria saw her draw the breath for the next question.

She did not ask it.

She let the breath go, and opened the ledger, and turned a leaf.

"You were asked to take the vow," she said. "In the tents, after the stair. Again the next summer. And at Midwinter, by me, in this room."

"Yes."

"Three times."

"Three times."

"You refused it."

"Yes."

"Why?"

It went across the room like a stone across ice, and the toothless sister on the far bench opened her eyes.

Liora raised her head to the Abbess.

"Because a vow is a house too," she said. "And I'd just left one."

For a moment nobody spoke. The heat stood in the room like water in a cistern.

"And now?" said Cassandra.

"Now I'm in yours."

Cassandra's teeth clicked. Once.

"Tomorrow," she said. "From the first."

---

The two sisters from the door came in for Liora and took her between them, one at either elbow, not holding her, a half-step behind. Liora left with them without a glance at the bench. Valeria waited until the Abbess had gone through the far door with the ledger under her arm and Sister Philippa had sanded her page. Then she got up and followed them out into the barred light of the passage.

They had stopped halfway down the passage, at a door across from the shutter she had watched from the walk. One of the two had her hand on the latch. Liora turned round.

"Captain."

The two sisters exchanged a glance.

"A word," said Liora. "She can stand there. You can stand here. Nobody needs to touch anybody."

The sister with her hand on the latch hesitated, then took it away and stepped back to the wall, and the other did the same on her side. They stood with their hands folded three paces off and studied the shutters.

Valeria came down the passage and stopped where she was put.

"Every day?" she said. "From the first?"

"Every day. Since the day after you came to the gate. That was the seventh time. Sister Philippa has it word for word, seven times over. If anybody ever wants it, they'll be able to see I never once said it differently." The corner of her mouth went in. "I've always been good at saying things the same way every night."

"She didn't ask you."

"She's never going to." Liora looked down the passage toward the long white room. "If she asks, I'll have to answer, or refuse to; and either way it'll be about them. She knows that. She was never a fool." She turned back. "This is about me."

"It's a trial."

"It's a reflection." She said it in the house's voice, flat; and then, in her own: "It's lawful, Valeria. That's what you were let in to see. Every bell of it is in the rule. I walked in at the door. Nobody has laid a hand on me." She drew a breath. "I'm not going to fight my way out of it. I told Marcus. I'll tell you."

"I've not asked you to."

"No. But you'll have looked at every window on the way up that stair."

Valeria had. She had not known it until she was told.

"Don't do anything," said Liora. "Don't do anything, Valeria. Any of you. If one of those girls on the door gets a split lip because of me, or a broken wrist, or a sister's knocked down a stair, I'll have to heal it, and then I'll have to say it at night." She stood straight between the two white walls. "I've enough to say at night."

Valeria held her eyes. Then she took in the door with the latch, and the two sisters along the wall, and the bars of light lying down the passage on the boards, and the head of the stair.

"I'm the crown's officer," she said. "There's nothing the crown can do in here."

"Good," said Liora.

She went in. Past her shoulder Valeria saw a whitewashed wall, and a window standing open on the court with the light in it. Then the sister nearest the latch came off the wall and shut the door behind her, and did not bar it, because there was no bar on that side. It was a room. It had a window that opened.

Valeria found her own way down the stair and across the court, and took the Farrier from behind Sister Agrippina's stool, and the big sister lifted the bar out of the grille and let her out without a word.

The steam rose off the canal, up the white wall and over it into the sky.

She stood on the walk until the sixth bell, as the old man did. Then she climbed the hill to the barracks and sat on her cot in the loft with her mail off and her shirt stuck to her back, and did not eat, and thought.

*There's nothing the crown can do in here.* The crown's officer had sworn to report everything, and she would. She knew what her report would do when it reached Cyrene. The Prefect would read *thinner*, and the engineer would read *door*, and somebody in that yard would be measuring something before the ink was dry. Whatever came up the Lower Canal after that, at night, the crown's officer could not be standing on the walk when it came. If she were, she would have to write what she saw.

Below her in the barracks her men were at their supper, and Galba was telling the one about the goat in the harbor shrine, and somebody laughed in the wrong place.

What she could do was be somewhere else. It was most of a soldier's life, being where she was put.

In the morning she put on her mail, and her good cloak over it, the blue one, though it was too hot for any cloak. She combed out her plaits and did them again, tight, with the iron in them showing. At the first bell she was on the Lower Canal with the Farrier on her shoulder and four of her men behind her. She stood across the walk from the Abbess's gate, where the whole city could see her, all day, in the sun.

---

She did it for seven days.

She spoke to nobody. She kept away from the grille. She held the walk across from the poor gate with the Farrier grounded, from the first bell to the dusk bell. Two of the Lanciarii stood at her back in the morning and two in the afternoon, in mail. By the third day the rings had galled both her shoulders raw under the padding, as they did every summer, and she let them.

The city looked. The old soldier came on the first morning and stood beside her, and on the second brought a friend with two legs and a drum. The bargewomen brought her water in a jug. The boy with the blessed candles moved his tray along the walk to stand by her, because the pilgrims came where she was now as well as where the house was. His candles stank of hot tallow in the sun. By the fourth day the lower town had a song about her, short and bad, about the prince's captain at the Angel's gate, which rhymed *spear* with *here* twice. By the fifth it had it that she stood there by the prince's own command, to watch over the Angel till the prince came for her; by the sixth, that the king had sent her. On the seventh a woman selling cherries told her in a whisper, as a kindness, that she was the Angel's sister.

She stood. Along the walk from her the old man stood by his litter, from the third bell to the sixth. They did not speak again. On the third day, when the basket came back, he turned to her with it in his arms, and she inclined her head to him, an inch, and he inclined his.

At the dusk bell on the seventh day a boy in the Marshal's livery came along the walk and gave her a folded note, and stood waiting.

*The Lord Marshal will sup with the captain tomorrow, at the first lamp.*

"Tell him yes," said Valeria.

---

The Marshal's house was the same tall narrow house of grey stone under the palace wall where the lane turned, but it was summer, and the door stood open to the lane for the air. There was no pine bough over it. The stone bull's head over the chimney-piece had no garland on its horns, and the hearth under it was swept and laid with a bunch of green bay in a crock. All the windows were open. From the garden of the tombs across the lane came the warm dusty smell of box hedge, and the sound of somebody's sheep, which the king let graze among the tombs in summer to keep the grass down.

Ursus was at the door with his sleeves rolled up and his red beard spread on his chest. He looked over the Farrier, and the new wire on it, and her.

"Bound," he said.

"Bound."

"Good wire." He stood aside.

On the peg inside the door hung a white cloak of good wool, with a steel clasp.

She knew it now. She had sat across a room from it for most of an afternoon. It hung where it had hung at Midwinter, on the same peg, its clasp turned out. Somewhere above, on the stair or in a room over the hall, a small high bell rang once, short, as the bell at the socket of a spear rings when it is set down against a wall. Then a door shut, and there was no other sound.

The Marshal was at his table by the open window. It had been carried across from the hearth for the air, and the evening light lay over it, yellow and slanting. His coat hung on the back of his chair with its ribbons in their rows, lit down one side. He was in his shirt, which she had never seen: broader without the coat, and older, and the grey cropped hair wet at his temples.

"Captain," he said. "Sit. Eat."

There was a cold ham on the table, a whole one, with the fat on and a crust of browned crumbs over the fat. There was a dish of the little green pickled things that grow on the salt-marshes, a round loaf, a bowl of plums with the bloom still on them, the jug, and the cups. And a paper.

The paper lay at the Marshal's left hand, folded once and then once again, long and narrow, to go into a sleeve. The folds had been pressed as sharp as a blade, so that it would have stood on its edge by itself. As she sat down he put his hand flat on it without looking, as a man in a crowd puts his hand on his purse.

She had caught the writing before he covered it, a line or two on the outside fold: the most beautiful hand she had ever seen on a paper. It was too good for a clerk's, every letter as clean as a cut, the long strokes going down below the line and turning up at their feet. And it was too careful for a lord's. She did not know it. She read *the order's rule* upside down, and *the Lower Canal*, and then his hand was on it, and she had no more.

"You've been on the Lower Canal," said the Marshal.

"I have, my lord."

"Seven days."

"Since the morning after I sat in their chapter-room. By the king's leave. Asking after the healer."

"You've not asked anything for seven days. You've stood there." He cut the ham himself, two thick slices with the fat on, and put them on her plate. "In mail. With that. Under the house's windows, where the whole lower town can see the prince's captain at the Abbess's gate. They're singing about you."

"Badly, my lord."

"I've heard it." He poured the wine. "From my own vines."

"On Vessa," said Valeria.

He met her eyes over the jug, and set it down, and sat back with his hands on the arms of his chair, and turned his face to the window and the palace wall going gold in the last of the sun.

"I've read your returns," he said.

"Did you read them aloud, my lord?"

"To myself." He pressed the bull's head of his ring into the heel of his hand, and took it away, and watched the little white bull come up there and fill in red. "He wouldn't buy the gate."

"No, my lord."

"Two hundred, against the war. On a quarry's books." He was quiet. "Would you have gone through?"

"Yes, my lord," said Valeria. "That morning."

"And now?"

"Now I've written it down."

The Marshal turned his head and gave her a long look, and she ate her ham. It was good ham, boiled in hay before it was baked, as they did it in the hills behind Vessa; she could taste the hay, and the bay leaves. The fat was sweet, and the salt-marsh pickles were sharp enough to make her eyes water. She ate it all.

"The Abbess," said the Marshal at last, "is within her rule."

"She is, my lord."

"You'll have seen that she is. The order's claim on its training and its hands is older than this council, and older than the dome on that hill. The crown does not go inside a holy house. It never has. Not the old king, and not his father, and not this one." He took a plum and turned it in his thick fingers. "His Majesty knows it. His Majesty sent a captain to ask, and the captain asked, and was answered. That's all the crown's business there is."

"Yes, my lord."

"And a woman who killed three men in cold blood," said the Marshal, to the plum, "under no vow, and no order, and no law but her own, is a matter for somebody." He put the plum back in the bowl. "If it isn't the order's, it's somebody's."

Valeria put down her knife.

"How do you know there were three, my lord?"

He raised his eyes.

"I can read, Captain," he said. "It's in the songs. Half the canals have it. *Weed, frost, heart.*" His hand found the folded paper again. "Everybody knows."

She had stood seven days on the Lower Canal and heard every song the lower town had, the good and the bad and the one about her. She knew where those three words lived. They were the cohort's: said low on the *Forward*'s deck on the way west, and under the breath of the whole Fourteenth ever since, and never louder. She had said them herself, once, in the elves' wood, with her palm on a man's back.

Nobody had ever sung them.

She put that where she put such things, in the column with no heading, and picked up her knife.

"I couldn't say, my lord. I don't sing."

"No." The Marshal almost smiled. "No, you don't."

He picked the paper up then and put it inside his shirt, folded as it was, against his chest. She watched it go. Then he leaned forward with his forearms on the table among the plums.

"I want you off the Lower Canal," he said.

"My lord."

"I don't want the prince's captain under the Abbess's windows with a lancea and a song. It looks like something. It looks like the crown at a holy house's door, and the crown isn't, and I'll not have the city thinking it is. Next it'll be a crowd at the great door at night, with torches, and the prince's captain in the middle of it whether she likes it or no." He set the flat of his hand on the table. "The guard on the palace wall wants relieving. The Ninth's men who keep it nights are going home to Vessa at the new moon, for the hay. Your company can have it."

"The palace wall, my lord."

"The Old Hill. The wall from the harbor tower to the tombs, and the lane, and the garden. All night, from the dusk bell to the morning one. From the new moon until I say." He sat back. "Where I can see you from my window."

"By whose order, my lord?"

"Mine."

"I'll want it written, my lord."

The Marshal considered her. Then he laughed, short, through his nose. She had never heard him do it.

"You'll want it written," he said. "You'll want it in a report."

"I write everything, my lord."

"So you do. So you always did." He reached behind him to a shelf in the window for a sheet of his own paper, a pen and an inkhorn, and wrote it himself there at the table, in a big square soldier's hand: four lines. He did not sand it. He folded it once, roughly. A servant brought a candle, and the Marshal warmed a stick of red wax at it and dripped it on the fold. Then he took the ring off his finger and pressed the bull's head down into the wax, hard, and gave it her across the table.

She put it in the neck of her mail, beside the king's slip.

Then she picked up the wine, which she had held level and set down full at Midwinter, and drank it.

The Marshal watched her do it. She set the cup down empty.

"It's good wine, my lord."

"It's the south face," said the Marshal. "Above the camp." He turned his face back to the window and the wall. "Go on, Captain. Go and tell your men they've got the hill."

---

She wrote the report that night at the open hay-door of the loft, while the long midsummer evening went out of the sky. Up the hill, under the palace wall, one window was lit, and she thought it was his.

It went on one side of one sheet, plain, with nothing on it she would not have stood behind at the council table while Titus, the Crown Prince, read it aloud and asked which word was untrue. The folded paper did not go in. She had read a few words of it upside down, and she could not have stood behind them. When she had done she read it through once, for the dead places and the ways out. There were none.

*To the Prefect of Cyrene, at Cyrene. From the captain of the Lanciarii, at the capital.*

*The returns are with the council. It has noted them. Nobody has read them aloud.*

*The healer is in the order's house on the Lower Canal. She is well. She is thinner. She eats at the bells. She has a room on the court side, with a window that opens. At the dusk bell the house shuts its door, as it always has, and two sisters stand at her door day and night, for her protection. There are no chains. It is the order's rule.*

*She worked at the poor gate in the afternoons until I first went in. The next day the gate afternoons were stopped.*

*She is questioned every day by the Knight-Abbess, in the chapter-room, before two witnesses, with a secretary writing. I sat through one day of it, at His Majesty's asking. She is made to give the details of how the three died, from the first, every day. She gives them. She is not asked why, and she does not say.*

*Her father comes to the poor gate every afternoon and stands there three hours. He is refused. He sends food in, what the rule allows. It comes back.*

*The Knight-Abbess's cloak hangs in the Lord Marshal's house.*

*The Lord Marshal knows there were three. He says it is in the songs. It is not in the songs.*

*The healer asked me not to do anything.*

*From the night of the new moon I am on duty on the Old Hill, all night, with the Lanciarii, by the Lord Marshal's own order.*

---

# Chapter Four: The Third Volume

**GAIUS**

The capital came up out of the sea on the afternoon of the fourth day: a smudge first, then the Old Hill, then the steam.

Gaius saw it from under the stern deck of the *Patience*, where he had sat most of the passage with his back to the rudder-trunk and the jack across his knees. The jack was in pieces, rolled in sailcloth and lashed with marline, a roll the length of a man, and he had let nobody else carry it since Cyrene. Twice a day he had unlashed it and laid the pieces out on the planking: the screw and its nut, the frame in its four parts, the bed, the shoe, the two bars. He had greased the thread with tallow from the cook's tub until it shone, and wiped it, and greased it again. A screw that is to be turned under a roof of shields in the dark has to turn slow and without a sound, and grit in a thread can be heard a street away.

He set the roll down and climbed to the stern deck.

The *Patience* was short of oars. Avitus, her master, had said so from the helm at Cyrene, to nobody in particular, with a rag to his nose, and he had said nothing else since. He kept to his helmsman's elbow with a cup of something hot. When he had to tell one of the new hands something, he told the oar. They were pulling well. Some of them had pulled an oar before, in chains, and the *Patience*'s own free rowers had stopped laughing at them by the first noon.

Gaius stood at the rail and looked at the city.

He had seen it before, as a young man with a bridge in his head, and never as he saw it now: a building somebody else had put up, that he had to take down without breaking. He had taken down one thing in his life. It had been a bridge of his own, and he had done it with fire and a sand-glass.

The Old Hill, terraced with tombs. The long palace wall along its shoulder, and the dome of the Hall of Kings. The canals running down from the springs under the hill, each with its grey breath standing on it in the heat, so that the city lay ruled in smoke from the hill to the harbor. And where the lowest of them bent toward the sea, before it ran out between its stone banks, a long white wall.

He could not see the door from here. It was on the street face, round the corner, under a porch with a stone sun over it. He knew it better than any door in the world, and he had never seen it.

"That's her," said Plancus, at the stroke oar below him.

He did not look round. He was a thick, slow, brown man who had poled barges on the Lower Canal for twenty years before he poled the wrong one, and he could smell the tide turn from a cellar. "The white one. Where she bends." He pulled. "Flood's making. High water about the dusk bell, and then she ebbs all night. You'll have the last of it before light." He pulled again. "Good night for it."

"Why?"

"No moon," said Plancus. He pulled. "And it'll rain."

Gaius put his head back. It was a hard, hot, white sky, with the sun in it like a coin in milk, and it had not rained in a month.

"It'll rain," said Plancus.

---

It had begun four days before, at Cyrene, on the forty-third day after the homecoming, with Marcus coming down the yard at dusk with a sheet of paper in his hand.

Gaius had been on the staging of the second hull, fairing a frame. He had seen the *Patience* come in on the morning flood, and had not gone down to see if there was anything for him, because for a fortnight there had not been. Later he had seen the Prefect go down to the quay himself, and climb back up it slowly, reading.

At dusk Marcus walked into the shed, to the bench at the far end where Gaius's lamp was. Gaius finished the frame, because a frame half faired is worse than one not started, and climbed down the ladder bad leg first, and followed him in.

The shed smelled of oak shavings and pitch and the day's heat going out of the roof. Marcus had lit the lamp. He stood by the bench with the paper in his hand and his eyes on the drawing pinned over it: a jack, and in a corner of the board, small, a door.

"Valeria," said Marcus, and held out the paper.

It was one sheet, written on one side, in a hand like a row of fence-posts. Gaius read it standing by the lamp, as he would have read a survey, for where the weight went. At the end his eye climbed back up the sheet to a line in the middle and stayed there. Then he put the sheet down on the bench, flat, and laid his rule across it so that it would not curl.

*She is thinner. She eats at the bells.*

"The Lord Marshal knows there were three," said Marcus. "That's the line I keep reading. That, and the last one."

Gaius looked at the last one.

*From the night of the new moon I am on duty on the Old Hill, all night, with the Lanciarii, by the Lord Marshal's own order.*

"When's the new moon?" he said.

"Plancus says four days. The Navarch says four and a half." Marcus kept his eyes on the drawing. "Avitus sails on the morning ebb."

"I know."

"I can't send anyone."

"I didn't ask to be sent."

"No." The lamp burned. Out in the yard somebody was singing on the sea-wall, badly, and stopped. "I'm the heir," said Marcus. "The heir doesn't storm a holy house. Not that one, with the city watching it every afternoon and the Marshal watching me." He turned his head and looked at Gaius for the first time. The old scar along his jaw had gone tight.
