"If I sent one man of this cohort up that canal, it would be the crown at the Vigil's door. They'd have her for the rest of her life, and they'd be right."

"I know."

"So I'm not sending anyone."

"No."

Marcus looked back at the drawing.

"Avitus was in this afternoon," he said. "He's got a cold."

"He's had it since spring."

"He says he's short of oars." Marcus laid one hand flat on the bench, beside the report, beside the rule; and took it away again. "I told him I'd see what the yard could spare. Rowers. For the passage." He crossed to the shed door, and stopped in it, with the dark yard behind him and the harbor black past the slip. "Send me word," he said. "Whatever it is. When it's done."

"There'll be nothing to send."

"Then send me that." And he went.

Gaius stood by the bench. Then he took the screw down from the shelf where it had lain since the smith cast it, waiting for a nut. He wrapped it in sacking, and walked out along the sea-wall to the forge, and woke the smith.

---

He had meant to ask.

He had lain awake on the shavings by the keel, with the bronze for the nut cooling in the smith's trough, and tried to put the words in an order that was not an order, and could not. He would rather go up the Lower Canal alone than with a man he had asked.

In the grey before dawn he carried the roll down the yard on his shoulder. Rufo was sitting on the bollard at the head of the slip, with his shield in its sailcloth across his knees and his hazel tally-stick in his hand.

He looked at Gaius, and at the roll, and got up.

"You're late," said Rufo.

They were all along the slip behind him in the half-dark. Ear, a long, loose, freckled lad of nineteen, with the ear a turnkey had once held to a brazier for a joke, and his shield on his back. Sennius behind Ear, very straight, in a clean shirt. Rusticus and Salvius, who had been caulking the old liburna for a week and arguing about it the whole time, not arguing. Fingers on an upturned bucket, with a leather roll of his own across his knees. The cousins, Naevius and Lollius, side by side, as they always stood. Vatinius with his eyes shut. Plancus, and Strabo, and Scaeva. Behind them stood sawyers from the slip-yard, and two of the carpenters who had built the healer's house, and men of the Silverwood cutting whose names Gaius did not know. He had seen every one of them at one time or another on a pallet at the Baths, with her hands on him. Every man of them had a shield in sailcloth.

Rufo turned the tally-stick over in his fingers. He had been notching it; the new cuts were white in the hazel. He did not say the number. He put the stick away inside his shirt, and his hand rose for a moment to the place over his ear where the fair lock was plaited.

"We heard the *Patience* was short of oars," said Rufo.

Gaius looked along the slip at them in the grey and could think of nothing to say about it.

"Swords stay at home."

A stir ran along the slip. Not much of one.

"The job's a door," said Gaius. "It comes off. Nobody goes through anybody. If anybody in that house bleeds, she won't come out, and she'll be right not to." He shifted the roll on his shoulder. "Shields. Nothing else."

Rufo looked at him a moment. Then he unbuckled his belt, with the short sword on it, and hung it on the bollard he had been sitting on, and stood back.

One after another, without hurry, they hung their belts on the bollard and the next one and the stanchions of the staging, until the head of the slip looked like a cutler's stall on a market morning. Ear had to unwind his twice before it came off.

The last was Sennius, who had never carried a sword in Aeridor's service. He had a knife in his belt, a short plain one, for bread. He looked at it. Then he drew it and laid it on the bollard on top of Rufo's belt, and took his hand off it quick, as if it had come out of a fire.

"Bread's on the ship," said Rufo. "Go on."

They walked down to the long quay as it grew light. The net-wives were on it already, and bakehouse girls, and the old men who had nothing to do but see who sailed; and they watched the cohort go down to the *Patience* with shields and no swords, and Gaius after it with the long roll. No one called out. At the foot of Netmakers' Row a net-wife put her hand on the shoulder of the woman beside her, and kept it there.

Sister Domitia was at the head of the *Patience*'s plank.

She stood in her white and her steel cap, with her spear in the crook of her arm, the Vigil's mail-bag at her feet and a letter in her hand. Gaius stopped in front of her because she was in the way. She ran her eyes along the roll on his shoulder, all the length of it, as she had run them along the latrine trench and Ear's dressing and the Farrier's haft.

Her finger lifted and tapped the roll twice, near the end, where the screw was.

"Keep that out of the wet," she said. "If the thread rusts it'll sing."

"It's bronze."

"Grease it again." She held up the letter, sealed, in her big square hand. "I write to the Abbess every night," she said. "This is last night's. I'll write again tonight, as I always do, and it'll go on the next *Patience*." She bent and put the letter in the bag at her feet, and left the bag where it was, and looked at him levelly with her grey eyes. "Mind the knee on that plank, Master Gaius."

He went up the plank.

When he had stowed the roll under the stern deck and climbed out again, the *Patience* was off the quay on the ebb, with the cohort at her oars and Avitus sneezing at the helm. Domitia still stood on the quay where the plank had been, in her white, with the Abbess's letter in the bag at her feet.

---

The *Patience* rowed into the royal harbor at the end of the afternoon, between the moles, with the bolt-throwers on the mole-towers asleep under their sailcloth hoods and the heat standing off the water like the mouth of a lime-kiln.

She moored at the galley quay beyond the empty berths, where she always moored. The port-reeve's man walked down with his slate, looked at the mail-bags and at Avitus's nose, and left. Nobody looks at the rowers of a mail galley. They stepped ashore by twos and threes, with their sea-bags on their shoulders and the long sailcloth rolls under their arms.

Gaius went last, with Fingers and the jack.

The city stank in summer. The steam off the canals hung in the streets and would not lift, a yellow fog that smelled of eggs and drains and fish-oil and the tanneries. Under it the lower town sat in its doorways in its shirts, and watched the roll go by on his shoulder without wondering about it. Everything had gone past those doors in a sack at one time or another.

Fingers knew the way. He had been born off the Gaol Stair, and he threaded the lower town like a man going home, by back lanes between warm walls. Once he cut through the yard of a dye-house, where a woman with arms blue to the elbow said "*Fingers*" in a voice of great surprise; and he said, "Not tonight, Ma," and went on.

The Drowned Lantern stood on the left bank of the Lower Canal, a little way up from where the canal ran out into the harbor. It had a badly painted sign of a lantern sunk in green water, and a canal window with no glass in it and a sack nailed over the lower half.

"That's where he went in," said Fingers. "The prince. Out of that window, the morning they buried his brother. He went for the General, and the General stepped out of his road, and he went straight through it into the canal." He considered the window with respect. "They never put the glass back."

"Why not?"

"Nobody's asked them to." Fingers led the way round the side of the house, down a passage no wider than his shoulders, into a yard behind it.

The cohort was there, sitting about the yard on the cobbles and the empty casks and the midden wall. The landlord, a heavy man with a wet rag over one shoulder, was going round them with a jug of small beer. He took in Gaius and the long roll, and kept pouring.

"He's been paid," said Rufo, from the casks.

"Who by?"

Rufo shrugged. "He says a man looked in an hour since with a purse, and said the yard would be wanted for the night, and some of the men might be tired after, and to give them beer. Grey man, he says. With a cold."

The landlord's boy brought out a tray of sausages split on the grill and laid in a round of bread, black at the ends and running fat, and set it on a cask. The smell of them got up through the canal's fog, pork and fennel and smoke, and turned Gaius's stomach over. He sat down on a cask with the roll at his feet and did not take one. Rufo tore one off the tray on his way past, put it in his hand, and sat down again on his own cask without a word. Gaius ate it. He did not taste it until it was half gone.

The light was going yellow. Over the yard wall the top of the Old Hill turned gold in the last of the sun. From somewhere up the canal beyond the roofs, under the noise of the lower town, a small high bell began to ring.

It rang for some time, thin and quick, and stopped. Then one heavy wooden knock carried along the water, flat and loud, the sound a beam makes when it is dropped into its seat.

It rolled along the canal and over the roofs and into the yard, and every man in the yard heard it and lifted his head.

*I had forgotten how loud the bar is.*

On the midden wall, Ear put down his beer.

---

With the dusk, lights showed on the Old Hill.

They were small lanterns, a long string of them, walking the top of the palace wall one behind another along the shoulder of the hill, from the harbor tower to the trees of the tombs. Below the wall one window was lit, square and yellow. The lanterns walked to the end of the wall at a soldier's pace, and turned, and walked back. When they reached the lit window they stopped, all together, a little longer than anywhere else, and moved on. They made the round in about a quarter of a glass, as near as Gaius could judge it without one.

"Is that the captain?" said Ear, beside him, low.

"That's the Lanciarii."

"Is she with them?"

"She's on duty," said Gaius. "All night." He watched the lanterns stop at the window. "That'll be his window."

Ear watched them go on.

"That's clever," he said.

"Yes."

"Is it hers or his?"

Gaius had been wondering that since Cyrene. He did not think it made any difference.

The rain broke an hour after dark.

It came as rain comes in the capital at the end of a dry month, all at once, like a cask stove in over the city. First there were big warm drops, hissing on the hot cobbles and the steaming water; then a roar on every roof of the lower town together. The smell of it rose, wet dust and wet stone, and the canal steamed twice as hard, until the yard behind the Drowned Lantern stood in a cloud. Plancus stepped out from under the eaves and put his face up into it with his eyes shut.

On the hill the lanterns smeared to a row of yellow blurs. Gaius could still see them stop.

---

They waited for the middle of the night, under the eaves and in the landlord's lean-to among his empty casks.

Some of the men slept sitting up, with their chins on their chests. Rusticus and Salvius argued in whispers about the old liburna's garboard, and Salvius was wrong, and Gaius let him be. Vatinius prayed. The cousins sat side by side and watched the rain.

Gaius unlashed the roll at last in the lean-to, by one tallow dip that Fingers had begged off the landlord. He laid the pieces out on the sacking and greased the thread, because Domitia had told him to. Behind the casks the talk had gone round, low, to what a holy house cost a convict.

"The quarries," said Rusticus.

"The oars," said Salvius. "Back on the bench, in the chain."

"I don't mind the oars," said Ear.

"You've never pulled one in the chain."

"I don't mind, though."

Vatinius opened his eyes. "Nor me," he said, and shut them again, and that seemed to be the end of it. Gaius kept his own eyes on the thread.

Fingers squatted down on the other side of the sacking and watched him.

"How long?" said Fingers.

"To set it? Forty breaths. To lift?" Gaius turned the screw a quarter-turn in the nut, and back, and listened to it. "Depends on the oak. A hundred breaths. More, if the pintles are dry."

"Who oils them?"

"A novice. Every spring, up a ladder, with a pot and a feather. Domitia did it four years."

"Then they'll not be dry." Fingers picked up the shoe and turned it over in his long thin hands, and ran his thumb along the lip that would take the bottom rail of the leaf. "You'll want to look at the tops of the pintles," he said. "Before you lift."

"Why?"

"Some smiths cap them. A pin through the top, above the eye, or a nut run down on a thread. So nobody can do what you're going to do." He put the shoe down. "If there's a cap, I'll have it off. Without a mark."

"There's none. She'd have said."

"She'd not have thought to. Sisters don't." Fingers wiped his hands on his breeches. "I'll look."

"Good."

Fingers returned to his bucket under the eaves.

Ear sat down where Fingers had been, and watched the screw go into the nut and come out again.

"What's she like?" said Ear. "Inside, I mean. The house."

"I've never been in."

"But you know it."

"I know the door." Gaius wiped the thread. "And the stair. And where her room is, along the passage. Domitia told me." He wiped it again. "That's all."

Ear looked at the screw.

"She's the most beautiful thing I've ever seen," he said. He said it to the screw. "I don't mean anything by it. I just think it, every time. When she did my ear I cried. It didn't hurt. I just did." He scratched the scar on his shin, which Domitia had made Hostus sew, and which itched. "I'm not going to say anything to her. Tonight."

"No."

"I just wanted to say it to somebody." He got up. "Before."

He went back out to the midden wall in the rain.

Gaius greased the thread again, and wiped it; and his hands stopped, and he started them again.

---

Toward the middle of the night the rain eased from a roar to a steady hiss. Plancus stepped in out of the wet, shook his head like a dog, and said to nobody, "Ebb's half run."

Gaius lashed the jack up in two rolls, the frame and the bed in one and the screw and the bars in the other, and stood up. Rufo was on his feet before he was.

They unwrapped the shields in the lean-to by the tallow dip. They were the cohort's own, plain oak faced with hide and rimmed in iron, with nothing painted on them; and every rim had been gone over with a file, so that no burr would catch on anything.

Rufo formed them up in the yard. He did it without a word above a murmur, in the rain, by touch as much as by sight: a hand on a man's shoulder, a push, a tug at a strap. Three ranks across the narrow yard, and two files down the sides, and the middle men with their shields ready for the roof. Gaius stood at the back with Fingers and the two rolls and watched it go up course by course, as he had watched walls go up, and could not fault a stone of it.

The front rank formed last. Rufo took the right of it, as he always had. Ear fell in on his left. Then there was a space a man wide where nobody stood. Then Sennius, at the left, square, with his hands steady on his shield.

Rufo looked at the space. Then he looked at Ear, and Ear moved his shield a little out from his body to the left, to cover it. On the other side of the space Sennius moved his a little to the right, and the two rims met across the gap and held it. There was nobody behind them in the gap. The men of the second rank had closed up on either side of it and left it, without being told.

Gaius looked at it, and then at the back of Rufo's neck, and then away.

"Engineer," said Rufo, without turning his head. "Where do you want to be?"

"Third rank. In the middle, with Fingers." He hefted the screw roll. "At the door I'll want the front rank to open, and the roof down low, and two men to turn."

"You'll have them."

"And when we're inside—"

"I know what to do when we're inside," said Rufo. "I've been inside a lot of places." He settled his shoulder to his shield. "*Ready.*"

The landlord stood in his back door with the wet rag on his shoulder and watched them file out of his yard by the passage, by ones, to form again on the walk. When the last of them had gone by he stepped inside and shut the door; and Gaius, at the back, heard the bar go down on it, softly, from inside.

---

They went up the Lower Canal walk in the rain.

The rain had cleared the lower town off its doorsteps. At the middle of the night the walk was a long wet causeway between the steaming water and the shut houses, empty but for puddles and the smell of eggs. The water hissed. Under the hiss Gaius could hear the tortoise: the soft shuffle of men walking in step on wet stone, and their breathing, and now and then the soft knock of one shield against another. There was no other sound in it. If Rufo called the step, it was too low to carry to the third rank; Gaius took it through his feet, off the men round him.

Gaius walked in the middle of the third rank with the screw roll on his shoulder and Fingers beside him with the frame. Under the roof he could see the backs of the men ahead of him on either side, and straight ahead nothing: the empty file ran from his face to the place in the front rank where nobody stood, and through it went a strip of the wet walk, and the dark. The rain drummed on the roof. The air under it was hot and close and smelled of wet hide and tallow and men.

He did not need to see. He had walked it in chalk on the *Patience*'s planking all the way north, with Plancus telling him the canal and Fingers the lanes, until he could have walked it asleep. The bend. Then the poor gate on the right, set back under its arch, and the champions' sand across the walk on the left, below it, with its stakes. Then the end of the white wall, and the corner, and the street face, and the porch.

They had passed the bend when a voice spoke from the second rank, low. Strabo, who could see oars through fog.

"*Litter.*"

The tortoise did not stop. It kept the same pace, and Gaius put his eye to the crack between two shields on his left and looked out.

It stood on the left of the walk, by the rope of the champions' sand: a long box with curtains, set down on its poles on the stones. Four bearers in grey squatted on their heels under the edge of its roof, with sacks over their heads against the rain. A lantern hung from one of the poles, hooded, so that only a little yellow leaked from the bottom of it onto the wet stone. The curtains were drawn.

One of the bearers lifted his head and watched them go by. He did not get up, or call out. He put his head back down on his knees.

Gaius took his eye from the crack.

He thought he knew whose it was. He had the story in pieces, and never from her: a champion's sand, a fishwife's son, a father who made his daughter watch; and a girl who got down out of a litter and walked away behind a paupers' barrow. He was still thinking about it at the end of the white wall. Then the tortoise turned the corner, and there was the door.

---

The street face was narrower than he had drawn it.

It was a cobbled lane, not a street, running up from the canal between the white wall of the house on the right and the blank backs of warehouses on the left. A gutter down the middle of it ran like a mill-race in the rain. The porch stood halfway up it on the right, deep, with two pillars and a roof of stone slabs. Over its arch, cut in the stone and gone soft and green with four hundred years of the canal's steam, was a sun with its rays out round it like the spokes of a wheel. Under the porch roof, on an iron bracket, a lamp burned behind horn. The house kept it lit all night for anybody sick enough to knock. It made a small dry room of the porch, with the rain falling bright across the mouth of it; and in that room stood the doors.

Gaius saw them down the empty file as the tortoise climbed the lane, and for as long as it took to see them he forgot what he had come for.

Two leaves of oak, black with age and the steam, each as wide as two of a heavy woman's forearms laid end to end, and a bit, and as high as the porch roof. Four fingers thick at the meeting edge: he could see it by the shadow down the stile. Three iron straps across each leaf, with their nail-heads standing in rows along them, clenched over on the inside, Domitia had said, every hand's length. At the outer edge of each leaf the eyes of the straps dropped over three iron pintles that stood out of the stone of the jamb, leaded in, the lead gone white and crusted with age. He had drawn this door from a sister's arm laid along a table and the fifth rung of a ladder, and he had not got one thing wrong.

"Engineer," said Rufo.

"Yes." He shook himself. "The right-hand leaf. Front rank, open. Fingers."

The tortoise climbed into the porch and filled it, and the rear files turned their shields to the lane. The front rank stepped apart, Rufo to the right and Sennius to the left. The top pintle stood higher than Rufo could have reached with his arm up. Ear bent and made a step of his hands without being told. Fingers climbed him to his shoulders and stood there, one hand on the jamb and his face to the top pintle, close enough to kiss it; and the tip of his tongue showed between his teeth. He touched the top of the pintle with one long finger, where it stood up through the eye of the strap. Then he dropped down off Ear, and did the middle one, and crouched, and did the bottom.

"No caps," said Fingers, low. "Nothing. Never had." He took his hand away. "Somebody trusted the bar."

"Oiled?"

Fingers put his finger to his nose.

"Last spring," he said. "Somebody with a feather."

"Roof," said Gaius.

The shields of the second rank lowered over the front rank's heads, and the roof sank low over the place where the right-hand leaf met the jamb, so low that the men holding it had to crouch. Under it, in a cave of oak and hide, with the horn lamp's light leaking in yellow at the gaps, Gaius knelt in front of the door. He knelt on the bad knee, because there was no time to put the good one down first.

He unrolled the screw.

He set the bed first, a flat slab of oak shod with iron underneath, on the step hard against the foot of the leaf at its hinge end. He leaned on it with the heel of his hand to feel whether it rocked. It did not. He stood the frame on it, in its four parts, and Fingers dropped the pins in without a sound. He put the screw into the nut in the head of the frame and ran it up by hand until the lip of the shoe came up under the bottom rail of the leaf, and touched. There he stopped, and felt along the underside of the rail for the place where the shoe would bite on sound oak and not on rot. The oak was as hard as iron, and dry. He moved the shoe a little toward the jamb, under the bottom strap, so that the lift would go up through the iron and not through the wood. Then he shipped a bar through the eye in the head of the screw.

"Two men," he said.

The cousins came, Naevius and Lollius. They crawled in under the low roof and knelt one each side of the screw, and took the ends of the bar, and looked at him.

"Slow," said Gaius. "Slower than you think. As if it was asleep and you didn't want to wake it. Together." He laid his palm flat on the face of the leaf, low down, to feel it move. "Now."

They turned.

The screw went up into the shoe and stopped. They turned. Nothing. They turned, and through his palm on the black oak he felt the whole leaf take the weight: a long slow strain going up through it, through the ledges and the straps, like a breath drawn in. The thread made no sound at all. They turned. Somewhere in the leaf a joint ticked, once, as a house ticks at night. They turned.

Fingers lay on the porch floor with his cheek on the stone and his hand on the bottom pintle, where it entered the eye. "Moving," he said, before there was anything to see.

Then Gaius saw it. Under the eye of the bottom strap, where the pintle had been hidden in it since the door was hung, a thin dark line of oiled iron showed, no thicker than a shaving. Then it was the width of a finger. The cousins turned, and turned, their breath going in and out together, and they looked at nothing but the screw. Out in the lane the rain hissed on the cobbles.

The leaf came up the whole depth of its pintles and a little more, as Anaxis said it would in the third volume. At the top of its lift it did a thing Anaxis had not drawn, and Gaius had.

The bar came up with it.

He heard it go, inside: a long dry creak of oak in iron. The great bar lay across the backs of both leaves in its iron keepers, one on each leaf and one each side in the stone. The keeper on the back of the right-hand leaf rose with the leaf, and the end of the bar rose in it, up out of the keeper in the stone of the jamb. It hung there, held up at one end by the door it was meant to hold shut.

*What use is a bar that lifts from both sides?*

"Hold," said Gaius.

The cousins held.

"Front rank," he said. "Take the leaf. By the straps. When I say, it'll come out at the hinge side, at the top. Let it come. Walk it out. Don't let it fall." He wiped his face. "Rufo."

"Here."

"When it comes out, the bar comes down. Inside. On the floor." He saw it once more: the oak bar, longer than a man and as heavy as one, falling chest-high to stone in the middle of the night, in a house full of sleeping women. He had thought about it for four days. There was no way round it that did not break something else. "It'll be loud."

"How loud?"

"She said she'd forgotten."

Rufo looked at him under the roof, and his teeth showed in the dark.

"Then she'll know who it is," said Rufo.

"*Now.*"

The roof went up. The front rank took the leaf by the eyes of its straps, where they stood clear of the pintles, Rufo high and Ear low, and drew it gently outward. The great black leaf, free at the hinge, tilted out of the jamb at the top like a tooth coming out of a jaw, slow and all of a piece. The men took its weight as men take a mast off the sheers, with their knees, and grunted, and did not drop it. As the leaf swung out and away at the hinge side, turning on its meeting edge against its fellow, the end of the bar slid off the tilting keeper, and there was nothing under it.

It fell.

The sound went through the house. One great flat wooden crash on stone, inside, so loud that the men under the roof flinched beneath their shields; a second, smaller, as it bounced; then the long shudder of it settling. After it the house was so still that Gaius could hear the rain again.

Then, somewhere above them inside the house, a bell began to ring, hard and fast, as a bell rings when somebody has run to it.

"Walk it," said Gaius.

They walked it, the front rank and two from the second, with the leaf held upright between them, out of the jamb and round, step by step, its bottom rail grinding a little on the porch floor. They leaned it against the side wall of the porch, carefully, and stood back from it.

Nothing was broken. Gaius looked, because he had to. The leaf stood against the porch wall with its straps and its nail-heads and its eyes whole, and the pintles stood out of the jamb whole, oiled and shining, with no leaf on them.

Beside him Fingers already had the pins out, and the jack apart and lashed in its two rolls. He put the screw roll into Gaius's arms. Gaius stood up off the bad knee. His hands were shaking.

"In," said Rufo. "*Shields.*"

They filed in through the one leaf's width by twos, over the bar, and closed up again in the hall.

---

The hall was long and high and dark, and smelled of lye and beeswax and old incense and, under all of them, the warm sulphur of the canal coming up through the floor. A lamp burned in a niche at the far end, by the foot of a stair, and a little more light reached in behind them from the porch. On the scrubbed stone just inside the door, across the hall at a slant where it had fallen, lay the bar.

It was a great dark beam of oak, square and smooth, black at the middle where hands had lifted it night and morning for longer than anybody living. It was not broken. At the far end of it stood a big woman in a white shift, with her hair under a nightcap and her feet bare, looking down at it as though a calf had got into the house.

A low door off the hall stood open behind her on a dark cell. She lifted her head. She did not scream, or run, or ring anything. She bent and got her two great hands under the end of the bar, alone, to lift it back into its keepers.

Then he knew her. *Two sisters to lift it, or Sister Agrippina, who is from the hills.* She was as tall as Rufo, nearly, and big in the bone, with wrists as thick as a sawyer's. She had the end of the bar up off the floor before she saw what she would be barring: one leaf, and the rain.

She let it down, and straightened.

The bell was still going overhead. There were other sounds now from above: doors, feet on boards, women's voices calling one to another, quick and low, in the dark. A lamp passed at the head of the stair. Agrippina looked at the tortoise, and at the stair at the far end of her hall. Then she strode down the hall ahead of them, and turned, and planted herself in the middle of it with her bare feet set apart on the stone.

The front rank reached her and stopped.

"This is the Vigil's house," said Agrippina, in a voice like a farm gate. "You're in a holy house. *Out.*"

Rufo looked at her over the rim of his shield.

"Sorry, Sister," he said. "*Push.*"

The front rank leaned in.

They did not hit her. They put their shields to her, flat, Rufo's and Ear's and Sennius's, and across the place where Tappo was not, the two rims; and they leaned their weight into them, and walked. Agrippina set her feet and put her hands flat on the shields and pushed back with all of her great body, and the tortoise stopped dead in the middle of the hall. Gaius heard Ear grunt, and Rufo laugh, once. Then the second rank put their shoulders to the backs of the first, and the third to the second, and the whole tortoise leaned and *pushed*. Sister Agrippina's bare feet slid back over her own scrubbed floor, caught, and slid again, all the way down her hall, with her hands still on the shields and her face dark with fury. At the foot of the stair she sat down on the bottom step quite suddenly, because there was nowhere else to go.

The tortoise opened round her and closed again on the stair above her. Ear, going by, put down a hand to help her up, from habit; and she slapped it away.

"Sorry, Sister," said Ear.

They went up the stair.

---

It was a wide stone stair, worn into hollows at the middle of each tread by all the sisters the house had ever had, and it climbed in two flights with a turn. The tortoise took it badly, the roof tilting, men stumbling in the hollows, the shields knocking; and at the turn the light came down to meet them.

The sisters were at the head of the second flight, a dozen of them. White shifts; white cloaks thrown on over the shifts, or nothing over them; steel caps on some, hair loose on others. Every one of them had a short spear levelled, its head catching the light of the lamps the sisters behind them held up, and at every spear's socket, under the head, hung a little bronze bell. The bells were all ringing at once, small and dry and furious, as the spears shook. The sisters filled the head of the stair from wall to wall, and they were not going to move.

"*Halt,*" said Rufo, and the tortoise halted on the stair, three steps down.

Gaius had seen these women on the moor at Ghoul's End, in a ring round a chapel door, burning the dead as they came and not giving a step. Some of them he knew by sight. They held their spears as women hold spears who have used them, low and steady, with the butts tucked under their arms.

Beyond them the passage ran on, shutters on one side and doors on the other. Halfway along it a lamp hung on a hook between two sisters who stood either side of a door; and past them the passage widened into a bay, and the bay was dark.

"Let us by, sisters," said Rufo.

No one answered.

"We've no steel," said Rufo. "None. Look." He did not lower his shield. "We've come for the healer. We'll take nothing else and break nothing else, and if anybody in this house bleeds tonight it'll not be from us." He waited. "Let us by."

A young sister in the front of them, with a round face and her coif crooked, said in a high shaking voice, "You broke the door."

"We took it off," said Gaius, from the third rank, before he could stop himself. "It's on the porch, leaning on the wall. It'll go back on in a quarter of an hour, with two men and a bar." He stopped. "I'll put it back myself, if you want. It's a good door."

"*Push,*" said Rufo.

The tortoise went up the last steps into the spears.

They came in at once, as Gaius had known they would: every point at the front rank's shields together, with every bell on them going. He heard them hit. A short spear driven by a strong woman into an oak board faced with hide makes a sound like an adze going into green timber. The shields rocked back, and the front rank leaned into them and took them, and the points bit through the hide into the oak and stuck there, quivering; and the front rank pushed. The sisters pushed back on their shafts. For a breath the tortoise and the spears stood locked at the head of the stair.

Then a spear got in through the gap.

It slid in low from the left, where the rims of Ear's shield and Sennius's met over the empty place in the front rank and had opened a little under the strain. It nosed in under the roof, down the empty file where nobody stood, looking for something, and came to Gaius. He saw it come, a long bright leaf of steel with its little bell ringing on it, a span from his belly; and it stopped.

Sennius had it.

He had let go of his shield with one hand and taken the spear by the shaft behind the head, in his fist. The sister at the other end heaved on it, and he held it, half turned in his place; and Gaius saw the side of his face close by, in the lamplight that leaked in under the roof, white and fixed and young. Then Sennius's hand opened on the shaft, quick, as if it had come out of a fire, and closed again lower down. He drew the spear out of the round-faced sister's hands, gently, and turned it, and held it out to her butt first, with the bell ringing at his end.

"Your spear, Sister," said Sennius, in his Lessa voice. "Mind the point."

She took it.

The tortoise pushed.

It gained the head of the stair, and the spears pulled out of the shields one after another as the sisters gave back along the passage, bells ringing, feet sliding on the boards. The tortoise walked them down their own passage, slow, like a plough going down a furrow. It walked them past the lamp on its hook, and past the two sisters at the door, who stood with their backs to the wall and did not move. Gaius passed that door in the middle of the third rank, an arm's length from it, and could not stop. The tortoise walked the sisters as far as the bay, where they could go round it, and did.

Gaius looked at every one of them as they filed round, and at the boards behind them in the light of the lamps. One sister held her wrist where it had been jarred, and swore in words he would not have thought a sister had, and one had lost her cap. Nobody had bled.

Then the tortoise stopped, because there was somebody in front of it.

---

The Knight-Abbess stood in the middle of the passage in her shift.

It was a plain white shift of coarse linen, and she had nothing over it. She had put the steel cap on over her coif. She had come out of a door beyond the bay, which stood open behind her on a dark room. She stood between it and the tortoise, tall and thin and still, planted as he had seen her plant herself in roads. She had no spear, and her hands were bare. A sister behind her held up a lamp, and its light fell over her shoulder onto the long plain face, creased from sleep, and the two straight dark brows under the rim of the cap, level as a lintel.

She took in the tortoise: the front rank, Rufo and Ear and the gap and Sennius; the roof; the faces she could see under it; and the shields, with the white wounds of the spear-points in their hide. Then her eyes went past the front rank to the third, and found Gaius, and stopped.

He met them.

"The one who reads walls," said Cassandra.

The sisters had stopped too, behind the shields and beside them, with their spears up; and the bells had stopped. The house was quiet. He could hear somebody breathing hard, and far down at the foot of the stair Agrippina saying something to somebody in her farm-gate voice. Cassandra did not look at her sisters. She looked at nobody but him. He waited for what a Knight-Abbess says to a man who has taken her door off in the night. He waited for it to be something that could be written down, and read out in a hall.

Her teeth clicked, once.

"Sisters," said Cassandra.

All along the passage the spear-heads sank, slowly, one after another, until they pointed at the boards. The Knight-Abbess turned her back on the tortoise, without hurry, and walked back between her sisters in her shift and her steel cap to the open door of her room; and went in, and shut it.

He heard the latch drop. Nothing else. No bar.

Rufo breathed out.

"Engineer," he said. "Your door."

---

Rufo turned the tortoise about. It came apart in the passage and made itself again facing the stair, with the sisters standing back against the walls to let it, until its front rank stood a pace short of the lamp on its hook: Rufo, and Ear, and the space, and Sennius.

The two sisters at the door had not moved.

They stood one either side of the door with their backs to the wall and their spears grounded, and the lamp burned on its hook between them, with a moth going round it. The one on the left was old and grey, and watched the tortoise like weather coming. The one on the right was small, with a freckled face and lashes so pale they hardly showed. She held her spear upright in both hands by the shaft, not levelled, and she was crying without any sound.

Gaius came up the empty file and out through the place in the front rank where nobody stood.

He had given the screw roll back to Fingers; he did not remember doing it. He was standing in the passage in front of the door, between the two sisters, with the lamp over his head; and his right hand was up to knock, and had stopped there.

It was a plain door. Pine, not oak; ledged and braced, the braces rising from the hinge side, as they should; hung on two strap hinges, small ones, that would not have held a gate. A latch with a thumb-lifter. No lock, no bar, no keeper for one. Anybody could have opened it from either side.

He looked at the latch, and could not put his thumb on it.

The door opened.

She was standing just inside it in the dark room, with a candle in one hand that she had not lit, as though she had picked it up and forgotten what it was for. She was dressed, in the plain white of the house: a long shift and an over-gown of coarse undyed wool, belted, with her bag over her shoulder. Her hair was under a cloth, tied badly, with a long honey-coloured end of it coming out at the side. She was thinner. He saw that first, because Valeria had written it, and then because it was true. Her face had come in under the bones, and between her brows there was a short straight line that had not been there on the quay at Cyrene, as clean as a line snapped in chalk. She was looking at him.

Behind him the whole tortoise stood in the passage and did not breathe.

"I heard the bar," said Liora.

"It's not broken."

"No. It wouldn't be." She looked past him, over his shoulder, at the men in the passage with their shields: at Rufo, and Ear, and Sennius, and at the place in the front rank between Ear and Sennius where nobody stood. Her eyes stayed there. Then they came back to him. "Is anybody hurt?"

"Nobody."

"Anybody, Gaius. Theirs or ours."

"Nobody," said Gaius. "A wrist jarred. A sister sat down at the foot of the stair. She'll have a bruise on her pride and nowhere else." He swallowed. "Nobody's bled. Nobody's going to."

She looked at him in the doorway, in the lamplight, as she looked at a dressing somebody else had tied.

Then she put the candle down on the floor inside the door, unlit, carefully, and straightened; and put her hand into the bag on her shoulder, and took out something red. It was a cap: a soldier's cap of felted wool dyed with madder, crushed and stained, and faded at the crown where the sun had got at it. There was a sound behind him from the tortoise that was not a word.

She did not put it on. She held it.

Then she came out of the room.

She did not hurry. She stepped over the sill into the passage and stopped in front of the small freckled sister, who still held her spear upright in both hands and was still crying. She put her free hand on the sister's arm.

"Thank you, Helvia," she said. "For the lamp. And the bread." The corner of her mouth went in. "And for tying the cloth better than I do. I'll not need it now."

The sister gaped at her.

"Go and see to Agrippina," said Liora. "She'll have sat down hard. She'll say she hasn't." She took her hand away. "Go on."

Sister Helvia went off down the passage toward the stair with her spear; and Liora turned and looked at the tortoise.

"Well," she said. "Make room."

They made room. The front rank stepped apart, and the second, and the roof lifted. She walked into the middle of the tortoise as if she were walking into a house, with Tappo's cap in her hand, and stood in the second rank at Rufo's shoulder; and the shields closed over her head.

Gaius went back to his place, and took the screw roll from Fingers.

"Out," said Rufo. "Slow. *Step.*"

---

They went down the stair, and through the hall, past the bar.

Agrippina was sitting on the bottom step with Sister Helvia beside her, her nightcap in her lap and her great bare feet on the stone. She looked up as the tortoise passed her, and looked into the gaps between the shields, and saw who was inside it. She opened her mouth. Then she shut it. Liora, going by in the middle of the shields, did not stop; but she put her hand out through the gap between Rufo's shield and the one behind it, and touched Agrippina's shoulder once, and took the hand back.

Then they were in the porch, with the right-hand leaf against the wall and the pintles shining in the horn lamp's light and the rain falling across the mouth of it; and they went out through it into the lane.

The tortoise turned at the foot of the lane, at the corner of the white wall, and took the canal walk back toward the harbor and the galley quay and the *Patience*. It passed the poor gate; and the litter was there, where it had been.

The curtains were open. The bearers were on their feet, with the sacks off their heads, standing by the poles. The lantern had been unhooded. It hung from its pole and lit the rain all round it, and the wet stones of the walk beneath; and in the middle of its light, out of the litter, in the rain, stood an old man.

He was small, smaller than Gaius had thought a lord would be. He was bent at the shoulders, and his hair was white and wet and stuck to his skull. He wore a long coat of good black wool, old-fashioned, cut for a broader man than he was now, so that it hung off the points of his shoulders in empty folds. The rain ran off the shoulders of it, and he did not seem to feel it. He had been standing there, Gaius thought, since the bar fell. He held something in both arms against his chest, wrapped in oilcloth, as a man holds a child.

He was looking into the tortoise, into the gaps between the shields, as it drew along the walk toward him; and Gaius, at the crack in the third rank, saw his face in the lantern light. It was an old face, long and fine-boned, with the skin fallen in, and pale wet eyes. He was afraid. Not of the shields. Of the shields going by.

"*Rufo,*" said Liora, inside the tortoise. "Stop."

Rufo stopped.

The tortoise stood still in the rain, in the lantern's light, a pace or two from the old man, and the canal hissed and steamed beside them.

Then the shields in front of her parted, and Liora stepped out between Rufo and Ear, and stood in the light.

The old man looked at her.

"Liora," he said.

He said it slowly, with the long vowels of the Old Hill, as if it were a word in a language he had not spoken for years and did not want to get wrong.

She stood in the rain in the white of the house, with Tappo's cap in her hand, and looked at him. Gaius could not see her face. He could see the back of her head, and the badly tied cloth, and the wet hair coming out of it at the side; and he could see her shoulders, and that they were very straight.

"Father," she said.

The rain came down on them both.

"Do you eat?" said the old man.

"At the bells. There won't be bells now."

"No." The old man thought about that. "Then when you like."

"When I like," said Liora.

He nodded. He did not come any nearer to her, and he did not put out his hand. Then he looked past her, into the opening in the shields, at the men behind her one after another, searching, until he had Gaius. Gaius did not know who had told him. Perhaps it was the roll on his shoulder.

The old man came forward then, slowly, past his daughter, to the edge of the shields; and held out what was in his arms.

"For the engineer," he said.

Gaius came out of the third rank. He put the screw roll down on the wet stones and took the bundle. It was heavy, and square, and the oilcloth was warm from the old man's body; and he knew it by its weight, as he would have known a block of oak. He had carried two like it for ten years.

"It's the fourth," said the old man. "Of Anaxis. The house kept it." He looked at Gaius with the pale eyes. "The third went with somebody. A long time ago."

Gaius held the book, and laid his other hand flat over the oilcloth, where the rain was getting at it, and kept it there.

The old man looked at that. Then he nodded, as if something had been measured and found the right length, and stepped back into his lantern's light beside his litter, and stood there with his arms empty.

Liora stood in the rain and looked at him. Then she sank down before her father on the wet stones of the canal walk, in the plain white of the house, the whole slow way down, in the reverence of the old court, with her head bowed and Tappo's cap held against her breast; and stayed there; and rose.

She turned round and walked back into the shields, and they closed over her.

"*Step,*" said Rufo, not quite steadily.

The tortoise moved on along the canal walk in the rain, toward the harbor. Gaius walked in his place in the third rank with the screw roll on one shoulder and the fourth volume of Anaxis held against his chest under his shirt, out of the wet. He put his eye to the crack between the shields once, at the bend. The lantern was still there behind them on the walk, and a small black shape standing in its light, not moving. Then the bend took it.

---

The *Patience* left the royal harbor on the last of the ebb, a little before the light, with the cohort at her oars.

Avitus stood at the helm with his cup and his cold and watched them come aboard. He sneezed. Then he said to the helmsman, "Short of oars," and the *Patience* went out between the moles.

The rain stopped as they cleared the mole-towers.

Gaius looked back once from the stern, before the city was gone. The Old Hill stood up black against a sky going grey. Along the palace wall the lanterns of the Lanciarii were still walking in their line, from the harbor tower to the trees of the tombs, and the window below the wall was still lit. As he watched, the lanterns came to the window and stopped there, all of them.

Then one of them, at the end of the line, lifted, high, as far as an arm would reach, and held there; and came down.

He did not know if it was meant for anything, and he thought about it until the hill was gone.

---

The sun came up out of the sea to larboard, red, with the *Patience* well out and the capital low behind her and the steam going pink over it, and the sea long and quiet and the colour of a pigeon's breast. The men pulled, and did not sing. None of them had slept, and none of them seemed to want to.

Gaius sat under the stern deck with his back against the rudder-trunk, where he had sat the whole way north.

She was beside him on a coil of rope. She had taken the cloth off her hair as soon as they cleared the harbor, and wrung it out over the side, and let it go into the sea, and watched it go. Her hair was coming down out of its knot, wet and darkened, over the shoulder of the white wool. She had Tappo's cap in her lap. She had said nothing since the canal walk, and neither had he. Once he would have had something to say by now, about a lap or a load or a lacing, and she would have had something back for it, and Fingers would have chalked it up. Nothing came.

The fourth volume lay across his knees in its oilcloth. He had not unwrapped it. He did not want to unwrap it on a wet ship, and he did not want to put it down.

When the sun was clear of the sea she put out her hand and laid it on his knee.

The bad one. Flat, through the wool of his breeches, as she had laid it on the quay at Cyrene the evening the cutter took her. He looked down at it, and waited for the warmth to come into it, and the red-gold into the rims of her fingers, and for himself to lift it off and give it back to her, and say what he had said then.

There was no warmth in it. It was only a hand, cold from the rain: the knuckles a little swollen, a scrape across the back, a thin white mark at the base of the thumb where the Vigil's lye had got into a crack. It rested on his knee as a hand rests on the rail of a boat, because it is there.

He let it stay.

Fingers came aft along the gangway between the benches then, with a heel of the *Patience*'s bread in each hand, as he had every morning, and stopped at the edge of the stern deck to hand them in. He studied the hand on the knee for some time, with the tip of his tongue between his teeth, as he read a lock.

Then he put the bread down on the coil of rope beside her, both heels, and went back along the gangway to his oar; and did not take out his chalk.

Liora picked up one of the heels and broke it, and gave him the bigger half.

"Is it waiting?" she said. "The knee."

"It's waiting."

"Good." She ate a mouthful of the bread, and turned her head to look aft, at the long pink sea and the smudge where the city was. Her hand stayed where it was. "So am I," said Liora.

---

# Chapter Five: The King Laughs

**MARCUS**

The clerk climbed the stair at the second hour with the paper held in both hands in front of him, like a dish he had been told not to spill.

He was a young clerk, with ink on the side of his middle finger and a careful walk. Marcus knew the paper: three sheets sewn at the corner with green thread, the watch-captain of the Lower Canal's report of the night before last, written fair. The guards on the stair door stood aside for him without a word. He left the door ajar behind him, because both his hands were full, and his feet went round the first turn of the tower, and up, and were gone.

Marcus stood at the foot of the stair and waited to be sent for.

The Hall of Kings in high summer was the coolest place in the capital. The dome kept the night in it until noon. Light came down slanting from the ring of windows in the drum, full of slow dust, and lay on the floor in pale ovals that crept across it through the day like a sundial nobody read. Under the dome it smelled as it always had, of stone and old incense and lamp-smoke, without the wet fur of winter. The council was not sitting. Its long table and its benches had been carried out, and the flags at the foot of the stair were swept bare, so that the king's chair stood alone on its step above nothing. Somewhere among the pillars a woman was sweeping. The dome took the sound of her broom and gave it back from every side at once.

Gaius sat on the lowest of the steps where the biers were laid, with his bad leg out in front of him and nothing in his hands. It was as near as Marcus had ever seen him come to sitting still. He had not slept either. His eyebrows were coming back short and stiff and paler than the rest of him, so that he looked faintly surprised all the time, even now.

"He'll not see us," said Gaius.

"He'll see the paper."

"Same thing, for him." Gaius looked up the stair. "What's in it?"

"Everything. A tortoise on the canal walk in the middle of the night, a litter, a door leaned against a porch wall, the alarm bell, sisters on a stair in their shifts." The watch-captain had left it lying open on his table at the guardhouse before light, and gone out into the yard, and taken his time about it. "He wrote down that nobody bled. He wrote it twice. The second time he underlined it."

"Did he spell pintle right?"

"He called it a hinge."

Gaius grunted, and turned back to the stair.

They had come up the hill at first light, the two of them, because Marcus would not have the king hear it from the watch alone. A guard who did not know where to put his eyes had told them at the stair door that His Majesty was not receiving, and that the Prefect might wait if he wished. So he wished. He had waited at the foot of this stair a good many mornings as a boy, to be told something at the top of it, and he knew how to stand there. He had not known he still knew.

He had come north at a run, and arrived too late. That had been the idea.

---

He had sent nobody. He had told Gaius so in the shed, with Valeria's report on the bench between them, and had meant it. Gaius had gone at dawn with every man who had come down to the slip unasked; and Marcus had stood at the slip gate while the *Patience* pulled out between the moles, and done nothing.

He had done nothing for a day. It was the hardest day's work he had done since Bastion. The next day he did it again.

On the morning of the third day he walked down to the long quay where *Constancy* lay. Nerva was already aboard her, and her stores were coming down the quay on the backs of the Tharsian yard-hands, though Marcus had given no order for either.

"Two days behind them," said Nerva, when Marcus stepped aboard. His eyes went aft, to the steering oar and the man at it. "She'll make up one of the days. Not both. If you mean to stop them, you'll not."

"I don't mean to stop them."

Nerva nodded, as at a bearing he could live with, and began shouting at the yard-hands again.

Alana came down with her bag and the weather-book under her arm, and did not ask why. Felix followed with a jar of the fort's spring water on his shoulder; nobody had told him the day or the hour. Domitia was last, in her white and her steel cap, with the Vigil's mail-bag on her hip and the belled spear in her hand and a face like a closed shutter, and stopped at the foot of the plank.

"I'll go north with you, Highness," she said. "If there's room."

"There's room."

"My letters to the Abbess have sat on the quay in this since the *Patience* sailed, waiting for her to come back." She shifted the spear into the crook of her arm, and tapped the bag twice with one finger. "It'll be quicker if I carry it myself."

"It will."

"I mean to tell her everything in it," said Domitia, "in person, and nothing that isn't in it." She climbed the plank, corrected the angle at which Felix was holding the water jar, and went below. She was sick, Felix reported, before they cleared the moles, and sick the whole way north, in a bucket she had chosen herself and would let nobody else empty.

*Constancy* ran north with the summer wind on her quarter, and for two days Nerva did not sit down. Marcus spent most of the way in the bows, and drank Felix's water, and thought about what he would say to his father. He had a great many things to say, by the end. By the third morning he had thrown most of them over the side.

An hour after sunrise the lookout in the bow sang out a sail ahead; and then, a little after, that it was a galley; and then, as she lifted over the curve of the sea, that she was the *Patience*, southbound, with all her oars out.

So it was done. Marcus knew it before they were near enough to see faces. A mail galley does not come south out of the capital the morning after she makes it, with every oar out, unless she has something aboard she wants away.

Nerva brought *Constancy* round across her bows and hove to. The *Patience* came on, and lost way, and lay rolling within easy hail with the sea slapping between the hulls. Avitus was at her helm with his cup. Under her stern deck Gaius was getting to his feet; and beside him, with her hair loose and wet and a crust in her hand, sat Liora.

She was thinner. She was eating. He could see both from his own rail.

Marcus put his hands to his mouth.

"Is anybody hurt?"

Gaius's voice came back flat.

"Nobody."

"Theirs?"

"Nobody."

The men on the benches had stopped pulling and were sitting up to see, with their oars in. Rufo's head and shoulders stood up above the rest, with Ear beside him, and Fingers was up on a bench for a better view until Avitus told him to get down. None of them wore a sword. All along the benches the faces were turned toward *Constancy*, waiting; and Marcus understood that they were waiting to be told whether they had done right.

"Avitus," he called. "Bring her round."

The sea slapped between the hulls.

Then Gaius said, in the same flat voice, "She's out."

"I know."

"If we go back—"

"If you don't go back, you've run." Marcus said it loud enough for the benches. "Every man of you. The writ stops your sentences while you serve in Cyrene and its waters, and you're out of them. Walk off this ship at Cyrene after last night, and you're convicts who broke a holy house and ran home from it: deserters. The Marshal can have you back in irons by the next *Patience*, and he'll be right. Go back, and you're convicts who broke a holy house and stood still to answer for it. That's a different thing."

"Is it?" said Gaius.

"I'm betting your necks that it is."

Gaius said nothing for a moment. Then he turned his head, and looked down at the woman beside him.

Liora stood up. She stood with one hand on the rail and the bread still in the other, and looked across at *Constancy*, and then back over the sea to the north, where the capital was a grey blur under its own steam.

"I didn't run out of that house," said Liora. "I walked. I'm not going to run from the city."

She sat down again, and went on with the bread.

So the *Patience* came about, slowly, with her men grinning at each other along the benches. They had been up all night. Marcus had both ships stand off out of sight of the city through the heat of the day, and let them sleep on their benches. He brought them in at dusk, when the lower town would be out on its doorsteps for the cool. They rowed in under the Old Hill with the harbor bell ringing the hour and the steam going up off the canals into a sky the colour of a hot coal.

Domitia went ashore first, green, with the mail-bag, and away toward the Lower Canal without a word to anyone, at a pace that would have done for a forced march. She had not been on deck once since Cyrene, and had not laid eyes on Liora across the water.

Marcus walked the cohort up the canal walk to the Drowned Lantern in the last of the light, in plain sight of the lower town. Their shields were on their backs, and the woman they had stolen walked in the middle of them. The lower town came out of its doors to see them go by, and stood there, and did not go back in.

---

Up the stair, a long way up, somebody made a sound.

It came down round the turns of the tower and out through the door the clerk had left ajar, small, and broken by all the stone it had come through. Marcus turned his head. It was a dry sound, a cough with something wrong in it, or a man choking on a crumb. It went on too long for either, in gusts, and stopped, and began again, higher; and then it came down the stair one last time, a single long breath of it, out of somebody old, and stopped altogether.

The sweeper's broom had stopped too.

The two guards on the stair door had turned to each other. One of them was a grizzled man of the old palace guard who had stood on that door, Marcus thought, since Marcus was in short tunics. His mouth had come open, and stayed open.

"Is he ill?" said Marcus.

The grizzled guard shut his mouth. "No, Highness," he said; and then, less sure, "I don't think so, Highness."

Gaius had got up off the step. His short new eyebrows had gone up as far as they had to go, and stayed there.

"What was that?" he said.

"I don't know."

Marcus stood at the foot of his father's stair and tried to put a name to it, and could not. He thought of the pain in the hip that old men get, and of a man weeping who has not done it in years and has forgotten how it goes. He thought of the wind in a crack. None of them was it.

The clerk came down a little after, slower than he had gone up, with his hands empty, and stopped on the last step.

"His Majesty," said the clerk, and swallowed, and started again. "His Majesty will see the parties tomorrow, at the third hour. Both parties. The Lord Marshal, the Knight-Abbess, the Prefect and," he looked at his wax, "the engineer."

"Tomorrow," said Marcus.

"At the third hour, Highness." The clerk hesitated, and then said, low, and not as part of the message: "He asked me to read him the part about the spear twice. The one that was handed back."

He crossed the hall with his careful walk. The sweeper began again among the pillars. The guards looked at the wall in front of them.

Marcus stood on at the foot of the stair. Then he walked out into the heat with Gaius limping beside him, and down the hill.

---

The Drowned Lantern had not charged them for anything since they came in at dusk, and would not.

Marcus had tried, the night before, with the purse on the counter. The landlord had looked at it, and then at him, with the wet rag on his shoulder and his heavy grey face giving nothing away. Then he turned the purse round so that its strings faced Marcus, and pushed it back across the counter. "Not this week, Highness," he said, and drew small beer for Rufo.

So the cohort ate and drank at the Lantern's charge. They had slept on its floor and in its yard and the lean-to, with their shields stacked against the midden wall, and all that day the lower town came by to see them. It did not come in. It stood in the lane, and on the canal walk under the window, and looked in over the sack. Then it took itself off, and left things on the step. A basket of eggs, that morning, with straw in it. A string of onions. A cheese wrapped in dock leaves. A cooked fowl, still warm, under a cloth with no name on it, and nobody in the lane when Fingers stepped out to see who had left it. By the afternoon there was a row of things along the Lantern's wall like the offerings along the wall of a shrine, and Plancus had stopped bringing them in and begun to sort them by what would keep.

Marcus sat at the table under the canal window. His old table was across the room against the wall. He had sat there more nights than not in the years before Cyrene, with his chair tipped back and his boots on the board. Fingers had it now, and was losing at dice on it to the cousins. Marcus had not gone near it.

He had a cup of water in front of him. Felix had seen to that before the bench was warm.

The window had no glass in it still. A sack was nailed over its lower half, gone grey and stiff with the canal's steam. Above the sack was the empty frame, and through it the canal going by, green and smoking, with the light coming off it and moving on the ceiling. He had gone through that window once, shoulder first, in the snow, on the morning of his brother's funeral, and come up out of the warm canal to see Roderic in the broken frame above him, settling his cloak. He did not remember much else of that morning. He remembered the window.

When the landlord came by with the jug, Marcus put a silver piece on the table under the empty frame.

The landlord looked at it.

"That's the window," said Marcus.

"I know which window it is, Highness." The landlord considered the silver, and the frame, and almost smiled. Then he took the silver and put it in his apron, and carried the jug on past Marcus's cup.

Upstairs, Liora was asleep.

She had slept most of the day and all the night before in the Lantern's one good room, under the roof, with the door on the latch and Sennius on a stool outside it. Nobody had told him to sit there. He was sitting there when Marcus came back from the hill, with his hands on his knees and his back against the wall. When Marcus reached the head of the stair he took his hands off his knees quickly, as if they had been caught somewhere they should not be, and stood.

"She's sleeping, Highness."

"Good."

"She woke once. She asked what the time was, and when she was to eat, and if anybody was hurt." Sennius looked at the door. "I said nobody. She said she knew, she wanted to hear it again."

Marcus left him there.

She came down at dusk, in the house's white shift and the undyed over-gown she had walked out in, with her hair tied back in a strip of somebody's linen and her feet bare on the boards. The common room, which had been loud, went quiet in pieces from the stair outward, and then got loud again a little too fast. She made the round of it first, every man in it, a hand on a shoulder here and a look at a knuckle there. Ear held out his shin to show her where the scar had healed white. She bent and looked at it gravely, and told him it had healed very well, which he had known; and he was happy for an hour.

Then she sat down across the table from Marcus, under the window, with her back to the canal.

The landlord brought her a bowl of the Lantern's mutton and barley without being asked, and a round of bread, and a cup of small beer; and stood a moment with the jug, looking at her.

"You saw him," said Liora.

"I saw his stair. He'll see us tomorrow. Me, Gaius, the Marshal, your Abbess."

"Not me."

"No."

"Good." She ate a spoonful of the barley, and then another, carefully, as if a bell might ring and stop her. "I don't want to stand in a room and be talked about. I've done that."

He let it lie.

"They'll be punished," she said. "Won't they. For me."

"They might."

"They knew they might." She put the spoon down. "Ear told me, on the ship. He told me the way he'd tell me the weather. He said they'd talked it over in the lean-to before the bar came down, and decided they didn't mind." The corner of her mouth went in. "He's nineteen."

"So was I, once."

"You were never nineteen like that," said Liora, and picked up the spoon again.

He laughed.

She ate the rest of the bowl and the bread, and drank the beer, and wiped the bowl round with the last crust as the cohort did. Then she sat with her hands round the empty cup and looked past him at the room. Gaius was at the far end of the long table, with the fourth volume of Anaxis open in front of him, a candle at his elbow and his chin on his fist.

"He hasn't read a word of it," she said.

"No."

"He unwrapped it on the ship, when the sun was out. He turned the pages one after another to the end, and shut it again." She turned the cup in her hands. "He's afraid it's better than the third."

"Is it?"

"I don't know. I only ever read the third." She set the cup down. "Once." She stood up. "I'm going to sleep again. I may sleep for a week. Tell your father, if he asks, that I'm eating."

"He won't ask."

"Tell him anyway," said Liora; and climbed the stair, and overhead a stool scraped as she reached the top.

---

Marcus took the king's stair fast, out of habit.

At the first turn of the tower he stopped, and waited, with one hand on the cold stone of the wall. Gaius came up after him at his own pace, the good leg first and the bad one after, with his jaw shut. He did not look up. He reached the turn and passed it without stopping, and Marcus climbed behind him at the engineer's pace, which was slow, and they reached the top together.

The steward was waiting there with the door, and for once without his lamp: the slit windows let in summer enough to see the dust on the steps by. He said, "Highness. Engineer," and opened it.

The room was full.

It was a small room for five people and a clerk. The king sat at his writing table with his back to the window and the summer light behind him. Marcus had to look into the light to see his face, and the face was only a long grey shape with light round its edges. The window was open. The smell of the harbor came up through it, warm weed and tar and the hot-egg breath of the canals. On the sill, within reach of the king's chair, lay the length of carved stern-rail with the crowned dolphin cut in it, where it had lain at Midwinter. The small table by the window, where Marcus had eaten herring and mussels off plain boards with his father in the winter, had been carried out, and its stools with it. The young clerk stood at the end of the writing table with his wax on a board against his chest, and the watch-captain's report on the corner of the table by his hand.

The Lord Marshal stood at the right of the table. He was in his ribbons, all of them, and he had the look of a man who has arrived early to a room in order to have been in it first. Across from him, at the left of the table, with the light from the window on the side of her face, stood Cassandra.

She was in her white, with her cloak over her mail, and she had taken off her steel cap and held it under her arm, as a soldier carries his helmet before a commander. Her long plain face was perfectly still. Her brows were as straight as a rule. Her eyes went to Marcus as he came in, and to Gaius behind him, and stayed on Gaius a moment longer; then they returned to the king, and did not move.

"Prefect," said the king. "Engineer."

"Majesty."

"Sit down."

There were no chairs but his. Nobody sat.

The king turned his head to the clerk. "The count," he said.

The clerk looked up, startled, with his stylus over the wax.

"Not you." The king turned back. "Engineer. How many men went into the Vigil's house on the night of the new moon?"

Gaius stood with his weight on the good leg. "Two dozen, Majesty," he said. "And me."

"Armed how?"

"Shields."

"Swords?"

"On the bollards at Cyrene. They hung them up at the head of the slip before we sailed." Gaius thought, and was honest. "And a bread-knife, Majesty. Sennius laid it on top."

The king did not move.

"Knight-Abbess. How many of your sisters bled?"

"None, Majesty." Cassandra's voice was level, and carried, as it would have carried down a line of shields. "A wrist jarred. A cap lost."

"How many of them did he take out of your house?"

"One. Who was not a sister."

"How many locks broken?"

"None."

"Doors?"

"One," said Gaius, before she could. "The right-hand leaf of the great door, Majesty. Lifted off its pintles. It's leaning against the porch wall. I offered to put it back."

"Then put it back," said the king.

Gaius looked at Cassandra.

She looked at the king, and not at him. "He took it down, Majesty. He may put it up. Sister Agrippina will hold the lamp."

"That's settled, then." The king laid one hand on the writing table, square to its edge. "Two dozen men and an engineer. Shields. Their swords at Cyrene, and the bread-knife with them. No sister bled, no lock broken, one door, and the door on its pintles by noon. Is that the count, Marshal?"

"That is the count, Majesty." Varro had not moved. "I would add to it that they were convicts."

"So they were."

"Under sentence. Whose sentences were suspended by Your Majesty's own writ while they served in Cyrene and its waters, and in no others." Varro's voice was a good deal deeper than the king's, and had a parade ground's distances in it. "Your Majesty's capital is not in Cyrene's waters. By the writ, they are what they were before it. And they have broken a holy house by night, in Your Majesty's capital, under the Old Hill, half a mile from where Your Majesty sits."

"A little more than half a mile."

"Majesty."

"I've walked it." The king turned his head. "Prefect."

Marcus had decided on the way up the stair what he would say, and he said it.

"They're mine, Majesty. They went because they thought I couldn't send them, and they were right, and I let them go. I turned them back at sea and brought them here to answer for it. Whatever the sentence is, it's mine."

"No," said the king. "It's theirs. Their sentences were theirs before you ever saw them. You can't have them now because you've grown fond of them." He was silent a moment. "The Marshal is right. By my writ they are convicts again, and they broke a holy house in my city by night, and every man of them could be sent to the quarries before the week was out, or worse; and I have no doubt the Marshal has the paper for it."

Varro did not deny it.

"I pardon them," said the king.

The clerk's stylus stopped over the wax. Down in the harbor a gull was screaming at something, on and on, and the open window let it in.

"Every man of them," said the king. "Of the sentences they came to Cyrene under, and of the night of the new moon. The engineer too." He looked at the clerk. "Write it."

The clerk wrote it.

Varro pressed the bull's head of his ring into the heel of his hand, hard, and took it away.

"Then I ask one thing, Majesty," he said.

"Ask it."

"That their names be entered. Every man of them, by name, in the crown's book, as convicts pardoned for breaking the Vigil's house. So that it is known, in the city and after, who they are, and what they did, and that the crown pardoned it; and that the next man who thinks of lifting a door in this city knows his name will be written down too."

"Enter them," said the king. "Every one."

The clerk wrote that as well. His stylus skidded once on the wax, and he smoothed the place flat and wrote on.

"And that the pardon be cried," said Varro. "Publicly. By the city's crier, at the door of the house they broke, at noon today, before the order and before the city. So that the order has its satisfaction, Majesty, for it is owed one; and so that the city knows the crown does not count the breaking of houses a small thing, whatever it pardons."

The king was silent for a moment.

"Cry it," he said. "At noon. At the door the engineer will have hung by then." He turned a little toward Varro. "And cry the names, Marshal. Every one. As you say. So that it's known."

"Majesty."

Varro bowed. He held the bow a little longer than the form asked for.

"Knight-Abbess," said the king.

"Majesty."

"You have something to ask."

"The healer, Majesty." Cassandra did not shift the cap under her arm or move her eyes. "She was in the order's keeping, in the order's house, under the order's rule. She was taken out of it by night. I ask where she is."

"Is she here?"

"No, Majesty," said Cassandra.

"Has she asked the crown for anything?"

The king did not turn to Marcus. Marcus answered anyway.

"Nothing, Majesty. She's asked if anybody was hurt, and when to eat."

"The crown has no business inside a holy house," said the king, to Cassandra. "The Marshal has told me so, often, and he is right. I'll not send my guard into your house, Knight-Abbess, and I'll not send my guard to carry anybody back into it." He laid his other hand on the table beside the first. "If she is yours, rule her. If she is not, she may go where she likes."

Cassandra's teeth clicked, once.

"The order rules its own, Majesty," she said.

She made the king a soldier's bow, short, from the neck, with the cap under her arm, and walked out past Gaius without looking at him. Marcus heard her go down the stair, quick and even, a long way down, until the tower had her.

The king lifted two fingers off the table.

Marcus had seen the wine carried out of this room on that sign in the winter. The Marshal went out on it now, and Gaius after him, and the clerk last, with his board held to his chest; and the clerk drew the door to behind them.

---

When the door was shut the king did not say anything for a time.

He sat with his back to the window. The light lay along the floor between them, and on the writing table, on the candle and the stack of plain paper. Marcus stood and waited.

The king drew the chain out of his shirt, and leaned forward with the key, and unlocked the deep drawer on the right.

He took out a paper and laid it face up at the corner of the table, turned so that Marcus could read it, and Marcus came to the corner.

He had never seen it. He had known it was there since his first winter at Cyrene, from Valeria and the Marshal's letters to her and the whole court's knowing, and he had known what it said. He had not known it would be so beautiful.

It was a single sheet of the palace's best, written from edge to edge in a hand like a run of music, not a letter out of step and not a line out of true. It was the most beautiful hand in the palace, and everybody knew it was Damien's. *By the King's command.* The Prefect of Cyrene, and such of his officers as sailed with him, to be brought home under guard to answer before the council for exceeding the writ. Such of his officers: that was Roderic, and Valeria, who had written *I went with him* and sent it up this stair. At the foot was a space for one name, and no name in it.

"The Marshal came up after Bastion," said the king. "He stood where you are, and asked me to sign it."

Marcus looked at the paper.

"He says you failed," said the king.

"I did."

"He says you lost a gate."

"I didn't buy it."

Marcus could see his father's face now, with the light come round a little: long and grey and lined, the hair white at the back, the eyes deep and tired, and steady. He studied his son as he studied a column of figures, to be sure of it.

Then he took up the recall and pulled the drawer wide to lay it back; and Marcus, at the corner of the table, saw what was in the drawer. A half-sheet of the plain paper with two words on it in his father's own hand, cramped and slow, and under them his father's name and the day, in the ink that had gone on wet in the Lesser Hall: *Leave granted.* He had left it on the high table at Midwinter when he stepped down to lead out the dance, and had not thought of it since. The recall went down on top of it, in its beautiful hand, and covered it.

"It stays where it is," said the king.

He locked the drawer, and the chain went back inside his shirt.

"You turned them round at sea," he said.

"Yes."

"They'd have been at Cyrene by now."

"Yes."

"Where I'd have had to send for them." The king laid his hands on the table again. "The whole council would have watched me send for them. It would have taken a month, and the songs would have had a month." He was silent. "You brought them up my hill yourself, to be judged. Before I asked."

"They're mine," said Marcus.

"So you said." His father looked at him a moment longer. "Have you eaten?"

"Yes." He was at the door before he remembered the rest of it. "The healer's eating, Majesty. She asked me to tell you."

"I didn't ask."

"She said you wouldn't."

The long grey shape against the window was quiet for a moment.

"Eat again," said the king. "You're thinner than I am."

Marcus went down the stair slowly, a step at a time. At the foot of it Gaius was sitting on the lowest of the bier-steps with his leg out, waiting for him, and got up.

---

The door went back on a little before noon.
