The leaf stood where the front rank had leaned it, against the porch wall under the stone sun. Through the empty half of the doorway Marcus could see the oak bar still lying on the hall floor where it had fallen. Nobody in the house had chosen to move it. A sister stood in the gap where the right-hand leaf should have been, and the house's sick went round the corner to the poor gate.

The sister in the gap was Agrippina.

She was big and raw-boned, in her white and her coif, and she held the horn lamp. She had taken it down off its bracket and lit it, in broad day, in high summer, with the sun straight down into the lane and no shade left in it anywhere. She held it up at arm's length at the hinge end of the doorway, with the flame gone invisible in the daylight and her face like a magistrate's.

The cohort had come down to the lane with Gaius for the work, and the lane had begun to come after them: people stopping at both ends of it to see, and children up on the warehouse walls. Rufo's front rank walked the leaf off the wall, hinge side first, and held it upright in the doorway. The cousins set the jack under its bottom rail and ran the screw up until the leaf stood over its pintles. Marcus stood on the cobbles with his back to a warehouse wall and watched, because there was nothing else on earth he would rather have watched.

Agrippina did not speak to Gaius, and Gaius did not speak to her. He knelt on the porch slabs with his rule and a pot of tallow. Whenever he wanted a better look at a pintle he leaned toward the lamp a little, and she moved it a little, so that it was where he wanted it. "Thank you, Sister," he said, every time; and every time she looked over his head at the lane.

"Down," said Gaius at last.

The cousins turned the bars back, a little at a time. The leaf came down onto its three pintles one after another, sweetly, and settled; and the jack came out from under it, and the leaf hung.

Gaius got up, with a hand on the jamb for his knee, and swung it.

It swung. It turned on its pintles without a sound and met the left-hand leaf true, and he swung it open again and stood with his hand on the oak.

Then he took something out of his pouch. Marcus had to step forward to see what it was: three small round caps of bronze, each with a lip, each no bigger than an acorn cup, still bright from the file. He must have had the smith on the Canal Steps make them before he ever climbed the hill. He held them out to Agrippina on his open palm.

She looked at them over the lamp.

"Pintle-caps," said Gaius. "Set one over the top of each pintle, when the leaf's hung, and peen the lip over, and nobody lifts that door off from outside again. Not with a jack. Not with anything."

Agrippina put the lamp in his other hand without asking, and took the caps off his palm one at a time. She set the bottom one kneeling and the middle one standing. For the top she had the house's ladder brought out, and climbed it to the fifth rung with one of the cousins at its foot, and set the last cap there. Then she held her hand down for his hammer.

He gave it to her. She peened each lip over, the top one from the ladder and the others on her way down, hard, not looking at him, like a woman nailing shut something that has been open too long. Then she gave him back the hammer, and took the lamp, and blew it out, and shut herself inside.

Gaius stood on the porch with the hammer.

"It's a good door," he said, to nobody.

By then the lane was full.

It had been filling since before the door was hung: down the canal walk from the bend, up from the harbor end of the lane, and out of the backs of the warehouses where men had been loading. The dye-house women came with their blue arms folded. The fishwives climbed from the galley quay with the morning's last baskets on their hips, on their way somewhere else, and stopped. The canal's poor came from the champions' sand and the poor gate round the corner, in the rags they had been healed in. The crier pushed down the lane from the bend with his bell and his staff and the palace's blue sash across his chest, and the Marshal's clerk behind him carrying a roll. There was no room left to stand by then, and he had to be passed forward over a cart.

The cohort stood in a block in the lane before the porch, with their backs to the door Gaius had just hung, and could not have moved if they had wanted to. The city stood round them on three sides and up on the walls, and was very quiet.

The crier rang his bell. He was a stooped man with a great hollow voice, which was the trade.

"*Hear the King!*" he cried. "*Aurelian, by the grace of the Sunstone King of Aeridor and the Isles, to his city: whereas certain men, being convicts under sentence and in the service of the crown at Cyrene, did by night enter the house of the Vigil by the Lower Canal, and did take out of it one Liora, a healer, and did draw no blood—*"

He stopped there, because he had to. The lane had made a sound. It was not a cheer yet. It ran along the walls and the walk and back like wind going over standing barley.

"*—the King of his grace pardons them,*" the crier went on, louder, "*every man, of their sentences and of that night; and wills that their names be entered in the crown's book, and cried at this door, that it be known who they are!*"

He held out his hand behind him without looking, and the Marshal's clerk put the roll in it.

"*Decimus Rufo,*" cried the crier, "*optio, of the penal cohort!*"

The lane roared.

It came off the walls and the walk and the cart and the warehouse roofs all at once, a great flat crash of sound that climbed between the houses and broke back off the Vigil's white wall. Rufo, at the front of the block with his tally-stick in his fist, flinched. His hand went up to the plait above his ear, and stayed there.

"*Lucius, called Ear!*"

Somebody on the warehouse wall shouted, "*Which ear?*", and a woman screamed with laughter, and Ear turned his head and showed them the left one; and the lane gave him more than it had given Rufo.

"*Sennius of Lessa,*" cried the crier, and stopped, and looked at the roll, and read it again, as if to be sure it was written there: "*prisoner of war!*"

For a moment the lane had no answer for it. The quiet came down all at once. Somewhere among the fishwives a man said *Tharsian*, in the old voice; and Sennius, in the second rank, stood straight with his hands at his sides while his face turned the colour of the Vigil's wall.

"He held the healer's basin," shouted a woman up by the poor gate. "At the Baths. In chains. It's in the song."

It was not in the song. Marcus knew the song, all its wrong verses. But the woman at the poor gate had the voice to carry it, and a hand that Liora had mended, from the way she held it up. The lane took her word over the song's, and shouted Sennius of Lessa down the walk and up it again until the crier had to wait. Sennius stood in it as he would have stood in rain, with his face working, and let it come down on him.

So it went on. *Spurius Lento*, cried the crier, and nobody knew who that was until Fingers took his hat off; then a dye-house woman with blue arms to the elbow screamed "*Spurius!*" like a gull, and Fingers put his hat back on and called something to her that Marcus could not hear and the women round her could, and they shrieked. *Rusticus and Salvius, brothers.* *Naevius and Lollius*, who stood shoulder to shoulder as ever and did not move a muscle, and were cheered for it. *Vatinius*, who shut his eyes. *Plancus. Strabo. Scaeva.* The sawyers of the slip-yard and two of its carpenters, by name, who were not convicts at all. The Marshal's clerk had written them down with the rest, not knowing the difference; they were cheered as convicts and loved it. The men of the Silverwood cutting, one after another.

"*Gaius,*" cried the crier, last, "*engineer!*"

Gaius was still on the porch with the hammer. The lane turned and saw him there, in front of the door he had taken off and put back, and the noise it made then was different from the others: it had laughing in it. Gaius considered the hammer in his hand, and put it behind his back.

The crier rolled up the roll and rang his bell, and gave the lane the last of it: "*Pardoned, by the King's grace!*" That was the end of the king's business.

The lane came in.

---

They were carried to the Fishmarket Steps, as near as made no difference.

Marcus remembered afterward only scraps of the way. The canal walk solid with people from bank to wall, and the cohort in the middle of it like a boat in a flood. A man he had never seen pressing a skin of wine on Rufo, and Rufo handing it on to the next man without drinking. The Lantern's landlord standing in his door with the rag on his shoulder, watching it go by. Somebody shouting up at the Lantern's roof, *Angel! Angel!*, until the window under the eaves opened and Liora put her head out, with her hair down, and looked at the lane full of people from wall to canal. Then the window shut; and a little after, the Lantern's door opened and she came down into the lane, barefoot still, and Sennius pushed out of the cohort and fell in at her back, and the lane made room.

It made room all the way down the canal and round the harbor head and along the galley quay to the Steps. It opened ahead of her and closed behind, so that she walked the whole way in a space the width of a cart, as she had walked once behind a barrow. Marcus walked behind her with Alana, who had found him somewhere at the bend and taken his arm. The city did not touch her. Women held up children to see, and an old man with no teeth in his head stood on a bollard and wept, and nobody came nearer than the cart's width. She walked through it looking at faces, and when she saw one she knew from the poor gate she stopped and spoke to it; and then it was all right to come nearer, for that one, and it came.

The Fishmarket Steps climbed from the water between their houses, flight on flight, with the fish-bell at the bottom and the evening light on the top. The Steps had been at it all afternoon, while the lane made its slow way round the harbor head. Somebody had dragged the fish-boards together on the landing above Gemella's step to make a table, and somebody else had lit braziers along the quay at the bottom, and there were eels splitting on the coals and mutton turning. A smell came off the whole hill of fat and onions and charcoal and fish. Marcus would have known it with his eyes shut, from the mornings when Felix could not get him home up the hill without feeding him first.

At the foot of the Steps the Corvus women came out of their lodging-house and took Alana indoors. Felix's mother had Marcus before he had got his foot on the first step.

She was small and broad, in her shawl in spite of the heat, and she came down through the crowd as a barge comes down a canal, by not stopping. She took his chin in her hand and turned his face to the nearest brazier, and let it go.

"You've not been eating," said Gemella.

"I've been eating."

"Not what I'd call eating." She put her hand on his arm, and he was sat down on the bottom step beside her eel-board before he knew he had been moved, with his back to the warm stone and a board across his knees. There was an eel on it, split and done on the coals with salt until the skin blistered, and a slab of mutton off the spit with the fat crackling at the edge. There was half a barley loaf, and onions roasted whole in their skins. "Eat that," said Gemella. "And then I'll see."

He ate it. It was good.

Felix was at his elbow with a cup, which was water; Marcus had not seen him arrive, and had not seen him fill it. Felix stood with the jug, looking out at the quay, with an expression of grave disapproval that Marcus had learned over the years meant Felix was happy.

"Your mother," said Marcus, with his mouth full.

"Strictly, Highness, it's her step."

"I know whose step it is."

"Then strictly you should have asked before you sat on it." Felix filled the cup again, which was not empty. "She'd have said yes. But strictly."

On the third step, the dry one, an eel-wife as brown and creased as one of her own smoked eels had Rusticus by one ear and Salvius by the other. She was weeping and scolding them both at once.

Valeria found him a little before dusk.

She came along the galley quay from the harbor head, in her mail with the blue cloak over it and the Farrier on her shoulder, with two of the Lanciarii at her back. She stopped in front of Gemella's step and looked down at him eating mutton there with the city all round him.

"Highness."

"Captain. You're going up the hill."

"All night. The Marshal's order." She said it flat. Then she said, "Did you hear it? Yesterday."

He did not pretend not to know what she meant. "I heard something."

"The whole tower heard it. The guard at the top sent down to the guard at the bottom, after you'd gone, to ask what it was, and the guard at the bottom didn't know." Valeria looked out over the braziers. "I waited at the foot of that stair for your brother more nights than I could tell you. Up at a run with a dispatch, down a step at a time with the answer." She was quiet a moment. "I never heard it once."

"Heard what?"

Valeria looked down at him with great patience.

"He laughed, Highness," she said. "Your father. It was him laughing."

She went back along the quay the way she had come, with her two men behind her, toward the harbor head and the hill and the long night on the wall. Marcus sat on Gemella's step with the board on his knees, and could not eat.

---

Gemella took the board off his knees, and he got up and climbed to the edge of the landing above her step. He had not seen Domitia come. She was across the landing at the foot of the second flight, with her back against the corner of a house where the Steps turned. She was in her white and her mail, with the steel cap under her arm and the belled spear grounded beside her. The crowd ran round her as water runs round a post. Nobody on the Steps knew who she was, only what: a sister of the Vigil, three days after the Vigil's door came off. The fishwives gave her a little room for it, and eyed her sidelong. She did not seem to notice them.

She was looking at Liora.

Liora was on the landing by the fish-boards, with the canal's poor round her and some of the Steps' children, and Gemella's eldest granddaughter at her shoulder doing something to her hair. Somebody had got her a gown. It was green wool, a girl's best, with the hem too short for her by a span and the sleeves stopping at the elbow. She had let it be put on her as she would have let a dressing be put on a wound, and then held out the short sleeves and laughed. Her hair was down. The granddaughter had combed it out, and it fell heavy and honey-coloured over the green to her waist, and the light of the brazier under the landing lay in it.

Marcus had never heard Domitia called anything but plain. She was heavyset and weathered, and her face under the coif had been made for telling people they were wrong. She had seen Liora once before, on the quay at Cyrene, in a grey gown and an apron with her hands red from the basin. She had looked her over then from her hair to her shoes, as she had looked over the cutter's plank. Now she looked at her from the corner of the house, and her face went quiet. Everything in it that had been ready to correct went out of it, and left it open, and a good deal younger.

She stood so while the piper got through a whole tune. Then she looked down at herself, at her own side under the white cloak, where Marcus knew the mail had been let out. She lifted one finger and tapped it there twice.

Then she looked up again at Liora.

---

The piper on the Steps was blind, or said he was, and had played for the Fishmarket since before Felix could walk. He sat on an upturned eel-tub on the landing with a boy beside him on another, beating a fish-box with two bones; he had played every tune the Steps knew, twice, and was starting on the third time round.

Liora crossed to him between tunes, and bent, and said something in his ear.

He listened, with his blind face turned up. Then he nodded, and wet his lips, and put the pipe to them; and what it gave was not a quay tune at all. It was slow and grave and turned on itself in long steps, like people walking round a hall in their best clothes. In the lodging-house door at the foot of the Steps the Corvus women turned their heads; it had been theirs before it was the court's. It was the Long Measure.

The landing cleared without being asked. The children were pulled back by their collars.

Liora took something out of the bosom of the green gown, and stooped, and laid it on the stones in the middle of the clear space. It was a red cap of the king's madder, gone dark and stiff and out of shape, with a crease across the crown where a fist had crushed it.

Marcus knew it. Rufo's head came up by his brazier, and Ear's, and Fingers went still with a cup halfway to his mouth. It was Tappo's. He had jigged with it in his fist on the long quay at Cyrene on Midwinter night while this woman danced the court way round him, and he had died at Bastion carrying her down the hill to Roderic.

She stood over it a moment. Then she sank.

It was the court's deep reverence, the one a girl is taught for queens, so low that the short green skirt lay round her on the stones and her bowed head was over her knee. She made it to the cap. She rose out of it slowly, and held out her hand, palm up, to the empty air above it, where a man would have stood.

And she danced the court way round it.

The piper had the measure, and the boy on the fish-box had the beat under it, slow. She went round the cap in the long walking steps of it, with her hand out and her head up and her bare feet sure on the warm stones. Marcus had seen it danced by every lady of the court, every Midwinter of his boyhood, and seen his brother lead it out. He had never seen it danced on a fish-landing, to a red cap, by a woman in a borrowed gown with no shoes. She made one turn with the space empty about her, and the whole of the Steps watching from the stones and the windows and the roofs. Across the harbor the dusk bell was ringing on the Old Hill, and nobody on the Steps turned to it; and he saw, before she came round to face him, that her face was wet.

On the second turn her hand went out again, to the place in the air where the court way puts a partner.

Ear was in it.

He had come off the quay and up the steps and through the crowd, and across the clear space onto the landing, without anybody seeing him go; and he did not take her hand. He did not know how. He stood beside the cap with his long arms hanging and his freckles gone dark in a white face, and began to jig.

He could not jig, either; none of them could. He jigged all knees and elbows and boots, with no notion of the beat at all; and it was not his own dance he was doing, Marcus saw. It was another man's, as near as he could remember it.

Then Rufo was there.

Rufo was up the steps and onto the landing at once, and began to stamp, gravely, with the tally-stick still in his fist, like a bull trying to put out a grass fire; and then Sennius, who had never seen the jig and did it anyway; and Rusticus and Salvius, making a contest of it, with their mother shouting the score from the third step; and the cousins, side by side, in silence, in perfect step with each other and with nothing else; and Fingers; and Plancus, and Vatinius with his eyes shut, and Scaeva, and the sawyers, and the men of the cutting. They came up off the quay and out of the crowd one after another and then all at once, and filled the landing round her. Every man of them jigged as badly as a man can, round her and round the red cap on the stones. The piper played the Long Measure grave and slow through the middle of it, and the boy beat the fish-box under it, and the Steps began to clap, and then to stamp; and then somebody up the second flight started *The Angel of the Baths*, and the Steps sang it over the pipe in another time altogether.

She stopped dancing. She stood in the middle of them with the cap at her feet and her hands over her mouth. She was laughing or she was crying, and Marcus could not tell from the edge of the landing which; he did not think she could.

Then she went round them, every one, as she went round a ward. She took Ear by the shoulders and pulled him down to her and held him, and he held her back, carefully, with both hands flat, his face over her shoulder screwed up like a boy's. She held Rufo, who had to bend nearly double, and who put his arms round her and lifted her off the stones without meaning to, and set her down again, gently. She held Sennius, who stood rigid for a moment, and then did not. She held the cousins together, one in each arm, and they held her, and for once neither of them looked at the other. She held Fingers, who said something that made her laugh into his collar. She held them all, the men of the cutting whose names she knew from the pallets and the sawyers whose names she did not; and every one of them held her, and none of them let go first.

Marcus looked across the landing for Domitia.

She was still against the corner of the house, with her spear and her steel cap. She had not moved. Her face was wet too, and she had done nothing about it.

---

Alana came to him at the bottom of the Steps, by the water, when it had got dark.

He had come down out of the noise to stand by the fish-bell, where the harbor slapped at the bottom step and the lights of the far side lay on the black water in long broken lines. Across from him the galleys lay at their moorings, and beyond them the old berths ran off along the foot of the Old Hill into the dark, with a lantern at *Constancy*'s stern among them. Up on the hill the Lanciarii's lights were strung out along the palace wall. He could see the king's window from here, at the top of the tower, lit.

"There you are," said Alana.

She stood beside him at the edge of the step, close, with her shoulder against his arm. She smelled of woodsmoke and Gemella's onions, and faintly, as she always did, of ink. Her right hand was in the fold of her skirt, where she kept it when it was shaking and she did not want him to see. He had seen.

"Rufo is teaching the Steps to jig," she said. "It's the worst thing I've ever watched. Gemella has given Ear a pie. A whole one. He doesn't know what to do with it, so he's carrying it about. Felix says that's your father's window."

"It is."

"Is he watching?"

"I don't know. Valeria says he laughed."

"I heard. The whole quay's heard. There's a verse already." She was quiet. "It's wrong, of course. It has him laughing in the Hall of Kings, from the throne, at the Marshal."

"That's a better story."

"It's not the one that happened." Alana's lips moved a little over something, a figure, or a line of the verse she was getting right for the weather-book. "I'll put down the one that happened."

He turned his head and looked at her. Her dark hair had lost half its pins on the Steps, and there was a smudge of ink on her jaw, and her eyes in the dark were on the lit window.

"Marry me," he said.

She did not look away from the window. "At home, I said."

"We're at the capital."

"Then not yet."

"You told me to ask again."

"I did." She took her right hand out of her skirt and put it in his, shaking, and let him hold it. "I like it very much. Keep doing it." Her eyes were laughing at him in the dark. "But not here, Marcus. Not with your father's window lit up over us like a lamp in a play. I'd never know which of us you'd said it to."

They stood on by the fish-bell with her shaking hand in his, and the music coming down the Steps behind them, the Long Measure and the jigging under it, and the city's lights on the water.

Then they went back along to Gemella's board, where Gemella fed her, and stayed until the piper stopped. He walked her to the lodging-house at the foot of the Steps, where the Corvus women had kept her old room. On the threshold she turned in the light from the stair, and touched his face once with her shaking hand, and went in.

He walked the cohort home to the Lantern along the galley quay, in the dark, with Liora in the middle of them asleep on her feet and Sennius holding her elbow; and nobody in the lower town was in bed.

---

He slept on the bench under the canal window, with his cloak rolled under his head and his men on the floor all round him, breathing.

The window was over him. Through the empty frame above the sack he could see a strip of sky, and the steam going up off the canal into it, lit faintly from the room by the lamp the landlord had left at the stair foot. The canal ran by under the window with the small sounds a canal makes at night: a slap, a trickle, a boat's fender knocking somewhere along the walk. Rufo was snoring. Somebody in the yard was talking in his sleep, arguing with his mother.

He woke in the last of the dark, and did not know why.

He lay still. The canal went by. Rufo snored. Up the stair, through the ceiling, there was no sound, which meant Liora was asleep, and Sennius on his stool outside her door, awake.

Then he heard it, the thing that had woken him: footsteps on the canal walk, coming down from the bend. One person, walking alone, not hurrying. They came along the walk toward the window, and the sound of them dropped in through the empty frame over his head, small and clear; and they did not go past. They stopped at the Lantern's door.

The sky in the window had begun, barely, to grey.

Somebody knocked at the Lantern's door: three times, patient and even, as a person knocks who has every right to be let in, and means to wait until they are.

---

# Chapter Six: Cast Out

**GAIUS**

Gaius was nearest the door.

He had not been asleep. He had lain on the boards of the common room with his coat under his head and his bad knee drawn up, and listened to the house cool. The Lantern's main beam was old oak with a shake in it from end to end, and it ticked as the heat left the roof. He had been listening to it for an hour, and thinking that somebody should put an iron strap round it before it failed. So he heard the feet on the walk outside, and heard them stop at the step, and he was sitting up before the first knock was done and on his feet by the third.

Marcus was up on one elbow on the bench under the window. Their eyes met across the dark room. Gaius stepped over Salvius and drew the bolt.

It was Sister Agrippina.

She filled the doorway from post to post in her white, with the hem of it dark from the wet of the walk. She had the horn lamp in her hand, and it was not lit: the sky over the canal behind her had begun to grey. Her other hand, scrubbed red to the wrist like a laundress's, held a paper, folded once and sealed. Over her arm was a bundle done up in a sheet. The canal's morning smell came in past her, weed and wet stone, and from up the bend the sourness of the dye-house vats.

"Engineer," she said.

"Sister."

"I've a paper for the healer. I'm to give it into her hand." Her eyes went past him into the room, where men were sitting up out of their blankets one after another, and Rufo was rising out of his like a hill coming out of fog. "I'll wait here."

He did not ask her in, and she did not ask. He climbed the stair.

Sennius was on his feet at the top beside his stool, with one hand on the jamb of Liora's door. He took it off quick when he saw Gaius. Behind him the door stood open, and Liora was sitting on the edge of the bed in the grey light with her feet on the boards, in the house's white shift and the undyed gown over it, awake, with her hair down her back. The borrowed green gown lay folded at the foot of the bed.

"Who is it?"

"Agrippina," said Gaius. "With a paper."

She looked at him for a moment, and put her hair back over her shoulder with one hand, and stood.

"I'd better have it, then," she said.

She went down the stair in front of him barefoot, with her hand on the wall. The men in the common room got out of her way without a word, and she crossed to the door and stood in it. Agrippina was taller than she was by most of a head. The two of them faced each other on the step in the grey, and the canal went by under the walk.

"Sister," said Liora.

"Healer." Agrippina held out the paper. "From the Knight-Abbess. Into your hand."

Liora took it, and broke the seal, and unfolded it, and turned a little so that the light from the sky would fall on it; and turned it a little further, so that it would fall on it for him too, at her shoulder.

It was in the quick slanting hand of the letter Marcus had brought him at the foot of the staging, and this time the Abbess had signed it herself.

*By the rule of the order of the Vigil, in chapter, at the dusk bell. Liora, of the house of Mela, entered in the book of the Unlit in her sixteenth year and taught the warmth and the Seeing by this order, who left a vigil of reflection unfinished, and who has confessed before the Knight-Abbess and two sisters to taking three lives by the order's arts, is put out. Her name is struck from the book. The houses of the order are shut to her. She is not to be called of the Vigil. By the same rule, what she confessed goes to the crown's justice with her name.*

*Cassandra, Knight-Abbess.*

Gaius was still on the line above the name when she folded it along its crease, and folded it again, small.

"At the dusk bell," she said.

"In chapter," said Agrippina. "I was there."

"Was it read properly?"

"It was read right." Agrippina's face did not move. "Sister Philippa read it, and nobody spoke after, and the Abbess said the words, and it was done. It goes up the hill at first light. The other part. As the rule says."

So at the dusk bell yesterday, Gaius thought, she had been on the landing at the Fishmarket in a girl's green gown with her hair down, going round a red cap on the stones; and in the white house by the canal a woman had stood up in chapter and struck her name out of a book.

Agrippina held out the bundle.

"Your own things," she said. "From the room. The gown you came in, and your shoes. Your comb. The letters." She did not look at Gaius. "The engineer's letter."

Liora took the bundle against her chest with both arms. Then she put it into Gaius's, with the folded paper on top. She stood on the step and drew the undyed gown off over her head, and shook it out, and folded it, sleeves in, square, as a ward-sister folds a blanket for the shelf; and held it out.

"That's the house's," she said. "The shift too. I'll send it."

Agrippina looked at it. Then she hung the cold lamp on her wrist and took the gown in both hands.

"I held the lamp for him yesterday," she said. "For the door. I'd not have held it for this."

"I know."

"I'm sorry, girl."

"I know that too," said Liora. "Go home, Agrippina. They'll want you at the door."

The sister set off up the walk toward the bend, with the folded gown on her arm and the lamp swinging unlit from her wrist. Liora took the bundle back from him and stood barefoot on the Lantern's step in the white shift, holding it against her, and watched her out of sight. Then she came in past him, and Gaius shut the door and put the bolt across.

---

Valeria came at sunrise, the back way.

She came in at the yard door from the lane behind the lean-to and ducked under the lintel into the common room. She was still in her mail and the blue cloak from her night on the Old Hill, with the Farrier in her hand, and under the white scar her face was grey from a night on her feet. The room was full and silent. Liora was at the table under the window in a plain grey gown of her own, with her hair pinned up, eating.

She was eating everything. The landlord had brought bread, and a bowl of last night's mutton broth warmed through, with the fat beaded on it and the smell of it filling the room, and cheese, and an onion. She ate steadily, with her elbows on the table. Marcus sat across from her with water. The paper lay between them, open now, under the salt.

Valeria read it standing, without touching it. Then she sat down on the end of the bench as if her knees had decided it, with the Farrier upright beside her.

"I'll not have to tell you the first part, then," she said.

"Tell us the second," said Marcus.

"The Marshal was at the Hall of Pleas at first light, before the doors opened. He'd been waiting for them to open." Valeria's right thumb went looking along the Farrier's haft for the smooth place, and found it. "He laid an information before Justice Herennia. Three deaths at Cyrene, in the crown's keeping, in your first month there. Prisoners of war. Statius, Pansa and the sergeant Atilius, of Tharsis." She did not look at Liora. "The charge is murder. It's written. The justice signed the warrant before the sun was up, and the bailiffs take it out at the third hour, when the court sits."

Up in the beam the old shake ticked once, as the sun came on the roof.

"The order's paper went up the hill with the first light," said Valeria, "and the Marshal had his information drawn before it came. He knew it was coming. He knew which morning." She rubbed her eyes. "I came down the tomb stair. Nobody's seen me."

"Why couldn't he lay it before?" said Gaius.

Valeria looked at him. It was Marcus who answered.

"Because she was in a holy house." He said it quietly, to the water in his cup. "While the order claimed her, the order judged her, and the crown left her to it. The crown doesn't go in at a holy house's door. It never has. It's older than the dome." He turned the cup a little on the table. "Not even for murder. Not even for a king's son."

Gaius had spent his life knowing where a load goes when you take a member out. A man knocks out a prop under a gallery because the prop is in his way, and the gallery does not care that he had a reason; it puts its weight on the next thing along, and if there is no next thing, it comes down. He had gone up to that house in the dark with the cohort and a screw out of a book, and lifted the door off its pintles without breaking a thing, and walked her out into the street under the shields; and the order had let go.

He had taken out the wrong prop. He had taken out the only prop.

Liora put down the heel of her bread, and dusted her fingers, and looked at him across the table. The corner of her mouth went in.

"Don't," she said.

"I haven't said anything."

"You were doing a sum." She turned to Marcus. "The third hour."

"Wait a day," said Marcus. "One day. Let me go up the hill and find out what can be done, before they put a hand on you."

"No."

"Liora."

"No." She said it gently. "I walked out of that house because nobody had a right to keep me in it. They had a rule and a lock, and no law. This is a law. Three men are dead in the crown's keeping, and I killed them, and a court wants to know about it." She folded her hands on the table. "I won't be fetched. I'll go up before they come."

Marcus looked at her. The scar along his jaw went tight, and stayed so; then it let go.

"I'll find the way," he said.

"Find it, then. I'll be where they put me." She stood up. "Gaius will walk up with me."

---

The lower town did not know yet. It was too early, and the lane outside the Lantern was only the canal's ordinary morning: a man sluicing his step with a bucket, two women with a fish-basket slung between them on a pole, the bread-carts going up from the bakehouse by the lock with the loaves under wet cloths and the smell of them coming after. A boy at the corner of the Canal Steps knew her, and stood with his mouth open, and his mother cuffed him and then stood with hers open too. Nobody followed. Gaius had told the cohort to stay in the yard, and it was a measure of the morning that they let the two of them go up alone.

The Old Hill was steep, and the street that climbed it from the canal was cut in steps at every turn, dished in the middle like old whetstones. His knee started on the first flight and kept on, a hot drawn ache under the cap that ran down into the shin, and he climbed with his weight on the good leg and his hand on the warm wall and said nothing. She walked at his shoulder, at his pace, and said nothing either, until the turn at the tomb terraces.

"When this is done," she said, "I'm having that knee."

"After."

"That's what you always say."

"It's always true." He got his breath at the top of the flight. Below them the canals came down out of the hill to the sea, smoking in the early heat, and the harbor lay beyond them flat and bright as a new-tinned pan. "It's still after."

She almost laughed. Then she did not, and they went on up.

"They cried your names yesterday," she said, at the next turn. "Sennius told me. Every one: Ear's, and Fingers's, and yours last. And the hammer." She was looking at the steps. "When the crier said *pardoned* I heard the lane from the window. It came up the walls. I was glad. I was so glad I couldn't stand still."

"I know. I saw."

"Men are pardoned for the crimes of war," said Liora.

He waited for the rest of it. But she did not finish it, and he did not ask, and they climbed.

The Hall of Pleas stood on the shoulder of the Old Hill below the palace gate: a long low building of grey stone with a portico of square pillars along its front, and the crown's scales cut over the middle door, worn soft by weather. There were steps up to the portico, wide and shallow, with the morning sun on them. At the top, in the shade of the pillars, stood the Lord Marshal of Aeridor.

He was broad and grey, in his ribbons, with two men of his household behind him in his colours and a clerk with a writing case; and he was bent over the clerk's case, so that he did not see them until they were on the steps. Then he saw them.

She went up the steps to him in her own grey gown, with Gaius limping at her shoulder, and stopped on the step below him, and looked up.

"Lord Marshal," said Liora.

Varro's right hand closed, and the bull's head of his ring went into the heel of his hand and stayed there.

"Lady Liora." He had a good voice for a portico. "I'm sorry to see you here."

"You've been waiting since first light to see me here, my lord."

"I have. Three men were in the crown's keeping, at Cyrene, under the crown's word, and they died of it. If my men had done it, I'd hang them." His eyes came to Gaius. "If the Prefect's had, Engineer, he'd hang them himself. He gave his word for those men in front of his whole town. You were there."

Gaius did not answer. He had been there. It was the worst thing about the man, he thought, standing on the hot step with his knee singing: that now and then he was right.

Liora climbed past Varro as if he were one of the pillars. At the middle door a bailiff in the crown's blue stood with a staff: an old man with a pouch of a chin, who had been told to keep the door and had not been told about this. She said something to him that Gaius did not hear, and the old man took his hat off.

"Not you," she said to Gaius, from the doorway. "I'll go in alone."

"I know."

She came back out of the doorway, one step, onto the stone of the portico, and put up her hand. With one finger, lightly, she touched the line of his left eyebrow, where the short pale hairs were growing back in, stiff as a new brush, after the barrow.

"They're coming back," she said.

Then she went in. The old bailiff put his hat on again and shut the door behind her: a plain oak door, two leaves, iron-strapped. It closed without a sound, because somebody oiled it.

Gaius stood in front of it on the portico. Behind him Varro went down the steps with his household, saying something to the clerk about the court's hours, like a man accounting for a late cart.

---

The cohort waited for him in the Lantern's yard, all of them, among the water-butts and the landlord's hens.

He saw at the gate that they had been talking, and what about. It was in the way they stood: not in a crowd but in a knot. Rufo was at the middle of it with his tally-stick in his fist, Fingers at his elbow with the back of a sack drawn over in charcoal, the cousins at the edge with their arms folded the same way. Ear was sitting on the yard wall with his long legs hanging and his face swollen. Sennius stood by the lean-to with his back against the post, looking at nothing, and when Gaius came in at the gate he looked at Gaius, and away.

"She went in," said Gaius.

"We heard." Rufo's voice was quiet for a man of his size. "Felix's mother had it before you were down the hill. The whole Steps has it." He turned the tally-stick over. "A room, she says. At the top of the Hall. With a bailiff on the stair."

"Not a bailiff," said Fingers. "Two. One at the stair foot and one at the top, and they change at the bells." He put the charcoal behind his ear. He had been up the hill too, then, while Gaius was on the steps; Gaius had not seen him go, and that was Fingers. "There's a back door on the lane, for the carts with the court's firewood. It's a Vessan box lock. Old. Brass wards." The tip of his tongue showed between his teeth, as it did over a lock. "I could have it open before you'd done asking."

"We did it once," said Rufo. "We can do it twice."

"Nobody'd bleed," said Rusticus. "We'd see to it."

"We'd do it better," said Salvius. "We know how, now."

"We've got the jack," said one of the cousins, and the other nodded. It was more than Gaius had heard out of either of them since Stoneheart.

They were looking at him. All of them, the whole yard, the men of the cutting and the sawyers and Plancus with his barge-poler's shoulders and Vatinius with his eyes for once open. They were looking at him the way men on the staging look at the foreman when the frame starts to go: waiting to be told which rope.

"Not twice," Gaius said.

Nobody moved.

"She walked in." He did not raise his voice; there was no need, in the yard. "Up the hill, on her own feet, in her own gown, before they came for her. She stood on the step and told the Marshal so to his face. If we carry her out of there, she didn't walk in. She ran." He looked at Rufo.

"It's a rope, Engineer." Rufo said it flat. "Not a vigil. It's a rope at the end."

"I know what it is at the end."

"Then—"

"Then we wait for the Prefect," said Gaius.

He heard it go round the yard, and land. They did not know about the prop, and he was not going to tell them in a yard full of hens.

"And if he can't?" said Ear, from the wall.

His voice cracked on it. He was gripping the coping either side of him with his long hands, and his face was wet.

"He can," said Gaius.

He had never in his life said a thing about a structure he had not measured. But he said it to the boy on the wall, and the boy believed him. By and by the knot came apart, and the men drifted off about the yard to the things men do when they have been told to wait. At the butts the cousins washed shirts that were clean, and the smell of wet linen came across the yard. Plancus sat on the rim of a butt and watched the landlord's hens. Fingers went into the lean-to, where presently Gaius heard him start to sing, low, a song about a lock that would not open for anybody but a widow. Rufo put his tally-stick back in his belt and went to the gate and stood in it, looking up the lane toward the hill, and his hand went up once to the fair lock plaited over his ear and came down again.

Gaius sat down on the mounting-block by the gate, with his leg out straight, and his hands were shaking. Not much. Enough that he put them between his knees.

---

Domitia came at dusk, by the canal walk, with her spear and her bag.

She had her steel cap on and her white cloak over her mail. She came in at the Lantern's front door and through the common room and out into the yard as if she had been told to report to it, which, it turned out, she had. A smell of lye soap came in with her. She set the butt of the spear down by the gate, on the one cobble there that lay flat, so that the little bell at its socket spoke once. She took in the yard, and the hens, and the shirts on the line.

"Those want boiling," she said. Then: "The Abbess has sent me back to the army."

Gaius got up off the mounting-block.

"To the army," he said.

Her grey eyes were on him, steady, under the steel cap. "I'm to sail with the Prefect when he sails, and be the army's healer while the army has none." She shifted the bag on her shoulder. "I made my report this afternoon. In person. She kept me waiting three days to make it."

"How long was it?"

"Two pages."

"About the healer?"

"About the latrine trench." Her mouth was very straight. "Where it was, and where it is now, and why. And the cookhouse well, and the spring. And about the barber's hands." She stopped. "Hostus. His hands. How he keeps them, and what he does with them, and what the cohort's dressings were like when I came and what they're like now."

"What did the Abbess say?"

"Nothing." Domitia shifted the spear into the crook of her arm. "She read it. Then she read it again, all of it, from the top, the latrine and all. Then she put it down on the table, and set the inkpot on it, and said, *You were sent to the army, Domitia,* and I said, *Yes, Mother,* and she said, *Go back to it.*"

She lifted one finger and tapped twice on the shaft of her spear, below the bell. Then she looked at her finger as if it had gone off without her.

"She read it twice," she said. "I've written to her every night since I came south, and I never knew her read a thing twice in twenty years."

She unslung her bag, which Ear had come down off the wall to carry for her and had not dared to touch, and gave it to him, and went in to see about her supper before the bell.

---

On the third day the justice let him up the stair.

He had asked at the Hall of Pleas every morning, at the side door, of a clerk in a black gown who wrote his name each time on a slate and rubbed it off each time with his sleeve. On the third morning the clerk did not rub it off, but went away up a stair with it, and came back and said, "The justice says the engineer may go up. Once. For the time it takes the water to go down a hand." He looked at Gaius over the slate. "She says to tell you she's measured the jar."

So he went up.

The stair went up inside the back of the Hall in four short flights, whitewashed, with a rope for a rail, and he took every one of them on the good leg. There was a bailiff at the foot, sitting on a bench, and a bailiff at the top, standing. The one at the top was younger, and had a key on a chain at his belt and a water-clock on the sill of the stair window: a clay jar with a hole in its foot, dripping into a basin. He filled the jar to its mark from a jug as Gaius came up, and the drip began. Then he walked down the short passage to the only door in it, and put the key in, and turned it, and opened the door, and left it standing open, and went back to his jar.

It was a clean room.

He had come up braced for the steam-box house, and it was nothing like it: a whitewashed room under the roof, not large, with a window in the gable end standing open on a square of hot blue sky. The sounds of the hill came in at it, swifts and a cart and someone a long way off beating a rug. On the sill, in the sun, lay one plum. There was a cot with a blanket, folded; a table and a stool; a jug and a basin and a cup, and a plate with the crumbs of a meal on it. Her bag was on the floor at the foot of the cot. The boards were scrubbed white. It smelled of limewash and of the sun on the roof tiles, and faintly of the plum.

She was at the window with her back to him, with her hair down again, in her own grey gown.

"You came up the stair," she said, without turning. "I could hear the knee."

"Every rise the same height."

She turned round then, and he saw her face, and she was all right. She was thinner than at Cyrene and she had not slept enough, and there was the line between her brows that the house had put there; but her eyes were clear, and she was all right.

"Sit down," she said. "There's the stool. I've got the cot."

He did not sit down. He was looking at the door.

It opened outward, into the passage: oak, ledged and braced, two fingers thick, with two iron straps clenched through it, and the eyes of the straps dropped over two pintles leaded into the jamb on the passage side. The lock was a box lock on the passage face, iron, with its keeper let into the stone a span deep. Whoever hung it had known his trade. The pintles were on the outside. He had seen them from the passage as the bailiff opened it, and he could not stop seeing them now: both a little proud of their lead, and neither capped, and the upper strap with a hair of play in its eye, and the leaf's weight no more than two strong men could take on a bar. With the jack, from the passage, in the dark, a quarter of an hour, and the lock would never know.

"Don't," said Liora.

He looked round. She was watching him from the window.

"I wasn't," he said.

"You were measuring it."

"I always measure it." He made himself take his eyes off the pintles. "I'd measure the door of a privy."

"I know you would." She came and sat on the end of the cot, and put her hands in her lap. "Sit down, Gaius. You've got till the water's down a hand, and you're spending it on hinges."

He sat on the stool. It was a little too low.

"They're good to me," she said. "The young one's mother has a bad hip, and he asked if I'd look at it, if she came up, and the justice said no; and then the justice came up herself and said no to my face, so that I'd know it was hers and not his. She's very thin." The corner of her mouth went in. "I like her. I think she'd hang me tomorrow if the law said to, and not lose a night's sleep, and be right not to."

"That's not a comfort."

"It is to me." She looked at him. "She told me Marcus came. Yesterday."

"He did. He sat in her court all morning, and was with her after. He came down the hill and told us where, and not why." He turned his hands over on his knees. "We sail tomorrow on the morning ebb. For Cyrene, and then the whole fleet west, to Silverwood."

"Good."

"I'll not go."

"You will."

"There's nothing at Silverwood I'm for. They want sums at a council. Alana can—"

"Alana can copy your sums. She can't do them, and she'd be the first to say it." Liora leaned forward a little on the cot. "He'll need his sums, Gaius. He'll need every one of you. He's going to find the way, he said, and he will; but he'll find it at Silverwood, not here, and you can't help him find it sitting on that bench at the stair foot with the bailiff."

"I'd not be on the bench." He was looking at the door again. "I'd be on the—"

"Not twice," she said.

It came back to him in her mouth, quietly, from the end of the cot; and he had nothing to say to it.

The water was going down in the jar on the stair sill. He could hear it.

"Have you read it yet?" she said. "The fourth volume."

"No."

"Coward."

"It's more arches," said Gaius. "I know about arches."

"You're afraid it's better than the third."

"I'm afraid it's worse," he said.

She laughed. It was short, and it surprised her, and after it she glanced at the open door.

The bailiff said, "Engineer," from the passage, not loudly.

Gaius got up off the stool. She got up off the cot. They stood a pace apart in the clean room with the swifts going past the open window, and neither of them did anything about the pace, because they never had; and then she put out her hand, and took hold of the front of his coat, and held it, one fistful of the cloth over his breastbone, hard, for about as long as it takes to say a name. Then she let it go, and smoothed it flat.

"Go on," she said.

He went out. The bailiff shut the door behind him and turned the key. It went over in the box with a short iron sound, and the bolt shot home into the stone.

Gaius stood in the passage with his face to the door.

It was the outside of it. That was all he could see: the grain of the oak, and the clenched heads of the strap-nails, and the box of the lock with its keyhole, and the two pintles, proud of their lead, on his side.

---

**MARCUS**

On the second day Justice Herennia kept Marcus waiting until the afternoon, and he did not mind.

He sat on a bench at the back of her court among the fishwives and the coopers' wives and a carter who had come up about a horse, under the high windows of the Hall of Pleas, with Felix at his elbow sweating gently into his good tunic, and watched her work. The court was a long, plain, whitewashed room with benches down it and the crown's scales cut again over the far end in the stone, and it smelled of hot wool and the fishwives' baskets. Under the scales, on a dais no higher than a step, behind a table with nothing on it but a book, an inkpot and a water-clock, sat a narrow old woman in a black gown gone green at the seams, as upright as a lance.

He had known her name since he was a boy. Everybody on the Old Hill knew it, as they knew the names of storms. She had hanged a lord's son for a stabbing on the Lesser Hall stair in the same week, it was said, that she acquitted a fishwife who had laid a man's head open with a gaff. Varro had once called her, at his father's table, "that pike in a gown", and been looked at for it.

She took the morning's matters one by one. A carter's horse, sold lame. A widow's roof, and whose wall it stood on. A boy who had stolen a cheese and eaten it and was sorry, and was given to his mother, who was sorrier. She wore spectacles of Vessan glass, round, with thin horn rims, and she did not wear them to read: she read the book with them pushed up on her forehead. She put them on to look at whoever was standing in front of her, and looked, and said nothing; and Marcus watched three liars, one after another, fall to pieces under it like wet bread.

At the sixth hour she shut the book. The court rose. A clerk came down between the benches to Marcus with a slate.

"The justice will see His Highness now," he said. "Only His Highness."

"Strictly—" Felix began.

"Only His Highness," said the clerk.

---

Her room was behind the court. It was smaller than his writing room at Cyrene, and full of books from the floor to the beams: law books, the crown's rolls in their pigeonholes, the judgments of the Hall of Pleas bound in calf by the decade with the years stamped in gold on their spines, the oldest so dark with handling that the gold was gone. It smelled of calf and dust. There was one window, and a table under it with a book open on a slanted desk, and a chair. She did not offer him the chair. She stood behind the table with her hands on its edge and looked at him through the spectacles.

He had come up the hill with a speech. It had been a good one, at the foot of the hill.

"Highness," she said.

"Justice."

"You've come about the healer."

"I have."

"Then I'll tell you first what you've not come for, so neither of us has the trouble of it." Her voice was dry and low and carried no further than she meant it to. "You've not come to ask me to let her go. I can't, and you know I can't, and you'd not respect me if I did. And you've not come to tell me she's a good woman, because I know that, the whole Fishmarket knows it, and it's nothing to do with the charge. So what have you come for?"

Marcus let go of the speech.

"Who has the right to try those deaths?" he said.

Herennia was silent for a moment. Then she took the spectacles off, and laid them folded on the open book, and sat down in her chair, and looked up at him with her naked eyes. They were pale and a little watery, and as sharp as an awl.

"Ask the rest of it," she said.

"The deaths themselves. Not her. The three men." He took it slowly. "Statius drowned at the water-gate stair. Pansa fell from a ladder at the hull staging. Atilius died on the black beach, across the fire. At Cyrene, in my prefecture, in its first month, under my writ. They were prisoners I had spared, on my word, to work my slips. And they had done a thing at Cyrene, all three, before I came, that was never tried." He stopped. "There was never a court for it. There was no court in Cyrene. There was a garrison, and then there was me."

"And you want to know whether a court can try a man who is dead."

"Yes."

Herennia sat back. She looked past him at the shelves, at the dark old spines where the gold was gone; and when she spoke again it was in the voice of a woman reading a thing into a record.

"In my first year on this bench," she said, "there was a wrecker on the outer reefs, which are the crown's own shore and this court's, who had lit false fires on his headland for half his life and drowned the crews that came to them. He was drowned himself in his own surf a month before the assize, going down to the wreck of the *Gannet* to strip her. His village thought that was an end of it. The crown did not. This court tried him, dead, on the information of the *Gannet*'s owners, with his body unburied in the crypt below us and his widow on the front bench. It found him guilty of every death. It took his boats and his land for the families. And it hanged his name on the harbor gate, on a board, where it stayed until the board rotted. I was the junior. I wrote the judgment. It's in that book there, the third from the end on the second shelf, if you want to see the hand of a very young woman who thought she was doing something tremendous."

"Was she?"

"She was doing something lawful," said Herennia. "Which is better, and rarer. A court can try the dead, Highness. Not to punish them; they're past it. To say what they did, and what it was in law, so that the living know where they stand. The court of the place where the thing was done. It has always been so. It's been done three times since, in my years, and I've read every one of them into this book."

"And if such a court tried those three men," said Marcus, "and found them guilty, and sentenced them—"

"Then I would read its judgment into my record," said Herennia, "when this court sits on the charge, which is at the end of the summer assize, on the first day after the harvest bell. And I would weigh it. What I would do then is a matter for that day and this court, and not for a prince in my room in the afternoon. I'll not tell you what to do, Highness. I'll not tell you what a court should find. I've told you what a court can do. You asked."

Through the window behind her he could see the roofs of the Old Hill going down to the canals in the heat, and the steam on the canals, and the masts in the harbor, and past the masts the sea.

"The court of the place," he said. "Cyrene."

"Cyrene."

He could see that too. His own hall in the fort, his own men on the benches, three dead Tharsians on trial for what they had done to his healer, and her prince in the chair. The harbor would know what that was worth before the tide turned.

"Does a prefect's court have to sit in the prefect's hall?"

"A prefect's court sits where the prefect sits, Highness," said Herennia, "if he sits for the place, under his writ, in its name. The law cares about the chair, not the floor."

"Then I'll carry the chair to Silverwood." He said it slowly, and the rest came after it. "There's a council owed there. The elves, the halls, the clans and us. I'll call it for the end of summer, and sit as Prefect of Cyrene in the open, and try the three of them in front of all of it. Not in a corner of my own fort."

Herennia turned the open book a little on its desk, toward the light.

"Bring me your judgment written fair and sealed, with the names of those who heard it, before the harvest bell," she said. "If it's lawful, I'll know. If it's not, I'll know that too, and I'll say so in this room to your face, and in that court to the city's."

"I'd expect nothing else."

"No," said Herennia. "Your father wouldn't either. He's never once sent to me about a case. Not in thirty years. Not about the lord's son, and the lord sat at his council." She laid her hand on the open page. "He could pardon her by sundown, you know. He's only to write it."

"I know."

She put the spectacles on.

"Then why are you here," she said, "and not up his stair?"

"Because a pardon says she did a crime," said Marcus. "And I'm not going to let anybody write that down. Not my father. Not you. Not her."

Herennia looked at him through the round glass a moment longer.

"Good day, Highness," she said. "Mind the step on the way out. It's lower than it looks. Varro falls down it about once a year."

---

He went down the hill in the heat with Felix at his elbow, saying nothing, all the way to the Corvus lodging-house at the foot of the Fishmarket Steps, and climbed its stair to the room under the roof. Alana was sitting by the window with the weather-book open on her knee and a pen in her hand that she was not using.

She looked at his face.

"Sit down," she said, "before you tell me. You've been sitting on a bench all day, and you look as if you'd been standing."

He sat on the end of her bed, among her books, and told her.

She listened to all of it without speaking. Once, at the board on the harbor gate, her lips moved over something small, and he thought she was working out the year. When he had done she was quiet, looking out of the window at the masts.

"Silverwood," she said.

"The end of summer. All of them. Lyrielle and the elders. Kolbrand, if he'll come, and he'll come if it's about who pays, and it is. The clans, if Thalassa can find Sszarra and bring them up out of the sea to a table. The Vigil, if the Abbess will send anyone. She's sent Domitia back to us, so she'll have it written down whether she sends or not. And my father, so he hears it from me." He was quiet a moment. "We need it, Alana. We needed it the day we came off the road from Bastion. We lost there, and they should hear it to their faces from the man who lost it, and then be asked what they want to do next."

"And the court."

"And the court. Tell me if it's wrong."

"It's not wrong." She was writing now. Not in the weather-book: she had a sheet of good paper on the board across her knees and was ruling it with the side of her pen, quick and small, a margin and a heading. "It's late. That's all. The elves will want two months' notice. Kolbrand will want two kegs of the good ale before he sits." She glanced up. "You'll want letters. Fair copies, sealed, one to each, by the fastest ships there are. Tonight."

"Tonight."

"Tell me what they say," said Alana, "and I'll make them look as if they'd been thought about for a month."

He told her. She wrote. Felix went down and came back with a supper from his mother's step, an eel on a board and barley bread and a jug, and the smell of the eel filled the little room, hot and fatty and smoked. Alana ate with one hand and wrote with the other. After two hours of it her right hand shook on the pen, and she changed to her left for the superscriptions, and wrote those badly, and said so. By midnight there were five letters on the board, folded and sealed in his own wax with the dolphin, and she was sitting back against the wall with the board on her knees and her eyes closed.

"You should have been a clerk," he said.

"I'd have been a terrible clerk. I keep putting in adjectives." She opened her eyes. "Marcus."

"Yes."

"The Steps say Gaius sat all day on the step at the Hall's side door, with Sennius on the step below him. They write his name on a slate and rub it off."

"I passed him going in."

"Take him with you to Silverwood," said Alana. "Make him come. Give him something to add up."

"She'll tell him that herself, if they let him up."

"You tell him too," said Alana. "He'll do it for you before he does it for her. He thinks it's less."

---

The letters went out the next day in the fastest hulls in the harbor. On the morning after, the *Patience* and *Constancy* sailed for Cyrene together on the ebb, with the cohort and the sawyers and Domitia, and Avitus at his helm with his cup and a new cold. Nobody on the galley quay cheered them out, because nobody had been told the hour. Gemella was on her step. She lifted her hand once as *Constancy* passed the end of the Fishmarket, and went back to her eels.

The wind stood in the south the whole way down, and they rowed into it. They came in under Cyrene's fort at dusk on the third day out, into the smell of pitch and the spring's warm water. Roderic met them on the long quay in his good coat, with his beard combed, and listened to the whole of it standing up, from the knock to the harvest bell. At the end of it he said only, "A court of the place." He looked down at his coat. "Then I'll want the other coat."

Hostus was waiting at the foot of the *Patience*'s plank, with his bag in one hand and nothing ready to say.

Marcus was close enough to hear it, and it was no business of his. Domitia came down the plank with her spear and her steel cap, and Ear behind her with her bag, and she stopped short of the bottom, a little above the barber, and looked at him from under the cap.

"Barber," she said. "Hands."

He held them out, palms up. She looked at them, both sides.

"Good," said Domitia.

"Sister." His right hand went up to the back of his neck, and stayed there. "You've been away."

"I was sent to the army," said Domitia. "So I've come back to it."

She went past him up the quay toward the Baths. After a moment Hostus followed her with his bag, a pace behind, and the cohort on the quay watched them go with a great and careful lack of interest. Somewhere among them Fingers said something low, and three men laughed, and Rufo cuffed the nearest of them without looking.

There were three days to do it all in, and they did it in three. The fleet came off the slips and out of the berths. The *Forward* took on water from the spring, salt pork from the fort's stores and the bakehouse's bread in sacks. Sylara heard about the council from Rufo, and thought about it for the length of a supper, and said the forest would come. "They'll say they won't, first." The Navarch put her answer in his book.

---

He found Gaius on the third night in the shed, at the bench under the lamp. The drawing of the jack was still pinned over it, and the corner of the board where the door had been drawn was sawn off square.

He was not working. He had the fleet's stores book open in front of him and his slate beside it, and he was adding a column of salt pork that Marcus had watched him add that morning on the quay.

"Kolbrand will ask who pays," Marcus said.

"He'll be right to." Gaius did not look up. "Nobody's paid for Bastion yet. Not the ale, not the bolts, not the hulls. Somebody will want to know what it came to."

"Then somebody will have to tell him. Every page. At the table, in front of him, so he can watch it add."

The chalk stopped.

"Alana could do it," said Marcus.

"Alana copies it fair. I'd want it right." Gaius set the chalk down. He took his lenses off and held them up to the lamp, and put them back on without doing anything to them. "You don't need me at Silverwood. You need a ledger."

"I need the man who can say what's in it without looking."

Gaius looked up at him then. His eyebrows were coming in, short and pale and stiff, and they went up a little, as far as they could yet.

"Somebody told you to say that."

"Alana did. And Liora told you herself, I expect, and you've added the salt pork twice since." Marcus took the stores book off the bench and shut it and put it under his arm. "I'm telling you because I want it. I want you at that table with every page. Not for her. For me."

Out in the yard the night watch passed along the staging with a lantern, and the light crossed the shed door and was gone.

"Every page," said Gaius.

"Every page."

"I'll need the Navarch's book. Not his fair copy; he leaves things out of the fair copy." He held out his hand. "And give me that back. I'm not done."

Marcus gave it back.

---

The fleet sailed on the fourth morning, on the ebb, with the wind fair from the north-east and the Teeth white along the western sea.

*Constancy* led. Behind her came the *Forward* and the *Patience* and the cohort's three galleys, and the transports, and the merchantmen of Cyrene that had been asked and had come; and Cyrene came down to the long quay to see them go. Prisca was at her window over the harbor, and Fausta at the bakehouse door with flour to the elbow, and the home guard was drawn up under Tuccius in a ragged line that the old drill-master had given up straightening. The children ran out along the mole-head to the last stone and stood there in a row. The harbor bell rang them out, and the smell of the slip-yard's pitch came out after them on the wind. Far off to the south the Grey stood low and white along the edge of the world, where it had always been.

Marcus stood at *Constancy*'s rail with Nerva behind him, whose eyes went aft to the helm every time the helmsman breathed. The island sank astern slowly, as islands do: the quay and the slips first, then the sheds and the roofs of the town, then the fort, until there was only the hill with the spring on it and its steam leaning away south-west in the wind.

He was at the starboard quarter. From there he could see the *Patience* on her station a cable astern and to the north of them, with her men at their oars in the long slow stroke of a ship with a day's work in front of her; and he could see Gaius.

The engineer was at her starboard rail, abaft the mast. The men round him were looking back at Cyrene, or forward into the west. Gaius was looking north.

There was nothing to the north. It was open sea, hard and blue under the morning, with no sail on it and no land, all the way to the edge of the sky; and somewhere past that, two days and a night away, a long grey hill with tombs on it, and a hall on the hill's shoulder.

Gaius stood at the rail with his hands on it and the wind pressing his coat against him; and the *Patience* bore him west with the rest of them, stroke by stroke, and all the way out of sight of Cyrene he kept his face to the door.

---

# Chapter Seven: Royal Blood

**DAMIEN**

The ship's cook had done eels.

He brought them aft himself at the change of the watch, in a covered dish on a board, with the end of a loaf and a jug of the Vessan red that was the ship's cargo. He lifted the cover with a flourish that had been meant for a lord. They were stewed in their own fat with leeks and a little vinegar, in short thick lengths, the skin gone soft and blue-black and shining. The smell filled the cabin to the beams.

"Take them away," said Damien. "I'll have the bread."

The cook took them away. Damien heard him on the ladder, saying something to the boy at its head about lords, and the boy laughing.

He broke the bread, and left it on the board.

The *Good Year* had come round the black spit with the last of the light, under one sail, with her master at the helm himself because he did not know the water, and let go her anchor in the mouth of the western bay. As she swung to her cable the stern window came round and gave Damien the bay entire. The prince's fleet lay across it at anchor in two lines: galleys and transports, and the big new ship his uncle had named, *Constancy*, with her lanterns already lit along her rail. Under the spit three black hulls lay drawn up on the shingle, with fires going among them. Above the beach the grass went up in a long green shoulder to the trees.

At the head of the bay, above the trees, there were more trees.

They stood over the rest of the wood as a high table stands over a hall, five or six of them, high on their rise. The light still lay on their crowns when the bay below had gone to shadow. Their boughs had grown into one another over more years than anybody had counted, and made one roof, silver above where the sun was and black beneath. Under it there were lamps already, small and pale and too many to count, like the windows of a great house seen from its gate at dusk.

*A high table, then.*

The treaty-boughs. Neutral ground, the letters said: where the crown had signed its boundaries with the wood once, in another king's reign, and had not been back.

He had drawn it nine times.

The case lay open on the bunk beside him: black calfskin worn pale at the corners, made for him at twelve by the chancery's bookbinder, with the charts in its long pocket. He took them out and laid them on the table by the bread. Nine seatings for a council he had never sat at, of peoples he had met at a feast or not at all, under trees he had first set eyes on an hour ago. A long table. A round table, for the look of it, though nobody who could help it ever sat at a round one. Two tables facing, as at a treaty, with the crown between. Three tables, with the dwarves where they could not see the elves eat. The dwarves at the head and the elves below the salt. The elves at the head and the dwarves by the door. The lizard-people, about whom he knew only that they did not blink, in each place in turn, and in none.

He knew where everyone ought to sit. He always had. It was a gift, like an ear for music, and about as much regarded. Give him a list of names, and a knowledge of who had borrowed from whom and whose grandmother had been jilted at whose wedding, and he could say where in a hall a man would be content. He could say, too, where the same man would sit all night with his meat going cold and his face going red, and remember it for a year. The best of his charts were dinners at which nothing happened. Nobody thanked the chart.

And it worked the other way.

He took out his bone folder and ran it down the fold of the round table, which had come a little open, and set it flat again.

He could set it out like a sum.

The king had stopped eating the suppers his nephew sent up. That was the winter's. Since the spring he had stopped saying yes to him as well, and he did it so kindly, so absently, with the old hand laid a moment on Damien's sleeve, that the court had not noticed yet. It would. Damien had asked if he might carry the pardon down the Steps to the cohort's lodging himself, as a cousin, and his uncle had said that a crier would do it. He had asked, on an evening of his own choosing, whether the drawer might be cleared at last of the paper in it, since it was not to be used. His uncle had said that it cost nothing, in a drawer. That had been Damien's own word, years ago. His uncle had smiled as he gave it back, and Damien had not known what the smile meant. Last of all he had asked whether the crown would not send someone of the blood to his cousin's council, with a commission under the seal, so that the four peoples might see the house of Aeridor sit down with them, and not its younger son only. His uncle had glanced up from the shells on the windowsill and said, *My son called it. Ask him.*

He had not asked him. He had hired the *Good Year* out of his own purse, and her master's silence with it, and come.

The other side lay across the water. The man there had always weighed what came to him in the oilskin and paid by the weight, and since the southern road there had been little to weigh. The letters from the south had stopped. Not thinned: stopped, between one ship and the next, as talk stops at a table when the wrong man sits down; and Damien did not know why, and there was nobody he could ask. He had gone on sending what the capital gave him. The pardon. The girl from the order cast out, and charged. That had pleased him in its way, since the paper the Marshal had carried in his sleeve all summer was in Damien's own hand, and the Marshal had thanked him for it. A wedding talked of. Gossip. Gossip was what a court had instead of news, and the man across the water knew the difference, and had paid accordingly. The last purse had come up the hill lighter by a third. There had been no note. There never was.

And at the head of the bay, under the silver roof, there was a council that would make his cousin more than a prince.

He did not grudge Marcus. He had told himself so often enough to know it was true. Everyone was fond of a big untidy dog that knocked things off tables. At Midwinter he had watched the boy go down the Lesser Hall to the lowest table and leave Damien's whole seating standing behind him. He had admired it, because it was well done, and he could admire a thing well done even at his own expense. But a prince who had lost a battle was only a prince. A man whom four peoples had signed under, the elves and the dwarves and the fen and the crown, would be something else: a man who did not need a cousin to tell him where to sit. The court would love him for it, and be right to. And the king, who had stopped eating his nephew's suppers, would stop needing a nephew at all. The old man was thinner every month. After the old man there would be the son.

If the council failed, his cousin was the runner again, a beaten prince, and the court would remember what it used to call him. The king would want somebody near him who had never argued.

He did not want anyone dead. He thought it worth saying to himself plainly. Councils did not need killing. They died on their own, of precedence and weather and bad meat, of the wrong man served first and the right man kept waiting, of one word said at the one table where it would be remembered. A council was a seating chart, and there was no chart in the world that could not be made to go wrong by a man who knew how it went right.

He had brought a household of eight. Two clerks who wrote a clean chancery hand and had never once asked what they copied; a valet; a groom, though there would be no horses; four others. Most of them had been with him for years, and the groom since the spring, on a letter. They were good at being in a room.

He looked at the nine charts on the table, and the bread beside them, and folded eight of the charts and put them back in the case.

The ninth he kept out. It was the one with three tables.

Through the stern window the bay had gone grey, and the lamps under the treaty-boughs had come up brighter against it. Somewhere down the ship the cook was eating the eels himself. Damien could smell the vinegar.

He had passed Albia in the spring.

He had not meant to be on the Fishmarket Steps at all. He had gone down early to the galley quay about a ship, in a plain cloak, by the steepest way because it was the quickest. Two-thirds of the way down, where the Steps turned under the cooper's wall and the eel-women kept their boards along the landing, she had been sitting on a stool behind a board of her own. A short woman in a brown shawl, with a sacking apron over her good skirt and a tub at her feet. She had a knife in one hand and an eel in the other, and she was taking its head off, neatly, with one cut, as she had taken the heads off a thousand capons for his uncle's table. On the step below, a girl gutting into a bucket talked at her the whole time, and Albia did not answer.

She had looked up. He was sure of that. She had looked up from the eel at the plain cloak going by, and at the face over it, and down again, and taken the next head off.

He had had a ship to see. He had seen it. He had come back up the hill the long way, by the Street of Lamps, which took a quarter of an hour longer.

He put the ninth chart in the breast of his coat, where it lay flat, and shut the case. Then he put on his good cloak, the dark blue with the plain silver clasp, and went up on deck to look at the lamps. He would go ashore late in the morning, when they were all gathered and nobody had yet sat down. An early guest was only early.

---

**MARCUS**
