The Hold-King of Stoneheart stood at the edge of the treaty-boughs with his crown under his arm and his head tipped back.

Marcus had seen him from the beach at first light, coming up from the cohort's lines with Felix and the water jar: a short broad shape in steel and gold at the top of the green slope, where the grass ended and the shadow of the great trees began. His back was to the trees. His crimson cloak hung straight down behind him to the grass. The rings in his beard caught the light off the bay, and so did the newest of his scars, a pink seam across the brow. He was looking up past the eaves into the open: a high thin blue going white at the rim, a pair of gulls, the morning.

"Has he been there all night?" said Marcus.

"Strictly, since the third hour of the night," said Felix. "The queen sat up with him till dawn and then went to bed. She said he'd come in when he'd seen enough of it."

The dwarves had come in the evening before, in three broad black ore-boats from the north-west with the halls' marks cut in their stem-posts, rowed short and hard and not for show. They beached at the north end of the bay under the spit and pitched their camp there on the shingle, not on the grass, as far from the trees as the beach allowed. Brokk was in the first boat with his hands hooked in his belt and a face like the end of a long argument. The queen was in the second. The king came in the third, standing in its stern in his plate with the crown on his head, the first Hold-King to leave the halls in four generations, and he did not wait for the boat to be run up. He stepped down into the surf and waded up the shingle streaming, and lifted his head to the sky.

He had not lowered it since, as far as anybody could tell. Marcus left him to it.

---

The treaty-boughs stood at the head of the western bay, where the crown's fifth ended and the wood began, and they were the largest living things Marcus had ever stood under.

There were six of them. They were the island's own trees, grey in the trunk and silver in the leaf like everything at Silverwood. But where the forest round them stood bare as masts to a height no mast reached, these had been let grow out instead of up, for longer than anybody's history went. Their great lower limbs had reached across the gaps between them and met, and grafted, and gone on, until the six were one tree on six feet. Under them lay a floor of short turf as wide as the Lesser Hall, and the roof stood three times as high as the Hall's. The light came down through the roof green and silver and moving, and the leaves turned over in the wind off the bay, all of them at once, silver and then green; the sound of it was one long slow breath that never ended.

In the middle of the turf lay the council stone: one slab of the green-gold stone that Valeria, the first time she saw a shard of it, had called crown stone. It was as long as a galley's mast and as wide as a man is tall, set flat on three low blocks, and its top had been worn as smooth as a table by weather or hands or both. There were no chairs. The elves sat on the turf, or did not sit. The crown, the last time, had brought its own chairs and taken them home again.

This time there were trestles, and Gaius had built them.

He had brought the timber from Cyrene on the *Patience*'s deck and put it up in three days with the sawyers: the tables under the roof, and a row of four open-fronted sheds along its north edge for the paper and the stores. The elves had watched him do it from among the trunks in a long grey silence, which Gaius had taken for interest and answered with explanations. There were three long tables of pale planed pine, with benches, set so far apart that a man at one could not hear what was said at the next unless it was shouted. At the end of each Gaius had made a little stand for papers, with a lip on it so they would not blow.

He was at the dwarves' table now, at the north end, with his coat off and his lenses on, laying the Bastion ledgers out along it in order. He had brought every page, and the Navarch's own book besides, in its oilskin at the head of the row.

"You'll want the barley at that end," said Marcus.

"I want the barley where it comes in the year." Gaius moved a ledger an inch. "He'll read it in order or he won't read it."

"He might not read it at all."

"Then I'll read it to him." Gaius looked up over his lenses, and the short new brows went up. "That was the point, I thought."

"It was."

"Then go away," said Gaius, "and let me lay it out."

Marcus went along the stone to the scribes' trestle under the eaves, where Pen sat on an upturned crate with a penknife and a bundle of goose quills, cutting pens for the council's record. Pen had offered himself for the record the day he heard there would be one. He had ruled two quires of the good paper already, so close and true that Alana had come to look at it and gone away again without a word, which from Alana was praise. He lifted the knife to Marcus without stopping.

"Every one with a slit, Highness. No blots at Silverwood."

"See there aren't."

"I've cut enough for a war." He held a quill up to the green light and squinted along it. "Pity it's only a council."

---

The wood came in under its own trees as if it had not been away an hour.

Marcus did not see the elves arrive. There was no column out of the forest, no horn, no stir at the edge of the trees that the eye could catch and say, *there*. Only, at some moment of the morning, when the sun was high enough to come through the boughs in spots on the turf, they were there. Lyrielle stood at the south end of the council stone with her bow unstrung across her back and her braid brought forward over one shoulder, silver again from end to end. Behind her stood the five elders of the council roots in a row, with Vael-Anar in the middle of them, ancient and narrow, his hands folded in his sleeves. Behind the elders stood the wardens of the roots. And out past the edge of the roof, where the shadow went grey and then green and then nothing, Marcus could feel rather than see the hundred: Faelar's bows, called out again for this, keeping the council grounds from a bowshot back among the trunks.

Faelar himself came out to the roof's edge, clear of the elders. He looked down the green slope at the dwarves' camp on the shingle and the cohort's lines above the beach, and at the bay with its ships, and along the eaves at the Hold-King with his face to the sky. Then he looked further down, at a small figure toiling up from the shingle with something on her back.

"Four peoples," Faelar said. "And one dwarf with a book."

The dwarf with the book was the keeper. She came up the slope alone, old beyond guessing and small even for a dwarf, in a black kilt and a grey shawl, with the halls' book on her back in a case of tooled leather that came down to the backs of her knees. She took the slope at her own pace, her knotted measuring-cord looped at her belt and its bronze beads clicking. Under the boughs she stopped and tipped her head back at the roof, as the king had at the sky, but only for a moment. Then she went to the dwarves' table, where Gaius's ledgers lay in their row, and stood at its end with her hands behind her back, and began to read the first one where it lay.

The fen had come in the night.

They had come round the north of the island in the cold water with the merfolk, as they had come to Bastion. But this time Thalassa had brought reed boats, a dozen of them, long and low and lashed at the bow, and the merfolk had towed them in with the clans lying in them out of the wind. Now the boats lay drawn up on the wet sand in the bay's south corner, in the shade of the trees. The clans stood about them in the sun, grey-green and brown-green and perfectly still, and not one of them had come up the slope. Thalassa sat on the gunwale of a boat with her feet trailing in the shallows and Kymon beside her, eating something out of a shell.

Sszarra was standing alone on the black spit, at its far end, where the rock went down into deep water.

Marcus went down to her. It was a long walk, across the beach and out along the rock past the glassy black scar where the flasks had burned. She watched him come the whole way without once moving: gold eyes with the upright black bar in each, taller than he was and narrower, her bone-handled knife at her harness and the sea moving behind her.

"One who asks," she said.

"Speaker. You came."

"You sent." She looked him over. "You sent late. The letter said the end of summer. The end of summer is the time of year in which the young are taught to swim. Every clan that is here has left its young to be taught by someone else." She closed one eye, slowly, and opened it again. Marcus had still not learned which of those were jokes. "I am telling you so that you know what it cost."

"I know."

"You know now," said Sszarra. She looked past him at the great trees on the hill. "The elves want us under their roof. It is very dry."

"There's a table."

"Yes. I saw it from the water. It has legs." She turned her head all at once, as the clans did, from the trees to the dwarves' fires on the shingle and back. "When it is time to ask," she said, "send to the water. We will come up."

---

A Vessan wine-ship lay in the bay's mouth. She had anchored at dusk and landed nobody all night; Felix had reported her at breakfast, and Marcus had thought no more about it. A little before noon, Damien came up from her.

Marcus saw the household first: eight people in dark good wool, coming up the slope from the beach in a file with chests and a hamper and a long black chest carried between two of them. The cohort on the grass stopped whatever it was doing to watch them come. Then he saw the man in front of them, bareheaded, in a cloak the blue of a summer midnight, who paused at the bottom of the slope to say something to a dwarf and make the dwarf laugh, and came on.

He would be smiling already. Damien always smiled from a long way off. It gave a man time to smile back.

"Cousin," said Damien, and took Marcus by both arms and held him off to look at him, as an uncle does. He smelled of clean linen and orris-root and, faintly, under them, of a ship. "You look well. Thinner. You've been at sea, and it suits you. The Grey suits you, they tell me, which nobody else can say." He let go and turned, and took in the council grounds at one look: the silver roof, the great stone, the trestles, the elves at the stone's south end and the dwarves' table at the north, and the king at the roof's edge. "Well. Look at it. Look what you've called."

"What are you doing here, Damien?"

"Coming, I hope, to be useful." He said it simply, and without offence. "A table like this needs royal blood at it. Not to do anything; you'll do everything, you always do. Just to be seen sitting. The elves will want to see the house of Aeridor, not only its younger son, and the dwarves will want to know the crown takes them seriously enough to send the blood. For the look of the thing." He spread his hands. "I'm the look of the thing. It's all I've ever been good for."

Behind Marcus, at the edge of the trees, a voice said under its breath, carefully, in an accent like water over stones: *for the look of the thing.* He turned his head. It was one of Sylara's wardens, the small one with her braid tucked into her collar, standing a little behind Ilvane with her face perfectly serious, trying the words over to see how they went.

Damien had heard it too. He bowed to her, slightly, and smiled, and she looked back at him without a flicker.

"Did my father send you?" said Marcus.

"Your father," said Damien, "said you'd called it, and I was to ask you." He tilted his head. "So I'm asking."

"Three years of war," said Marcus. "Not one word to the army in all of it. And you find us the week we're wounded."

He had meant to say something else. He heard how it sounded under the trees, and let it stand.

Damien bowed his head a little, as to a fair touch in the practice yard. "Yes. That's just. I'll own that." He lifted it. "It's the week I could be some use. In a good week you'd not want me. Nobody wants a seating chart at a victory."

Sylara had come up at Marcus's side without a sound, as she came everywhere. She had her hood down and her bow strung and her bark-leaf book open in her other hand, and she was looking at Damien over the top of it with her green eyes level.

"He'd know," she said.

It was not loud. Behind her Rufo's hand found the plait above his ear.

Damien turned his smile on her. It was a good smile, warm and a little rueful, and it did not change at all. "Lady warden. You'll be putting me in your report."

"Page four," said Sylara, and wrote something, and shut the book.

Damien laughed. He had a pleasant laugh, and he let it go on for exactly as long as it was pleasant; then he turned back to Marcus with his hand already going to the breast of his coat.

"I've taken a liberty," he said. "You'll forgive me." He brought out a folded sheet and opened it on his palm. It was a chart: three long tables, drawn in a hand so even that it might have been engraved, with every place marked and named, and small notes in the margin in a smaller hand still. "The dwarves here, at the north end, because they'll want to see the sea; I'm told they feel shut in under trees. The elves at the south, by the wood. The fen in the middle, where everybody can see that nobody has been put below anybody. The crown at the stone between, where you can go to any of them. I've put the lady of Corvus by the dwarves; they've a feeling for her. And the queen of Stoneheart—"

"There's no head," said Marcus.

Damien looked up.

"No high table. No crown at the stone." Marcus took the chart off his cousin's palm, gently, folded it again along its creases, and gave it back. "It's three questions. One table each. Whoever has the question sits at it, and whoever can answer it. I'll go where I'm wanted."

For a moment Damien looked at the folded chart in his hand. Then he put it back in his coat, and his hand came out with a bone folder in it, and he drew the folder along the fold of his cuff, which did not need it, and put it away.

"Of course," he said. "Quite right. Three questions." He smiled. "You've learned something at sea, cousin."

"I've learned how long it takes to lay a table for this many with ninety men who think a table's for dicing on." It was Gaius, coming along the stone with a ledger under his arm and his coat still off, on his way to the scribes' trestle, and he did not stop. "I said at Cyrene we'd no servers, and we've still none. Nobody to fetch from the spring. The elves won't carry and the dwarves won't be asked and the clans won't come up the hill. The cohort can pour or it can stand guard, and it can't do both." He stopped at the trestle, where Pen had a quill cut down to the barrel in his long white fingers and was looking at it in some surprise, as if it had done that by itself. "That was a good quill."

"It was," said Pen, and put it aside, and took another from the bundle.

Damien turned to him with real pleasure. "Engineer. Then let me lend you hands." He lifted one of his own toward the household standing on the slope among the chests. "They're good. Most of them have served at the king's table since they were children. They'll pour and fetch and carry, and nobody will see them do it, which is the art of it, and your ninety can stand guard."

Gaius looked at the household, and then at Marcus.

Marcus looked down the slope at the eight in their dark wool, and the chests, and the hamper. Past them lay the cohort's lines and the bay full of ships and boats, the dwarves' tents under the spit and the clans standing in the sun by their reed boats. In an hour he would have four peoples under this roof who had never once sat down together to anything, and not hands enough to put a cup in front of each of them.

"Thank you," he said. "Yes."

"It's nothing," said Damien. "It's what we're for."

---

They came under the roof at noon. It was not a procession. Marcus had asked that it should not be one, and for once he was given what he asked for.

The king came in first, because he was nearest. He came in from the roof's edge with the sun on his back and the crown in the crook of his arm, walked two paces into the shade of the boughs, and stopped, and stood, and let his eyes come to it. The queen was at his shoulder, short and dark beside him, with her heavy plait wound in copper wire and the brass steelyard at her belt. She said something low to him in the dwarf tongue, and he breathed out hard through his nose. Then they went to the north table together, and the halls came in behind them, Brokk and a dozen of the halls' heads in their best kilts with their braids clasped in gold, and stood behind the benches, and did not sit.

The fen came up from the water when Marcus sent down for them. They climbed the slope in a long single file with Sszarra at the head, slowly and all of a piece, as the clans moved on land. The dwarves at the north table turned where they stood to watch them come, and outside on the grass the cohort's talk died away. They came in under the boughs out of the sun. They did not go to the middle table. They went to the stone and stood along its west side in a row, and Sszarra stood in the middle of them and laid her hand on the stone's edge, and closed both eyes, once, at the warmth of it.

The elves were there already.

Marcus stood at the stone's east side, near its north end, with nothing in his hands. Alana stood a pace behind him and to his left, where he could feel her and did not have to look for her. Damien had taken a place at his right, a pace further off than Alana, looking pleasant and doing nothing, which he had done well since he came to court at seven. Felix stood behind Damien with the water jar. Along the edge of the shade under the eaves stood the cohort and the Lanciarii, three and four deep: Roderic with his arms folded, Valeria with the Farrier's butt-spike in the turf beside her, Rufo, Fingers; Hostus with his bag, and Domitia beside him telling him something about his hands.

Sylara was not with them. She had gone to the end of the elves' table nearest the stone, and stood there with her book open and a stick of lead in her fingers. Her four had gone with her, and stood in a row behind her, and not behind the elders. Two of the elders had turned their heads to look at that. Vael-Anar had not.

The turf smelled green where so many feet had stood on it. Somewhere up in the boughs a bird was singing three notes, going up, over and over.

Marcus had thought for three days what he would say. He said very little of it.

"We lost at Bastion." He said it to the stone, and then lifted his head and said it to them. "I lost it. I went south without waiting and without sending, and I came to the gate, and I had it open, and I wouldn't go through it, for a reason I'd give again. Then the line closed and we came away by the southern road, and we left men in the gorse, and the two hundred we went for are still in the pens." He stopped. "I said I'd tell you that to your faces, myself. Not in a letter. That's done.

"On the last day I'll sit here as Prefect of Cyrene, for a thing that was done at Cyrene. You'll hear about it then. It's mine to do, and it isn't yours to carry." The bird went up its three notes. "Before that, I've called you to ask you what you want to do next. Not to tell you. I've done telling." He looked along the stone, at the elves and the fen and the halls. "So ask."

Nobody moved.

On the corner of the slab by Marcus's hand lay a grey beach-stone, smooth and flat and about the size of a loaf, of the kind the sea rolls on the black spit. Pen had brought it up that morning to keep the council's paper from blowing. The king of Stoneheart came round the end of the north table to the slab, reached across, and picked it up.

He weighed it on his palm. He did it slowly, and his blue eyes stayed on Marcus's face while he did it.

"Who pays for Bastion," said Kolbrand, "heir?"

He asked it no louder than a man asks a price. Under that roof it carried to every table.

Without looking round, he held out his other hand, palm up, and the keeper put the halls' book into it.

It was the book Marcus had signed in the clans' hall at Stoneheart: black hide over oak, bronze at the corners, as long as a child. The king took its weight on one forearm as a clerk takes a ledger, laid it on the council stone, turned it over, and opened it from the back, where the halls keep what is owed them. Those leaves were dark and soft with handling. He turned them past the crown's old line, *The crown of Aeridor. Two winters' paper. Owed.*, with Marcus's promise written under it and his own stroke joining the two, which Marcus could have put a finger on with his eyes shut. He turned them past a line Marcus did not know, in the dwarf marks, with a name under it that he did. He stopped where the written leaves gave out, at the newest ink in the book.

He swung the book about on the stone so that it faced away from him, down the slab, toward the fen and the elves and the man at its corner.

The line was short. It was in the common letters, not the dwarf marks, in a big hand that had cut them into the hide more than written them: a line for any reader under that roof, and not for the halls alone.

*The heir. Forty days. Not sent.*

Marcus felt the old scar pull tight along his jaw.

He had made the promise on the shingle of the cove below the halls' gate, at the last stack of iron, with the whole cove listening. The king had weighed a pig of iron on his palm before he asked it. *Heir. When you go against the Survey's iron, send to me first. It's forty days to bring the catapults down to the sea. Not thirty.* And he had said, *I'll send first. I promise it.* Valeria had stood at the map table and reminded him of it in front of everybody, and he had said the king would have the pens to weigh against it.

Kolbrand said nothing else. He set the grey stone down on the open book, on the leaf beside the line, to keep the page from turning.

At the south end Lyrielle had not moved. But one of the four had left his place behind Sylara: a broad young elf with a scar through one eyebrow and an unstrung bow in his hand. He crossed the turf to Lyrielle's shoulder in front of the whole wood and stood there. His fingertip ran down a notch cut in the bow's grip, slowly, from top to bottom. Then he bent and said something into her ear that did not carry, and went back across the turf to his place behind Sylara. Every elder watched him go, and come back.

Lyrielle lifted her head.

"Who commands the next Bastion, human?" she said. "We lend bows by the song. The song is sung open when the danger is past, and the bows go home, and the next danger finds them at home. That is the treaty, and it is a good treaty for a wood. It is a bad one for a war." Her voice never rose. "The next time you go against iron, who gives the word? You? A dwarf? A woman with a spear? Whoever is nearest? Bastion was given its word by a man in a hurry, and the wood was not there to say no to him, because the wood had gone home." She looked at him. "We would like to know whose word we are lending our bows to. Before we lend them."

Behind her Vael-Anar's hands had not moved in his sleeves.

Then the fen.

Sszarra took her hand off the stone's edge. She did not step forward; the clans did not step forward. She simply spoke.

"When the war ends," she said, "what then?"

She let it lie on the stone a moment.

"The clans have been at the end of a war before. Not this one. Others. Men come into the fen with a war, and ask, and are given, and the war ends, and then it is somebody else who comes. Somebody who did not ask. Who does not know the channels. Who has a paper that says the fen was the crown's all along." The gold eyes went along the stone, from the king's book to the elves to Damien in his blue cloak, and rested on none of them. "You have given us a border with a name. It is a good name. When you have won, and you are a king, and your sons are kings, who keeps the name? Tell us what is done with the ones who lost. We were the ones who lost, once. And the dead you are fighting were the ones who lost, once, at Corvus." She closed one eye at him, slowly. "What then, one who asks?"

Behind his left shoulder he heard Alana draw breath, lightly, as she did before she gave a figure.

He did not look round.

---

"I'll answer the king first," said Marcus, "because his is owed."

He came the two steps along the stone to the book, and set his hands on the slab, one on either side of it. The crown stone was warm under them, as if somebody had just got up from it.

"The crown pays its own dead. That's Tappo, and Bruttius and Trebonius and Egnatius and Sosius, who carried our healer down the slope to the General and died doing it. That's three of the Lanciarii on the bank. That's the men of the rearguard who went back into the gorse for the ones who were down, and haven't come back. Their families have their pay from the crown till the war ends, and after it, and that's written already, in the crown's book, in my own hand. That's not what you're asking."

He looked down at the line on the leaf, beside the grey stone.

"You're asking who pays for the promise. I made it at your gate. I made it in front of your halls. And I broke it, because I thought the pens wouldn't keep, and I may have been right about the pens, and I was wrong to break it, and those are both true. You can't be paid back for a broken promise. There's no coin for it. It's only paid one way."

He lifted his eyes to the king.

"Next time I go against the Survey's iron, I send to you first. Forty days. And I wait the forty days, if it costs me, and when it costs me. That's the payment. You'll know it's paid when you see it paid. Not before."

The king looked at him. Then he reached out and lifted the grey stone off the leaf again, and weighed it, and the page lay open without it.

"It's written," said Kolbrand. "It's not struck."

He kept the stone.

"That's fair," said Marcus.

"I didn't ask if it was fair, heir." But at the north table the queen had shoved her sleeves to the elbow, and was looking at her husband as Marcus had seen her look at a sack of the crown's barley that came out true on the beam.

Marcus turned to the elves.

"Lady. I'll answer you. Not today. You asked whose word you're lending your bows to, and I don't know yet, and I won't tell you something I don't know just so it's been said under this roof." He met Lyrielle's eyes. "But I'll give you the honest count of what Bastion was, from somebody who was on the bank and isn't me. Then you'll know what you're asking about, and so will I." He turned his head. "Sylara."

Sylara looked up from her book.

"You saw it from the bank," he said. "Who held and who didn't, and what I did, and what I didn't. Give the wood the honest count. All of it. Whatever it does to me."

Sylara studied him with her level green eyes.

"I was going to anyway," she said.

"I know. Now it's asked."

Under the eaves Rufo coughed into his fist, which fooled nobody.

"Gaius." Marcus did not turn round for him. "The halls want to know what Bastion cost. Not the promise: the rest of it. The barley and the bolts and the hulls and the ale, what was spent and what was lost and what was wasted, and what's owed. Every page."

At the north table Gaius laid his hand on the ledgers in front of him.

And the fen.

He turned to Sszarra with a name ready, and it was the wrong name. He had given that question to Liora in his head on the first day out of Cyrene, before he remembered, and had gone on giving it to her all the way west. She was half the isles away, in a whitewashed room under the roof of the Hall of Pleas, with a plum on the sill and a door locked from the passage, waiting on a harvest bell. She would have known what to say to the fen about the ones who lost. She had killed three of them. She had healed more of them than anybody alive.

He looked along the eaves, at the people standing in the shade, until he came to the one he wanted. She stood a little apart from the cohort, as she always did, in borrowed grey wool, with her copper braid pinned up and no pin of her own in it, Alana's books under her arm and a square of linen in her cuff. The cohort's men left a space clear round her without seeming to. It had been a full pace in the spring. It was less now.

"Rusca," said Marcus.

Every head under the roof that knew the name turned. A good many that did not turned to see why the others had.

Rusca stayed where she was. Then she came out from the eaves and across the turf to the stone, and stopped a little way off from it with the books held against her chest.

"Highness."

"The clans want to know what's done with the ones who lost," said Marcus. "You're a Tharsian. You were the Survey's. When this is over, the ones who lost will be your people." He held her amber eyes. "Sit at their table, and tell them what you think will happen to your people when it's over. Then tell them what you think ought to. Then they'll ask you things, and you'll answer them."

Her face did not change; it hardly ever did. But her fingers went to the linen at her cuff, and rested there, and left it where it was.

"They will not listen to me," she said. "I wrote the order that emptied their reed-towns. Not that one. But orders like it."

"Then they'll know you know how it's done."

On the stone's west side Sszarra's head had come round, all at once, and she was looking at Rusca with both gold eyes, and nothing to be read in either.

"You are of the ones who count," said Sszarra.

"I was," said Rusca.

"Was." Sszarra considered the word, and the woman. "Then you can tell us what it costs," she said, and turned, and walked to the middle table with the clans moving after her like one long creature. She sat down on its bench as if she had sat on benches all her life, which as far as Marcus knew she had never once done.

Rusca stood a moment longer by the stone. Then she set Alana's books down on its end, carefully, with their spines to the edge, and followed the clans.

"And you, cousin?" said Damien at his shoulder, pleasantly. "Which table?"

Marcus looked at the three of them under the silver roof. The elves at the south, standing, with Sylara at the end and her four behind her. The fen in the middle, sitting straight-backed on the bench with Rusca at its end, every face in the row turned to her at once. The halls at the north, with the king at the head of their table now and the grey stone in his hand, the keeper at his shoulder with her cord, and Gaius beside him with a ledger open and his finger already on the first line.

"Whichever's angriest," he said.

---

It was a long afternoon.

The household came round with water from the spring in stone jars that sweated in the heat. They set cups before every place at every table, and poured, and stepped back, and were gone before anybody had properly seen them come. They were good; Damien had not lied about that. A young man in dark wool filled Marcus's cup at the stone and went away down the turf with the jar on his shoulder, and Marcus would not have known him again if he had passed him on the beach. Felix watched them at it with his own jar held against his chest like a grievance, and every time one of them filled Marcus's cup, he filled it again himself.

"It's the same water, Felix."

"Same spring, Highness. Not the same jar."

The dwarves had brought their ale, and by mid-afternoon the smell of it came across the turf dark and bitter, with something in it like burnt bread. Down on the beach the cohort's cooks had sheep turning on spits over driftwood, below the high-tide mark on the crown's sand. When the wind came round off the bay it brought the fat and the smoke up under the boughs, and every head at every table lifted a little, the elves' too, though the elves would have denied it.

Marcus went from table to table, and sat at none.

He stood at the far end of the elves' table while Sylara gave the wood the honest count. She gave it standing at the near end, where there was no bench, with her book open in her hand and her eyes not on it, in the plain flat voice she used for her report. She told them about the gate, and the tortoise that did not go in, and the three of the Lanciarii left on the bank under the golems, whom Valeria could not get down to. She told them that the prince had been told at the map table to wait, and had not waited. She told them that at the gate, with it open, Livia's voice had come out of the fog to the whole field: walk through his gate, and the pens were lost, and then they were hers. When she came to the window she stopped, and the leaves went on turning overhead, and then she went on. She told them he had called up to the window to ask if it was true, and been told it was entered, and turned round. She did not say whether she would have turned round.

The elders listened to all of it with their hands in their sleeves. Behind Sylara her four stood in their row, and the small one with the braid in her collar said the words over under her breath after her, a few at a time, as if she were learning a song.

He stood at the far end of the fen's table and went no nearer, because it was not his. The clans sat in their row on the bench with their long hands laid on the pine, as still as if they had been carved there, and Rusca sat at the end in her borrowed grey and talked. She talked low and level, and it carried no further than the table's end. Once she took a sheet from the paper-stand at the table's end and drew on it, a column and a line, and turned it to Sszarra. Sszarra studied it without moving, and then put out one long finger and touched a place on it. Once Rusca stopped talking altogether, her hands in her lap, and the whole row of the clans sat with her, unmoving, until a leaf came down out of the roof and lay on the pine in front of Sszarra, and nobody brushed it away.

Then one of the clans at the far end, an old grey-throated one he did not know, said something in the fen speech, and Sszarra answered. Rusca drew the linen from her cuff, folded it smaller, tucked it back, and began again.

He went to the dwarves.

The north table was the loud one. It was loud because Gaius was reading.

He was reading the Bastion ledgers to the Hold-King of Stoneheart from the first page, in the flat voice he kept for a stores list, with his finger going down the column and his lenses on his nose. The halls' heads sat along both benches with their ale in front of them and argued with every line. They argued in the dwarf tongue, which was all hard stops, then in the common one for Gaius's benefit, then in the dwarf tongue again because the common one was too slow. Brokk argued with all of it. The keeper stood at the king's shoulder running the beads of her cord through her fingers as Gaius read. Every so often she said a figure in a voice like a hinge creaking, and the table stopped arguing to hear it, and then started again.

The king did not argue. He sat at the head with the grey stone on the pine before him and his big hands round his ale, and listened, and did not drink.

"Picks and mattocks for the ditch at the causeway," Gaius was saying. "Forge iron, from Stoneheart, the cohort's own, drawn at Cyrene. Used one morning. Left on the bank when the line closed."

"Left," said Brokk.

"Left."

"Iron of the halls, left in the mud for the levy to pick up and sell back to us by the pound." Brokk's hands came out of his belt, and he set them on the boards. "Princeling."

"Engineer," said the king, without looking at Brokk. "Go on."

Gaius went on. Marcus stood behind him at the table's end and listened to his own defeat read out in iron and barley and the price of ale, a line at a time, without one word in it about what anybody had felt. It was easier to hear than the honest count had been.

Queen Dagny came round the table to him with two cups. One was ale. She looked at his face, and gave him the other, which was water, and kept the ale.

"Highness," she said. "He's not angry."

"He looks angry."

"He looks like that when he's weighing." She drank some of the ale. "When he's angry he's polite. You've not seen him polite yet." She eyed him over the cup, the white spark-scars bare up her forearms. "Your witch said two kegs."

"She said he'd want two."

"He wanted six. I let him have six." She smiled. "It's a long way to bring a king to sit under trees."

The light under the roof went before the light on the bay did.

The elves' lamps had hung in the boughs all day unlit, pale and clouded, like seed-heads. Late in the afternoon, when nobody was looking at them, they began to glow: one, and then a scatter, and then all of them, until the whole underside of the great roof was hung with a light like frost with the sun behind it. Nobody had lit them. The cohort had stopped asking how.

The dwarves lit their own: squat lanterns in black iron cages, hung on crooks at the north table's corners, quickly and without asking anybody. A thread of their smoke went up into the leaves, and at the south end one of the elders watched it go, and turned back.

The household brought the mutton round on boards, hot and running, with bread and a dish of salt. The elves did not eat. They stood at their table with the mutton in front of them for an hour and did not once look at it; which Sylara said, at Marcus's elbow, was a compliment.

"Is it."

"It means they've decided not to be offended by it." She had her book open again. "I'm writing that down."

Damien had been everywhere all afternoon. He had not once been at the stone when Marcus was there, and never far from it. Now he stood at the south end, at the rim of the lamplight under the trees, with one of his clerks, tilting a paper to the elves' lamps and saying something low, and the clerk was nodding.

Alana came to Marcus at the stone in the dusk.

She had been at the fen's table most of the afternoon, at the far end from Rusca, with a board on her knees and the council's paper on it, copying fair. She had ink on her jaw, and on her wrist where the cuff fell back, and on two fingers of her left hand; her right hand was folded away under her left arm.

"The fen want the border written into whatever's signed," she said. "In their own words. Iss's map. Sszarra says the name's no use if the next king can say he never read it."

"Then it goes in."

"I said you'd say so. The elves have asked Sylara for a second day. They want the gate again. And the three on the bank."

"They'll have them."

"And the halls." She looked toward the north table, where Gaius was still reading under the iron lanterns, and the argument had gone down under him to a low steady growl, like a forge banked for the night. "Gaius is on the transports' hire. He'll be on the ale by midnight. The king hasn't drunk his cup since noon." Her lips moved over the hours, and stopped. "He's kept the stone."

"I know."

She looked at him then, sidelong, in the pale light from the boughs.

"You didn't let me answer him."

"No."

"Good," said Alana. "I'd have given him figures. He didn't want figures." She freed her right hand from under her arm, turned it over once, and tucked it away again. "I'm going back to the fen. Rusca's been talking since noon, and she won't stop to drink unless somebody puts a cup in her hand and stands there."

She went. He watched her go down the turf in the lamplight with her board under her arm, to the middle table, where she put a cup in Rusca's hand and stood there.

Across the turf, under the eaves, Pen had lit a candle in a horn lantern on the scribes' trestle, and was copying something fair with his head bent so close over it that his long nose nearly touched the paper.

---

The angriest table was the dwarves'.

He went down the turf to it in the dusk, past the stone, where the halls' book still lay open at the new line as the king had left it, for anybody to read. The line was there in the pale light. *The heir. Forty days. Not sent.* He knew what it said.

At the north table Gaius was still on the transports. He did not look up. The keeper did, from the king's shoulder, with her cord running through her fingers. Brokk did, with his hands back in his belt. The halls' heads along both benches did, one after another, with their ale halfway to their mouths, until the whole table was looking at the heir of Aeridor standing at the end of it in the lamplight with nothing in his hands.

The king did not look up. He was looking at the stone on the boards in front of him.

There was a gap on the bench across from him, where nobody had sat all day.

Marcus put his leg over the bench and sat down in it.

The king lifted his head. He looked at Marcus across the table in the yellow light of his own lanterns, with the iron cages ticking as they warmed above him and Gaius's dry voice going on beside them about a merchantman's hire by the day. Then he picked the grey stone up off the pine.

He weighed it once more on his palm. And he leaned across the table, and set it down on the boards in front of Marcus, between his hands.

---

# Chapter Eight: Twice

**SYLARA**

On the second morning the wood wanted the gate again, so Sylara gave it the gate again.

She gave it standing at the end of the elves' table, where there was no bench, with the bark-leaf book shut in her hand and a finger in it at the place. She did not need the place. She had given them the gate yesterday from the first word to the last, and the elders had listened to all of it with their hands in their sleeves. This morning they had come back under the boughs at first light and stood in the same places, in the same order, and Lyrielle had said, "The gate. Again. And the three on the bank."

So she began at the causeway, where she had begun yesterday, in the same plain voice.

The turf was still wet from the night and cold through her boots, and the air smelled of crushed grass and leaf-mould and, faintly, from the north end, of yesterday's tallow. The sun was not over the trees yet. Under the great roof the light was green and thin, like light at the bottom of a pool, and far above her the silver leaves turned over on their stems with the long slow breath the wood never finished. Somewhere up in it a wood-pigeon was saying the same thing over and over and being ignored.

Up the turf the council stone lay bare. The keeper had been there before anybody, at first light, to shut the halls' book and carry it back to the north table in its tooled case.

She told them about the tortoise, and the iron line closing, and the smoke. She told them which way the wind had been. She told them where the three of the Lanciarii had gone down, on the bank under the golems, and that the centurion had tried twice to get down to them and been held off. The centurion had not tried a third time, because the line was closing behind her and she had forty others in it. Sylara said it as the centurion would have said it, which was with no kindness to herself.

The elders listened. Vael-Anar stood at the centre, so old and so narrow that he seemed to have been drawn on the morning with one stroke, with his hands in his sleeves to the elbow. Lyrielle stood at his right. Her braid hung silver again down her back, and today her bow was strung and in her left hand, the lower limb resting on the turf. The others Sylara knew only from the council roots, as faces at the far edge of a song, and she did not try to know them better now. She watched their eyes, which were all an elf gave a human at a table. They were hearing it the second time exactly as hard as the first.

Behind her, not behind the elders, her four stood in a row on the turf.

That had been noticed yesterday, and it was being noticed again. She did not need to turn round to know how they stood. Ilvane at the end, with her slate-grey braid and her hands folded on her bow. Eolan, lean and still, his hair cut short at the nape like a man's, which the wood had never liked, turning in his long fingers whatever arrow he was fletching, as he did when there was nothing to shoot. Yrrin, broad for an elf, with the scar through his eyebrow and his unstrung bow along his forearm. And the small one, Nirel, with her braid tucked down inside her collar, who would be up on her toes by now to see the elders' faces, which she had been told about.

When she came to the window, she stopped.

She had stopped there yesterday too.

"He called up to the window," she said. "He asked if it was true. The voice said it was entered. And he turned round."

"Why?" said one of the elders on the left. It was the first word any of them had spoken that morning.

"He said the pens wouldn't keep."

"Is that why?"

"It's why he said."

The elder considered this. Her eyes moved to Lyrielle for a moment, and back.

Behind Sylara someone moved. She knew Yrrin's step from the others' by now: a little heavier on the heel, a hunter raised on the low ground. He came past her right shoulder and stood beside her at the table's end, where nobody had asked him to stand, and every elder's eyes turned to him at once.

His fingertip slid once down the notch in the grip of his bow.

"When he turned round," said Yrrin, in the common speech, which he did not like using, "did he look back at the gate?"

They were the first words he had given a human table in a year. Lyrielle's face stayed exactly where she had put it.

Sylara thought about it.

She could have said yes. Some of the cohort would have said yes by now, and believed it. A story grew at a camp's fires, and by Midwinter there would be men who had seen the prince look back at the gate with tears on his face. She could have said no, and been believed, because she was the warden and she wrote things down.

"I don't know," she said. "I was looking at the window."

Yrrin looked at her out of his dark, unblinking, grudging eyes.

"That is an honest count," he said.

"It's the one I've got."

He went back to his place behind her. There the small one said under her breath, "*I was looking at the window*," trying the shape of it, as she tried the shape of everything; and Ilvane, without turning her head, said something short in the elven speech, and Nirel stopped.

The household came through the trees at the second hour with the water.

Sylara did not know their names and had not tried to learn them. She had called people by their trades since she was six, because a trade told her what a man would do and a name only told her what his mother had wanted. She learned a name when a man gave her a reason to, and these had given her none. There was the tall one with the jar, who carried it as if it were a crown he had been told to bring to the altar without spilling. There was the boy with the boards, fourteen or fifteen and solemn about the meat. There was the groom, a square, quiet, short-legged man with a horseman's walk, which was curious, because Damien had brought no horse. There were two more who carried, and a valet who carried nothing but his master's cloak, and the two clerks, who sat at the scribes' trestle and carried nothing heavier than a pen. All of them wore dark good wool that did not show the wet. They set the cups down without a sound and were gone again.

The boy set a bowl on the end of the elves' table, by Sylara's hand.

It was hazelnuts: last year's cobs, brown and dry and rattling, with a smell of old cupboards. A courtesy, she supposed. Water and a little something were put out at every table whether they were wanted or not, so that the council could not be called mean. Elves did not eat at a human table, and it was rude to notice that they were not eating, so they did not look at the bowl with a thoroughness that was almost an act of worship.

Sylara gave them the three on the bank.

When she had done, the elders stood the length of a long breath without moving, and Vael-Anar inclined his narrow head to her by perhaps the width of a straw, which was a great deal. Then the boy came back and took the bowl away. She glanced at it as it went past her, because looking at things going past her was her trade.

The heap had sunk on one side.

The elves had not touched it. She had stood beside it the whole time. She went back over it as she would over a shot: the boy had set the bowl down with one hand and lifted it with two, and for the space of the lift his left hand had been inside the rim. So something had come to the elves' table in the household's bowl and gone away again in the boy's hand, and nobody at the table had asked for it. She could not think what for.

She watched him carry the bowl away across the turf. The first sun had come over the trees and lay along the wet grass in long pale stripes between the shadows of the trunks, and he passed through them, light and dark and light. He did not go to the sheds. He went to the fen's table, set the bowl down at the far end by the witch's board, and went away empty-handed.

Sylara opened the book against her palm and wrote at the foot of the day's page, small.

*The boy. Left hand. Inside the rim.*

---

The roof was not as good as the one at the turn of the crown's road. But it was a roof.

The engineer had put the sheds up with the trestles, along the north edge of the turf, where the grass ran out under the last of the eaves and the shingle began. The wood had let him, on condition that there was not one nail in them, and he had taken this as a compliment to himself and pegged the whole row together with oak trenails and his own temper. They stood open to the turf at the front, with a plank door at the back for the shingle and the boats, and their roofs were laid at a low pitch, with old sailcloth tarred over the planks. The tar was warm in the afternoon sun and smelled of the *Forward*'s hold. Sylara lay along the ridge of the second shed on her belly, with her chin on her folded arms and the bark-leaf book open under her left elbow, and watched the council.

She had been up there since noon. Yesterday she had come up at the ninth hour, after the honest count, and stayed until the elves' lamps lit.

Below her the council went on: the three tables down the long turf, the stone with nobody at it, the prince going from table to table with Felix and his water jar at his back. The scribes' trestle stood by the eaves, almost under her. The forger sat at the end of it bent over a quire, ruling it, and Damien's two clerks sat along from him, copying fair. Through all of it, between the tables and the sheds and the spring, the household came and went.

On the first shed Ilvane lay as Sylara did, flat along the ridge with her chin on her hands, looking at the wood beyond the bay. She had taken that roof the first afternoon without being asked, and nobody had asked her why. Below them both, at the near end of the row, Strabo stood on watch with his shield grounded against his shins and his helmet pushed back. He was looking out across the bay at the fleet with a great deal of attention, which he could keep up only by not looking at the roof of the first shed at all. He had not looked at it once since noon. Sylara had seen men hold a ford with less discipline.

She watched the bowls.

There was more than one. At midday the boy had brought the elves' bowl back from the fen's table and filled it again from a sack in the second shed, under her. Then he took it out to the halls, where the dwarves ate every nut in it, Brokk cracking them in his fist and dropping the shells on the turf. Another went round the fen's table. The clans did not eat the nuts, and neither did the quaestor. The witch ate them one at a time without noticing, with her left hand, and wrote with the same hand between nuts, so that her board had a fine crust of shell along its lower edge. And one bowl stood at the scribes' trestle, at the forger's elbow, and was his. He had brought it up from the beach himself the first morning. Now and then he cracked a nut between two pebbles without lifting his head from his ruling, and pushed the bowl along to whoever came to the trestle, as he had been giving people things since Cyrene.

The groom came out from under the eaves with an empty jar on his shoulder and went down to the spring.

The tall one was there already, filling his. They stood a moment together at the spring's lip, two men in dark wool bent over the water, and Sylara saw the tall one's hand go to his sleeve, and come out, and close over the groom's on the neck of the jar. It stayed no longer than it takes to steady a load.

She did not move.

She had seen it yesterday from this roof, an hour before the lamps: the boy, and then the groom, at the same spring. She had thought then that it might be a coin, or a dice-debt, or something between two men that was nobody's business, and she had written it down anyway, because she wrote everything down.

The groom carried his full jar up the turf, past the stone and the end of the halls' table, to the scribes' trestle. He filled the forger's cup there, and both of the clerks', and set the jar down. The forger pushed his bowl along to him with his eyes on his ruling, and the groom's hand went into it and came out with nothing in it. Then he stood a moment at the end of the trestle as a servant stands, with his hands folded at his belt.

The nearer clerk, the one with the inky cuffs, reached into the forger's bowl without looking up.

The forger pushed it along to him a little, without looking up either.

The clerk took one nut out of the bowl and did not crack it. He put his hand in again for a second, cracked that one between his teeth, and ate it. Then he went on copying. The first nut was not on the trestle any more, and Sylara had not seen where it went.

The groom took up his jar and went away round the end of the sheds, under her, and she heard his feet on the shingle behind them, going off toward the dwarves' camp.

"Is there room up there," said the witch from below, "for someone who doesn't climb?"

Sylara looked over the edge of the roof.

The witch stood at the back of the shed by the water-butt, with her board under her left arm and a smear of ink beside her nose. Her right hand was folded against her waist under the board. She was looking up with her hazel eyes narrowed against the sun on the tar.

"No," said Sylara. "Come up anyway."

It took some doing. The witch could climb, after a fashion; she had grown up in a library with a great many shelves. But she would not use her right hand, and she would not put down her board. In the end she handed the board up, and came up by the water-butt and a stack of trestle-boards and Sylara's wrist, and lay down along the ridge beside her, breathing hard, with tar on her sleeve.

"You've been here since noon," said the witch. "I saw you come up."

"Yes."

"You were at the elves' table all morning."

"Yes."

"And yesterday you were at the elves' table, and then here."

"I attended everything twice," said Sylara. "Once to hear it. Once to see it."

The witch turned her head on her arms and looked at her.

"And what did you see?"

Sylara told her as she had written it: the bowl at the elves' table, the jar at the spring, the clerk's hand. As she told it she watched the household going about under the boughs, and pointed each of them out with her chin as she came to him, the tall one, the boy, the groom, so that the witch would know them.

The witch was quiet when she had done. Down at the middle table one of the clans had said something low in the fen speech, and the whole bench had turned its long heads together toward the quaestor, and the quaestor was answering. None of it carried.

"I know them," said the witch. "They pour my water. The tall one has a cousin in the Lower Canal who makes saddles. The boy's from the chancery kitchens. He told me about his mother's goose." Her lips shaped something without a sound, a name or a sum, and let it go. "Those are Damien's people."

"They're the council's. He lent them."

"He lent them," the witch agreed. "Those are *Damien's* people."

She looked down at the scribes' trestle. The clerk with the inky cuffs was copying. The other clerk was copying. The forger had finished his ruling and was holding the quire up to the light from the eaves, squinting along the lines to see that they were true.

"What's in a nut," said Sylara, "that you don't eat?"

"Something you'd rather didn't look like a letter." The witch's mouth moved again, on nothing Sylara could hear. "You'd seal the seam. Wax, or a little resin. There's a way of doing it at court, with a pin. Ladies used to send their lovers—" She stopped. "It doesn't matter what ladies used to do."

"Can you see which one's which? From here?"

"No."

"Neither can I," said Sylara. "That's what's wrong with it."

At the end of the row Strabo shifted his feet and went on not looking at Ilvane's roof. Ilvane had turned her head on her hands to watch him do it, with her grey eyes half shut against the sun.

"You'll tell him," said the witch.

"Yes."

"Today."

"When he's at the fire. Not here." Sylara shut the book. "He'd look up."

The witch considered the prince, a long way off down the turf at the elves' table, with Felix behind him. He stood at the table's end with his head bent while Lyrielle spoke to him too low to carry: something Sylara would have to hear tomorrow, from somebody, the second time.

"He would," said the witch. She gathered her board. "I'll go down the way I came. Don't watch me."

Sylara watched her all the way down, in case.

---

The cohort's cooks had mackerel at the fire that evening, a basket of them off the hand-lines the boys had put out from the rocks at the spit, split and laid on the grid over the driftwood and turned once. The smell ran up the shingle in the dusk, salt and fat and smoke, and every man in the lines came down to it with his bread in his hand.

The prince sat on the shingle with his back against a beached ship's boat and his boots dug in, eating a mackerel off a slab of bread with his fingers. Felix sat beside him with another, and a cup of the dwarves' ale, which he drank with disapproval and remarked on now and then. At the water's edge the fire cracked and spat where the fat ran into it. Out in the bay the fleet rode in its two lines with lamps lit at the sterns, and the reed boats lay along the south corner like a raft of weed. Far out on the water something that was not a fish turned over once and was gone.

Sylara sat down on the prince's other side without being asked.

"Warden," said the prince.

"I've something for you. It's not for the turf."

He looked at her, and then at Felix. Felix got up with his mackerel and went down the beach to see about the fire, saying to nobody in particular that it had been laid wrong from the beginning.

Sylara told him. She told it in the order she had told the witch: the bowl, the spring, the trestle; and then the witch on the roof. He listened with the bread forgotten in one hand and the fish going cold in the other, and his face did very little, except that by the end the scar on his jaw stood out white in the firelight.

"What's in them?" he said.

"I don't know."

"Could you get one?"

"Yes. Tomorrow. Off the boy, at the spring." She had thought about this all afternoon. "He'd know it was gone by the evening. They'd all know."

"Then don't."

She waited.

"If they know we've seen," said the prince, "they stop. They'll be very good servants for the rest of the council, and pour very well, and go home on the *Good Year* with whatever they've got, and I'll have nothing but you on a roof telling me about nuts. And Damien'll be sorry. He'll be so sorry. He'll be sorry in his good cloak." He looked down at the fish in his hand. "I want to know what they're for. Not what's in them. What they're for."

"Then I watch."

"Then you watch." He put the fish down on the bread. "Tomorrow and the day after. All of it."

"I was going to anyway."

"I know." The start of something came into his face, and was folded away again. "Now it's asked."

She got up. He did not tell her to keep it to herself, because he did not have to, and she did not tell him she would, because he knew. It had taken her all winter to get that about him into the report. She had put it in at last at the Forge, and not struck it out.

Rufo was waiting for her at the top of the beach, where the shingle gave out into the long grass on the slope above the black rock. He had his supper in his hand, three mackerel on one slab of bread, and he held it out to her without a word. She took one.

They ate walking. The slope ran up from the black rock to the edge of the pines, south-facing and still warm underfoot from the day, with the long grass dry and whispering against their legs and the smell of the pines coming down it. There were crickets in the grass. A little below the trees Rufo stopped, handed her the bread, and walked away from her across the grass with his big shoulders going square.

She knew what he was doing. She ate her mackerel and let him do it.

He paced it out. Six paces along the slope, toward the spit. Six up it, toward the trees. Six back along, and six down, until his boots came to the place in the grass where they had started, near enough. He had paced it out the first time on the voyage west, before Stoneheart, the night the fleet lay in this bay, while she stood and watched him with her arms folded. He would go on pacing it, she supposed, every time he came here, until there was something on it to pace round.

"It hasn't grown," said Sylara.

"Checking."

He came back across the grass and took his bread back, and stood beside her looking down at the six paces of empty slope with his supper in his hand. The light left the bay below them, and the sky, and the fleet's stern-lamps were brighter than the water.

Telling Rufo was not telling anybody; he was where she kept things.

"There's a man lent us his servants," said Sylara. "And they're still his."

Rufo ate a mouthful of his bread and thought about it. He thought as he paced, right to the end before he turned.

"Aye," he said. "They would be."

His hand went up then to the lock above his ear, touched it, and came down again. She saw it. But it was getting dark on the slope, and she was tired, and she leaned on him instead of looking at it. He let her.

---

On the third morning the elves asked for the first day's words to be read back.

It was a reasonable thing to ask. The elves remembered as a people, the whole wood at once, so that a thing said to one of them was said to all of them for as long as there were any. A human council forgot as a people too: a table at a time, in writing. Before anything more was agreed, the elves wanted to hear what the humans had decided they had heard on the first day. Lyrielle asked for it at the stone at the first hour, as everybody came in under the boughs with the dew on their boots. The prince said that it should be done. The engineer looked up from the halls' table, pushed his lenses up into his hair, and let his brows go up after them. The first day's paper, he began, was in four hands across three tables, and would want an hour to put in order.

"Cousin," said Damien. "Let me."

He had come up from his tent in the cohort's lines at first light, bareheaded, in a cloak the colour of sloes with a plain silver clasp. He had come by the elves' table on his way to the stone, and smelled faintly of orris-root as he passed, which was more than anything else under the boughs smelled of. His two clerks came behind him with a leather satchel each.

"My clerks have made a fair copy of the first day for every table," he said. "All in one hand, all in one place. They sat up with it." He smiled at the engineer. "I'd not have you lose an hour you could spend on the transports, Engineer. Nobody would."

Sylara, in her place at the elves' table with the bark-leaf book in her hand, watched him do it, and thought that it was the best-done thing she had seen at the council so far, and wished she had not thought so.

The prince looked at Damien, and then at the stone, and said, "Read it, then."

Damien read it.

He stood at the north end of the stone with the fair copy laid open in front of him and his bone folder lying beside it, and he read it well. He had a reader's voice, warm and unhurried, pitched to carry under trees without ever seeming raised, and he had the trick of looking up at the table whose words he was reading, as if he were giving them back their own property. He read the prince's opening: the defeat owned, the last day promised, the question asked.

Then he read the halls.

"*The Hold-King of Stoneheart,*" read Damien, "*asked what the crown would pay for the halls' iron lost at Bastion. The heir answered that the crown would pay, and the Hold-King was content.*"

He went on to the next line. He was three words into the elves' question when he stopped, because nobody at the north table was moving.

Sylara had been watching the north table for that line. She had known where it came, and she had wanted to see the king's face when it was given back to him.

It did not darken. It did nothing at all. King Kolbrand sat at the head of the north table as he had sat for two days, in his plate, with his crimson cloak back over his shoulders and the crown-helm on the boards by his right hand. His great auburn beard hung in its braids and rings down over his breastplate to his belt. He looked at Damien across the turf with his clear blue eyes, pleasantly. Then he got up.

"Thank you, my lord," said the Hold-King of Stoneheart.

Brokk took his hands out of his belt. Behind the king the keeper stopped her cord between two beads.

"Thank you," said Kolbrand again. "That was very clear. Very kindly read. I'm obliged to you, my lord, and to your clerks for their trouble, and their candle." He inclined his head to Damien, a small, correct, courtly inclination, such as a king might give another king's steward. "And I'm obliged to the heir, who'll pay us for our iron. That's handsome. We shan't trouble the council further."

He did not sit down.

Sylara had never heard him say *thank you*. She had heard him at the gate of the halls, and over the anvils at the Forge by the light of a fire she still dreamed about, and at this stone two days ago asking his question. He was saying it now in a voice as smooth as a peeled willow wand, to a man he had not once looked at in two days. At the far end of the north table Queen Dagny had set her ale down carefully on the boards and taken her hand off it.

The prince had turned. He looked at Kolbrand, and then at the fair copy on the stone, and his jaw had gone white again along the scar. Then, for no reason anybody could have seen, he looked along the turf to the elves' table, at Sylara.

She opened the book.

"That isn't what he asked," she said.

She said it at the pitch she kept for the report, and under the boughs that carried further than shouting. Every head at every table came round to her. Kolbrand's came last, and slowly. The smoothness stayed on his face, but his eyes changed.

"Lady warden?" said Damien, pleasantly.

"He didn't ask about iron." She found the place. "He asked, *Who pays for Bastion, heir?* Then he opened their book at the back and turned it round and showed us the line. *The heir. Forty days. Not sent.* That's what he asked." She turned the leaf. "The iron was the engineer's. In the afternoon, at their table, not the stone; I had it from the roof. Picks and mattocks for the ditch at the causeway. Left on the bank. Brokk said *Left*, and the engineer said *Left*, and that was the iron."

Brokk, at the north table, made a noise like a kiln door.

"And the heir's answer," said Kolbrand. He was still standing. His voice was not smooth any more. "Read the heir's answer, warden. If you have it."

"I have it."

She read it exactly, because she had written it exactly, standing at the end of this same table two days ago with the lead going as fast as he spoke: the crown pays its own dead, and their names, and the promise. *You can't be paid back for a broken promise. There's no coin for it. It's only paid one way.* And the forty days. And *You'll know it's paid when you see it paid. Not before.*

When she had finished, the only sound under the treaty-boughs was the leaves turning.

Then the keeper let her cord go on between her fingers, one bronze bead after another, and said a word or two in the dwarf tongue that might have been a figure; and Kolbrand sat down.

"Write it as the warden has it," he said to the keeper; and to the stone, without looking at it, "Your clerk has a fine hand, my lord."

"And a merchant's ear, I'm afraid." Damien had not moved, except to pick up the bone folder. He drew it once down the fold of the fair copy, unhurriedly, setting it, as a man smooths a cloth he has spilled something on. "He'll be spoken to. Your Majesty, I'm mortified. Cousin." He turned his warm face to the elves' table. "Lady warden. The council owes you a page."

"It's in the report," said Sylara.

Up the turf under the eaves, Rufo's big shoulders went up once and came down, which in Rufo was a laugh.

The reading went on. Damien read the rest as well as he had read the first: the elves' question and the fen's, the answers, the tables given out, and Sylara's own name, at which he smiled at her. She did not smile back, and it did not seem to trouble him. As far as she could tell it was true, line for line. She stood with the book open in her hand to the end of it, and when he had done she shut it and buttoned it into her jerkin. Up the turf Damien was folding the fair copy into his clerk's satchel. His eyes did not come her way again.

The prince came to the elves' table afterwards, on his way to nowhere in particular, with Felix and the jar a pace behind.

"Page what," he said, low.

"Six."

"I'll want to read page six."

"You won't like it. It's mostly you."

He went on toward the fen. At the north table, as the stone emptied at the second hour, Brokk stood at the corner nearest the sheds under a bare iron crook, with his voice up. Somebody, he was telling the whole halls, had had a lantern off this crook in the night, a whole lantern, cage and ring and all, and if he found the man he would have the ring back off him and an ear with it. The queen told him it had likely been some cohort lad who wanted a light to piss by, and to hush.

Sylara stayed where she was, looking up the turf at the empty crook.

Then she went to find the centurion.

---

She had been on the roof again since noon.

It was the third evening. The day had been long and hot and short-tempered under the boughs: the halls not quite mollified, the fen patient. The elves had stood all afternoon at their table while the prince stood at the end of it and gave them his own account of the gate, which they had asked for at noon. She had heard none of it from the roof. She had watched him give it. She thought he was not sparing himself, and she could see that he was giving it badly: he stopped more than once and began again further back, and once he looked at the table's end where she would have been, and she was not there. Tomorrow she would have it from Lyrielle, in the elven way, which kept the words and threw out the man.

Now the sun was down in the sea beyond the bay's mouth, and the light under the boughs had turned to rust, and was going. Ilvane was on the first roof. The other three had gone off the turf when Sylara came up, and had not come back. Strabo was on watch below. The witch was down at the fen's table, at the quaestor's elbow, copying.

And the centurion was in the third shed.

Sylara could not see her. The centurion and Pacuvius had gone into the third shed in the heat of the afternoon, one at a time, each with a sack on the shoulder, sent for barley; and had not come out. Sylara had asked her for it in the morning, at the cohort's lines, with the empty crook in her mind. *There's a lantern gone. And I'd like the sheds watched from inside, by somebody who'd look like stores.* The centurion had considered her, with the morning on the long scar down her cheek, and said, "I've never looked like stores in my life, warden," and then, "Pacuvius can. Pacuvius has looked like stores since he was born."

The elves' lamps came on in the boughs as they had the first night and the second, and the green light under them turned grey, and the faces at the tables went pale and strange. At the north table the dwarves lit their lanterns with a striker and a twist of tow, and the smell of hot tallow came across the turf, thick and kindly, like a kitchen. Three crooks had lanterns on them. The fourth, nearest the sheds, had nothing on it but Brokk's hat, hung there in protest. At the south end, under the first trees, Damien stood with his valet in the full light of the lamps, where anybody could see him.

Sylara watched the roofs.

There were four in the row. She was on the second, Ilvane on the first. The third and the fourth ran on toward the dwarves' camp with their sailcloth tarred dark, and past the fourth's ridge there was nothing but the long dusk and the smoke of the dwarves' cook-fires coming up off the shingle. It was the dark end. The elves' lamps did not reach it, and with the fourth crook bare, the dwarves' light did not reach it either. The shed-ends there stood up black against the last red over the bay.

Something moved on the fourth roof.

It moved low, along the far slope of the ridge, where the roof hid it from the turf. She saw it only because she was higher than the turf and looking for it. A man lay flat and came along the sailcloth on his elbows, as a man comes along a wall-top in the dark: the groom. He had a horseman's shoulders, and he moved like a horseman off his horse, a little stiff, a little too careful. Under his cloak, held against his chest, something showed through the dark wool as a faint smear of yellow.

He had lit it already.

She did not understand it. A man with a light on a roof at dusk might be going to signal, or to look for something he had dropped, or to piss off the end where the queen of the halls could not see him. On the first roof Ilvane had not moved, but Sylara saw her eyes leave the bay for the fourth shed, and stay there.

The groom came to the end of the ridge nearest the boughs, and lay a moment. Below him the dwarves were drinking at the end of the north table. Beyond them the great grey bole of the nearest of the six trees rose out of the turf into its own lamplight, as thick through as a tower. The lowest of the elves' lamps hung in a cluster from its first bough, low over the grass.

The groom stood up on the roof.

He stood up all in one movement, and the lantern came out from under his cloak in his right hand, lit and swinging. When his arm went back she understood it, with her bow in her hand and nothing in front of her that she was allowed to shoot. He threw it underarm, a long swing. The lantern rose off the end of the sheds and sailed out over the dwarves' heads, turning slowly, its yellow light going round and round inside its black cage like a thing alive, toward the grey tree and its lamps.

Then it stopped turning, and went sideways, and fell.

Sylara did not see Lyrielle draw; she was watching the lantern, and there was an arrow through its cage, and it was falling a good fifteen paces short of the trunk. Lyrielle had stood all day at the south end of the elves' table with her bow in her left hand and its lower limb on the grass. Now, the whole length of the turf away, she stood with the bow up and the string still moving and her face entirely calm.

The lantern hit the turf and burst.

The oil spilled across the grass in a long splash of fire, low and blue at first and then yellow, and caught the grass at its edges and ran. It was not much fire. It was one lantern. But it was fire on the ground under the treaty-boughs, with the lamps above it and the great grey bole behind it, and every elf there made the same sound. Sylara had never heard it from the wood in all her years at its edge: not a cry, but one hard breath drawn in, as if the whole wood had been struck.

The tables came up.

The elves came round the end of their table with their bows in their hands. The dwarves were on their feet at the north table, every one, and the benches crashed over, and the halls' heads were reaching for the axes they had grounded under the trestle. Between the two, the fen had risen at the middle table in one long movement, all of them, and stood with their gold eyes on the fire and their long hands open, waiting to see who would be first.

"*That's ours,*" said someone at the north table, in the common speech, too loud. "*That's one of ours—*"

Queen Dagny went past him at a run.

She went straight across the turf to the fire with her skirts kilted up in one hand, took the leather apron off a dwarf who stood gaping at it, and flung it over the burning oil. Then she stamped on it with her heavy boots, and stamped on it, until the fire under the leather was out, and kicked the apron off and went after the edges where the fire had run in the grass, stamping. Two of the halls came after her and did the same. One of the elders had come up to the burned patch and stood at its edge, looking at it. He did not stamp, and did not move.

Then Dagny bent and picked the lantern up.

She picked it up by the ring with her bare hand, though it must have been hot enough to raise a blister, and held it up into the light of the elves' lamps with Lyrielle's arrow still through the cage. She turned it about. She put her face down to it, and smelled it.

"It's ours," she said to the elves, loud and flat. "It's our cage. It's off the crook. Brokk's been crying about it since breakfast." She held it up toward the south end of the boughs. "That's not our oil."

Nobody moved.

"We burn tallow." The queen of the halls swung the lantern round so that the whole north table could see it. "Mutton tallow. It stinks. That's olive. That's crown oil. That's out of a jar on their beach, that is." She sniffed it again. "Somebody filled our lantern with their oil, and threw it at your tree."

Lyrielle had come up the turf to the burned grass, with her bow lowered in her hand.

She looked at the black patch at her feet, and at the trunk behind it, and up at the cluster of lamps hanging from the first bough, unharmed; and at the queen, with the lantern held out in her bare hand; and last, at the far end of the row of sheds, where the groom had stood, and was not.

"Someone," said Lyrielle, "has studied exactly how we would hate each other."

She said it quietly. It carried to every table. Sylara heard the elves hear it, and the halls, and the fen; and the prince, who had come out from the middle table and stood now in the open between all three of them, alone and unarmed.

"Then let's be students of the opposite," said the prince.

He turned round. He did not look at the fourth shed. He looked up the slope of the eaves to the second shed's roof and to her, as if he had always known exactly which roof she was on.

"Take them *alive*," he said.

Sylara was already up.

---

The groom had not run. That was what she had waited for, flat on the tar with the fire going below and every face under the boughs turned to it: whether he would run while they all watched the fire, or lie still until they had stopped. He had lain still. He had dropped flat on the fourth roof's far slope the instant the lantern left his hand, and lain there through the fire and the queen and Lyrielle, a dark hump on dark sailcloth.
